# Numerical simulations of oscillations for axisymmetric solar backgrounds with differential rotation and gravity

**arXiv ID**: 2508.19386v7
**Authors**: Ha Pham, Florian Faucher, Damien Fournier, Hélène Barucq, Laurent Gizon
**Published**: 2025-08-26
**Categories**: astro-ph.SR, astro-ph.IM, math.AP
**Comments**: 74 pages, 23 figures
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2508.19386v7

## Abstract

Local helioseismology comprises of imaging and inversion techniques employed to reconstruct the dynamic and interior of the Sun from correlations of oscillations observed on the surface, all of which require modeling solar oscillations and computing Green's kernels. In this context, we implement and investigate the robustness of the Hybridizable Discontinuous Galerkin (HDG) method in solving the equation modeling stellar oscillations for realistic solar backgrounds containing acoustic attenuation, gravity, and differential rotation. While a common choice for modeling stellar oscillations is the Galbrun's equation, our working equations are derived from an equivalent variant, involving less regularity in its coefficients, working with Lagrangian displacement and pressure perturbation as unknowns. Under differential rotation and axisymmetric assumption, the system is solved in azimuthal decomposition with the HDG method. Compared to no-gravity approximations, the mathematical nature of the wave operator is now linked to the profile of the solar buoyancy frequency $N$ which encodes gravity, and leads to distinction into regions of elliptic or hyperbolic behavior of the wave operator at zero attenuation. While small attenuation is systematically included to guarantee theoretical well-posedness, the above phenomenon affects the numerical solutions in terms of amplitude and oscillation pattern, and requires a judicious choice of stabilization. We investigate the stabilization of the HDG discretization scheme, and demonstrate its importance to ensure the accuracy of numerical results, which is shown to depend on frequencies relative to $N$, and on the position of the Dirac source. As validations, the numerical power spectra reproduce accurately the observed effects of the solar rotation on acoustic waves.

## Full Text

Numerical simulations of oscillations for axisymmetric solar backgrounds with differential rotation and gravity

## Numerical simulations of oscillations for axisymmetric
solar backgrounds with differential rotation and gravityHa PhamProject-Team Makutu, Inria, Université de Pau et des Pays de l’Adour,
CNRS UMR 5142, France.Florian Faucher11footnotemark:1Damien FournierMax-Planck-Institut für
Sonnensystemforschung, Justus-von-Liebig-Weg 3,
37077 Göttingen, Germany.Hélène Barucq11footnotemark:1Laurent Gizon22footnotemark:211footnotemark:1Institut für Astrophysik und Geophysik,

## Abstract

Local helioseismology comprises of imaging and inversion techniques
employed to reconstruct the dynamic and interior of the Sun from
correlations of oscillations observed on the surface, all of which
require modeling solar oscillations and computing Green’s kernels.
In this context, we implement and investigate the robustness of
the Hybridizable Discontinuous Galerkin (HDG) method in solving the
equation modeling stellar oscillations for realistic solar backgrounds
containing acoustic attenuation, gravity, and differential rotation.
While a common choice for modeling stellar oscillations is the Galbrun’s equation,
our working equations are derived from an equivalent variant,
involving less regularity in its coefficients,
working with Lagrangian displacement and pressure perturbation
as unknowns.
Under differential rotation and axisymmetric assumption,
the system is solved in azimuthal decomposition with
the HDG method.
Compared to no-gravity approximations,
the mathematical nature of the wave operator is now linked to the profile
of the solar buoyancy frequencyNNwhich encodes gravity, and
leads to distinction into regions of elliptic or hyperbolic behavior
of the wave operator at zero attenuation.
While small attenuation is systematically included to
guarantee theoretical well-posedness,
the above phenomenon affects the numerical solutions
in terms of amplitude and oscillation pattern, and
requires a judicious choice of stabilization.
We investigate the stabilization of the HDG discretization
scheme, and demonstrate its importance to ensure the accuracy
of numerical results, which is shown to depend on
frequencies relative toNN, and on the position of
the Dirac source.
As validations, the numerical power spectra reproduce accurately
the observed effects of the solar rotation on acoustic waves.

## 1Introduction

This work considers the time-harmonic equations modeling
linear adiabatic stellar oscillations in axisymmetric
backgrounds containing acoustic attenuation, differential rotation and gravity
in Cowling’s approximation.
It is carried out in the context of local helioseismology
which aims to reconstruct the interior and dynamics of the Sun
from observed oscillations in the photosphere, cf.[24].
The Green’s kernels of the associated wave operator play a crucial role
in the forward modeling of helioseismic data and inversion for solar
parameters, cf.[6,7],
and in fact in a
larger family of passive-imaging techniques,
cf.[28].
We compute Green’s kernels with realistic solar backgrounds
containing gravity and rotation, providing validations of the
numerical results with observables.
Realistic background parameters make appear
interesting mathematical properties associated with the specificity
of solar physics, especially due to the profile of its buoyancy
frequencyNNwhich is non-zero when gravity is considered,
and has repercussion on the numerical implementation.

## Observing the Sun

The history of helioseismology starts with the now well-known 5-min oscillations of the Sun discovered
by Leighton, Noyes and Simon in 1962[39]and observed in the Doppler velocity time series,
see, e.g., the reviews[24, Figure 2],[33, Figure 3].
Power spectra of these time series contain rich information of solar oscillation,
cf.[35,33]:
the power of the spectrum
is mostly concentrated in the
acoustic111The dominant restoring force in this frequency range is the
pressure gradient, cf.Eq.2.7for the equation
of motion.band (1.5–5.3mHz) along ridges in
the(ω,ℓ)(\omega,\ell)-space, cf.[35, Figure 2,4].
From these ridges which show the standing wave nature of oscillation,
characteristic frequencies are extracted and
labelled asωn​ℓ​m\omega_{n\ell m}with
radial numbernn, spherical harmonic degreeℓ\ell,
and azimuthal ordermm, cf.[35, Figure 6]and[33, Figure 6].

Comparing to the observation of oscillations, that of solar
rotation has a longer history starting with sun-spot tracking
from which the surface rate of rotation could be estimated;
however it is helioseismology that discovered the structure
of solar internal rotation[56].
The effect of rotation on the acoustic waves
leads to a dependence onmmof the characteristic frequencies,
called modemmsplitting.
This phenomenon can be observed on the(ω,m)(\omega,m)-space spectrum
for a fixedℓ>0\ell>0, with ridges being curved instead of straight (the latter corresponding to no rotation),
cf.[33, Figure 7]and[35, Figure 5].
In fact, the curvatures of these ridges characterize quantitatively rotation,
and will be employed to validate our numerical power spectrum with
the observed one from[37].

## Modeling oscillations

Starting with a hydrodynamic description of a stellar body as an
ideal fluid state, and in adopting a mixed Eulerian-Lagrangian
perspective222See[48]for explanation
and references which discuss leaving out other effects
such as magnetics, as well as the mixed framework description.,
stellar oscillations are encoded in the 3D vector variable𝝃\boldsymbol{\xi}which
represents small displacements at each Eulerian position, by comparing
the flow in a state of interest considered as perturbed to a (virtual)
reference state.
The variable𝝃\boldsymbol{\xi}plays an important role in the modeling of solar observables
including Doppler line-of-sight velocity and intensity[20].
There are several manners to derive the equation of
motion333See also[38,55]for a different perspective of derivation.,
leading to equivalent variants appearing in literature, e.g.,[42]and[30].
The equations considered in[42]are
also known as Galbrun’s equations[21]in
aeroacoustics, cf. the references in[49].
Although the equations in[42]and[30]are equivalent to each other for backgrounds in hydrostatic equilibrium, the
coefficients of the equations in[42]require more regularity (specifically it uses the Hessian instead of
gradient of background pressure and gravity potential),
see detailed discussions inRemarks6,5and9.

In the acoustic frequency range, the contribution of gravity is important
and needs to be included in the modeling equation to get closer to the observations. It allows to model the surface-gravity modes that are absent in the no-gravity
approximation444We recall that gravity is ignored to
work with convected Helmholtz-type equation,
see[22, section 2.1],
with flow and rotation encoded in the first-order
convection, see also[5,9,43]for numerical discretization,[3]in radial symmetry,
and[62,25]for applications.[22, Figure 10],
and to represent more accurately the location of the peaks in the power spectra[48, Figures 11 and 12].
The effects of gravity is also shown in this work to give rise to interesting numerical
phenomena555We refer to[48]for further discussion
of effects in terms of absorbing boundary condition..
In fact, the inclusion of gravity is standard,
(see, e.g., the monographs[61,29]),
as included in two reference codes ADIPLS[13]and GYRE[60]routinely employed in the stellar
community. These codes are eigensolvers for radially symmetric background
and the resulting eigenfunctions can be used to obtain the Green’s
kernels[6,7]. This approach is only possible
when the spectrum is discrete and the background medium is spherically symmetric.

In this work, we consider rotation which results in an axisymmetric background.
In this case, the problem decouples into 2D equations for each azimuthal ordermm.
This setup is interesting to study, for example, rapidly rotating stars[52],
or inertial modes as background rotation needs to be considered in this case[26].
We also mention[10]that considers the stellar
oscillation in form of Galbrun’s equation, although it employs different
discretization method and equation, and more importantly does not consider
realistic solar backgrounds.

## Highlights of methodology and results

Breaking with tradition from our previous works[49,4]which employ Galbrun’s equations (for radially symmetric background),
our working equation is derived from a formulation closer (and equivalent)
to that in[30], which has as unknowns the vector
displacement𝒖\boldsymbol{u}and the pressure perturbationwwand is written as,
seeSection2,𝑨​𝒖+𝜷1​w+∇w=𝐠,\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}\,\boldsymbol{u}\,+\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\,w\,+\,\nabla w\,=\,\mathbf{g},(1.1a)∇⋅𝒖+𝜷2⋅𝒖+ϱ​w=h.\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\,\boldsymbol{u}\,+\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\,\cdot\,\boldsymbol{u}\,+\,\varrho\,w\,=\,h.(1.1b)

Here, the operator𝑨\boldsymbol{A}contains two orders of material derivative(𝐯0⋅∇)(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)with𝐯0\mathbf{v}_{0}representing the
reference background flow velocity. The vector-valued functions𝜷1,2\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1,2}and scalar functionϱ\varrhoare
multiplication operators.
Their value and property are determined by a choice of background model.
The final working equation is obtained after a Liouville change of variable
to handle solar stratification near the surface, cf.Subsection3.2.
Under axisymmetric assumptions, the problem is solved in azimuthal
decomposition (called 2.5D problem) with the Hybridizable Discontinuous
Galerkin (HDG) method. Our choice of discretization method follows the
HDG framework of[18,48]in the
perspective of Cockburn et al.[15].
We refer to[48]and the references listed therein
for discussion of the advantages of the method for large-scale problems.

On each azimuthal modemm, the differential operator𝑨\boldsymbol{A}acts simply as a multiplication operator and is denoted by𝑨m\boldsymbol{A}_{m}.
With∇m⋅\nabla_{m}\cdotand∇m\nabla_{m}representing the action of the
divergence and the gradient in this decomposition, there is a connection
with a second-order scalar equation whose highest order terms
is∇m⋅𝑨m−1​∇m\nabla_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{-1}\nabla_{m},
cf.Subsections3.4and4.2.
This crucial fact facilitates carrying over the HDG discretization
framework of[18,48,45]to the current equation, specifically in formulating the local
boundary value problems and their boundary data, both of which
constitute key steps in formulating the HDG problem,
cf.Section5.
While the generic form of the working system
of equations resembles that of the scalar convection–diffusion
equation (e.g.,[45]),
its nature is different when the coefficients follow realistic
solar models, specifically due to gravity which is represented
in the non-zero buoyancy frequency,NN,[14].

This frequency plays a role in determining the invertibility
of𝑨\boldsymbol{A}and𝑨m\boldsymbol{A}_{m}(or ellipticity in view of the scalar PDE)
at zero attenuation, e.g., ellipticity is lost whenN2>0N^{2}>0and
for frequencies aboveNN, and is restored at non-zero attenuation.
While the numerical resolutions are always carried out with small attenuation
assumption, the solutions in these region behave differently, featuring zigzag
patterns associated with propagation of singularities, cf. discussion inSection6.
The work will also show that, in all cases,
the solutions in zero-gravity approximation are very different,
both in magnitude and oscillation patterns, and do not have this
relation withNN(asN=0N=0without gravity).
We also note that the well-posedness of the equation of motion in𝝃\boldsymbol{\xi}is proved under the assumption of attenuation in[32]in form of Galbrun’s equation and for generic settings, in particular,
without indicating the link between the frequencyω\omegaandNN.

## Organization

The discussion of the investigation is organized as follows.
The oscillation equations are derived from first principles inSection2and the working formulation is given inSection3.
The axisymmetric equations are obtained inSection4,
and the discretization using the HDG method is carried out inSection5. We solve numerically for the solar backgrounds inSection6and provide
a qualitative analysis on the choice of the stabilization.
Finally, the numerical solutions are converted onto helioseismic observables
and validated in this context inSection7.
The experiments are carried out with softwareHawen[19].
For reproducibility, the setups for the numerical experiments, including the
solar background models employed as well as the parameter files and scripts,
are available in the online Zenodo repository,https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.21297984.

## 2Time-harmonic oscillation equations

Linear solar oscillations are modeled as small-amplitude oscillatory motions
in a moving fluid which are caused by perturbations in the state of the fluid
compared to a reference configuration.
The derivation of the oscillation equation starts with
the hydrodynamic description of the Sun as an
adiabatic fluid in self-gravitation and
rotation around axis𝐞z\mathbf{e}_{z}, listed inSubsection2.1.
Perturbations are then introduced in a mixed Eulerian-Lagrangian
perspective, cf.Subsection2.2.
Under appropriate assumptions, linearization is performed
to obtain linear equations describing small perturbations, cf.Subsection2.2.
From these linear equations, the oscillation equations are derived inSubsection2.3,
in terms of the displacement𝛏\boldsymbol{\upxi}defined at a fixed position (i.e., Eulerian) which
represents the small difference in position of a particle
carried by the perturbed flow versus the reference one.
After introducing the working equations of oscillations,
we will also introduce the scalar Green’s kernelGpG_{p}inDefinition1, which forms a key ingredient in modeling observables.

There are several approaches to arrive at the final oscillation equations,
for a review we refer to[38, Section 1.7].
We note two approaches commonly found in literature:
The first one is to introduce Eulerian perturbations in the
conservation laws written in Eulerian form and
then linearize, as was considered in[27], see also[31]for a more mathematical discussion.
In the second approach, one works with Lagrangian perturbations of the conservation
laws on which form linearization is performed; after this, to obtain the equations
satisfied by the Eulerian perturbations and displacement, the
relation between Lagrangian and Eulerian perturbations are employed, cf.[8,38].
Another approach which works with Lagrangian perturbations is given
in[42], and adopted
in[32, Section 2.2]to provide a derivation of the oscillation equations.
In our work, we adopt a hybrid approach: we follow closely the rigorous derivation given in[38]for the perturbation of the conservation of mass, energy and equation of state, while for the conservation
of momentum, we adopt the first approach, i.e. working with Eulerian perturbations and linearization, for its simplicity.

Although the first approach is much simpler and provide readily
linear equations via Euler perturbation analysis of the conservation of mass, momentum and energy,
it does require assumption in integrating the time material derivative to arrive at two crucial ingredients
needed for the equation of oscillations: the relation between the perturbations
of the pressure and density in terms of the displacement,
listed below inEq.2.50b. This is discussed in[31]regarding the conservation of mass and[27]concerning conservation of energy, see also
discussion in[55]. We discuss this briefly inRemark3.
We also note that the derivation of the
oscillation equation are mostly obtained in physics and astrophysics literature for non-moving background (i.e with𝐯0=0\mathbf{v}_{0}=0see notation inEq.2.10), cf.[61,50,51,14].
In this case, the material time derivative is simply the time derivative which
allows for normal integration and avoid the problem listed inRemark3,
known as the no-resonance assumption.
A second problem with adopting a pure Eulerian
approach arises in the perturbation analysis of the equation of state which provides relations between the thermodynamics quantities (density, pressure, entropy, temperature etc), cf.Eq.2.15andEq.2.16.
These are Lagrangian in nature, which
means that the perturbations of the equation of state are first obtained in terms of Lagrangian
perturbations, cf.[61, Section 13.4 p.98].

## 2.1Hydrodynamics description: perturbed vs reference states

## Perturbed state

The mathematical description of a perturbed fluid state is
characterized by a set of (state) parameters:−the fluid density​ρ-\,\,\text{the fluid density }\rho,−the flow velocity​𝐯-\,\,\text{the flow velocity }\mathbf{v},−the fluid pressure​p-\,\,\text{the fluid pressure }p,(2.6)−the specific entropy​𝔰-\,\,\text{the specific entropy }\mathfrak{s},−the gravitational potential​Φ-\,\,\text{the gravitational potential }\Phi.

In a rigidly co-rotating observation frame rotating
at rateΩc\Omega_{c}, these parametersEq.2.6satisfy the conservation
of mass, momentum and energy equations, cf.[61, Eqs (13.1–13.3)]∂tρ+∇⋅(ρ​𝐯)=0,\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho+\nabla\cdot(\rho\,\mathbf{v})\,=\,0\,,(2.7a)ρ​(∂t𝐯+(𝐯⋅∇)​𝐯)=−∇p−ρ​∇Φ−2​ρ​𝛀c×𝐯−ρ​𝛀c×𝛀c×𝐱+𝔉,\displaystyle\rho\left(\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}\,+\,(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}\right)\,=\,-\nabla p\,-\,\rho\nabla\Phi\,-\,2\,\rho\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{v}\,-\,\rho\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{x}\,+\,\mathfrak{F}\,,(2.7b)∂t𝔰+(𝐯⋅∇)​𝔰=0.\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathfrak{s}+(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla)\mathfrak{s}\,=\,0.(2.7c)

The potentialΦ\Phiis related toρ\rhoviaΔ​Φ=4​π​G​ρ\Delta\Phi=4\pi G\rhowhereGGis the gravitational constant.
The thermodynamic quantitiesρ\rho,pp,𝔰\mathfrak{s}are related by the equation of state
which is of Lagrangian in nature and is listed below inEq.2.16.
InEq.2.7,𝛀c=Ωc​𝐞z\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}=\Omega_{c}\,\mathbf{e}_{z}.
The term𝛀c×𝛀c×𝐱\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{x}is
called the centrifugal force, while𝛀c×𝐯\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{v}is the Coriolis force.
The external source term is represented by𝔉\mathfrak{F}.
The equationEq.2.7cdefines an adiabatic flow, cf.[36, Eq. (2.5)],[61, Eq. (13.3)].

## Remark 1(Centrifugal potential).

The centrifugal term𝛀c×𝛀c×𝐱\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{x}can be written
as666This identity also holds for non-constant rotation.
For a general differential rotation
rate𝛀=𝛀​(r,θ)=Ω​(r,θ)​𝐞z\boldsymbol{\Omega}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}(r,\theta)=\Omega(r,\theta)\mathbf{e}_{z}around axis𝐞z\mathbf{e}_{z}, we have that𝛀×𝛀×𝐱=𝛀​(𝛀⋅𝐱)−𝐱​|𝛀|2=(Ω​z)​𝛀−Ω2​𝐱\boldsymbol{\Omega}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}\times\mathbf{x}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}\cdot\mathbf{x})\,-\,\mathbf{x}\,\lvert\boldsymbol{\Omega}\rvert^{2}\,=\,(\Omega z)\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}\,-\,\Omega^{2}\,\mathbf{x}.
This follows from the identity: for vector𝐚\mathbf{a},𝐛\mathbf{b}, and𝐜\mathbf{c},
we have𝐚×(𝐛×𝐜)=𝐛​(𝐚⋅𝐜)−𝐜​(𝐚⋅𝐛)\mathbf{a}\times(\mathbf{b}\times\mathbf{c})=\mathbf{b}(\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{c})-\mathbf{c}(\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{b}).𝛀c×𝛀c×𝐱=Ωc​(Ωc⋅𝐱)−Ωc2​𝐱.\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{x}=\Omega_{c}(\Omega_{c}\cdot\mathbf{x})-\Omega_{c}^{2}\mathbf{x}.(2.8)

AsΩc\Omega_{c}is constant, the centrifugal force𝛀c×𝛀c×𝐱\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{x}can be written as a gradient of a scalar potential:𝛀c×𝛀c×𝐱=∇𝐱ψS,withψS:=−12​(Ωc2​|𝐱|2−(𝛀c⋅𝐱)2).\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{x}\,\,=\,\,\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\psi^{S},\hskip 28.45274pt\text{with }\quad\psi^{S}:=-\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\Omega_{c}^{2}\lvert\mathbf{x}\rvert^{2}-(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\cdot\mathbf{x})^{2}\right).(2.9)

## Remark 2(Effective potential).

With centrifugal potentialψS\psi^{S}introduced inRemark1,
one refers to(ϕ0+ψS)(\phi_{0}+\psi^{S})as the effective potential, and we can
write∇ϕ0+𝛀c×𝛀c×𝐱=∇(ϕ0+ψS)\nabla\phi_{0}\,+\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{x}=\nabla(\phi_{0}+\psi^{S}).

## Reference state

The parameters characterizing an adiabatic reference state are denoted
with a subscript0:−the density​ρ0-\,\,\text{the density }\rho_{0},−the flow velocity​𝐯0-\,\,\text{the flow velocity }\mathbf{v}_{0},−the pressure​p0-\,\,\text{the pressure }p_{0},(2.10)−the specific entropy​𝔰0-\,\,\text{the specific entropy }\mathfrak{s}_{0}.−the gravitational potential​ϕ0-\,\,\text{the gravitational potential }\phi_{0}.

As inEq.2.6,(ρ0,𝐯0,𝔰0)(\rho_{0},\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathfrak{s}_{0})satisfy
the conservation of mass, momentum and energy,∂tρ0+∇⋅(ρ0​𝐯0)=0,\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho_{0}+\nabla\cdot(\rho_{0}\,\mathbf{v}_{0})\,=\,0\,,(2.11a)ρ0​(∂t𝐯0+(𝐯0⋅∇)​𝐯0)=−∇p0−ρ​∇Φ0−2​ρ0​𝛀c×𝐯0−ρ0​𝛀c×𝛀c×𝐱+𝐟ghost,\displaystyle\rho_{0}\left(\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}_{0}\,+\,(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{0}\right)\,=\,-\nabla p_{0}\,-\,\rho\nabla\Phi_{0}\,-\,2\,\rho_{0}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{v}_{0}\,-\,\rho_{0}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{x}\,+\,\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{ghost}}\,,(2.11b)∂t𝔰0+(𝐯0⋅∇)​𝔰0=0.\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathfrak{s}_{0}+(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\mathfrak{s}_{0}\,=\,0.(2.11c)

This is coupled with the equation of state, cf.Eq.2.16. The potentialϕ0\phi_{0}satisfies the Poisson equation with sourceρ0\rho_{0}, listed below inEq.2.45.
Here, we allow for the contribution of an external
source𝐟ghost\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{ghost}}, cf. alsoEqs.2.27and7for further discussion.
The coefficients of the oscillation equations also employ adiabiatic soundspeedc0c_{0}and adiabatic indexΓ1\Gamma_{1}emerging from the thermodynamic relations
and perturbation of the equation of states which are introduced below inEq.2.36.

## 2.2Small perturbation analysis

Perturbations can be formulated from an Eulerian or a Lagrangian point of view.
Eulerian approach measures changes between the two states at each fixed coordinate,
while the Lagrangian approach describes changes associated
with a fixed collection of virtual particles in the course of their movement by the flow.
In the following discussion, we follow the notation and formalism in[38, Chapter 1];
the same idea could be found in[51, Section 4.3]or[8].Figure 1:Representation of the reference flow𝝌0\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}and perturbed flow𝝌\boldsymbol{\chi}and displacementΔ𝝌L\Delta^{L}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}}defined inEq.2.17.

∙\bulletDenote the trajectory of a virtual particle𝐚\mathbf{a}in the reference flow by𝝌0\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0},
and the perturbed one by𝝌\boldsymbol{\chi}, represented inFigure1.
The position of the particle𝐚\mathbf{a}at timetton the reference flow𝝌0\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}and
on the perturbed flow𝝌\boldsymbol{\chi}are denoted respectively by,𝐱0=𝝌0​(𝐚,t),𝐱†=𝝌​(𝐚,t).\mathbf{x}_{0}=\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}(\mathbf{a},t),\quad\mathbf{x}^{\dagger}=\boldsymbol{\chi}(\mathbf{a},t).(2.12)

The velocities of the two trajectories are respectively denoted by,𝐯0​(𝐚,t):=∂t𝝌0​(𝐚,t),𝐯​(𝐚,t):=∂t𝝌​(𝐚,t).\mathbf{v}_{0}(\mathbf{a},t):=\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}(\mathbf{a},t),\quad\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{a},t):=\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}(\mathbf{a},t).(2.13)

∙\bulletFollowing[38], the notation⋅~\tilde{\cdot}is employed to rewrite a quantity
in Lagrangian coordinates(𝐚,t)(\mathbf{a},t)to Eulerian coordinates(𝐱,t)(\mathbf{x},t)which are related by corresponding flows. For example, for a quantityffand
in association with the reference flow𝝌0\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}, we writef​(𝐱,t)=f~​(𝐚,t),where​𝐱=𝝌0​(𝐚,t).f(\mathbf{x},t)=\tilde{f}(\mathbf{a},t),\quad\text{ where }\mathbf{x}=\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}(\mathbf{a},t).(2.14)

The equation of state relating pressure, density and entropy of the perturbed statesEq.2.6and reference statesEq.2.10are given in Lagrangian coordinates, i.e. cf.[38, Eq. (1.12), (1.17)],p=𝒫​(ρ,𝔰;𝐚),p0=𝒫​(ρ0,𝔰0;𝐚),p=\mathcal{P}(\rho,\mathfrak{s};\mathbf{a}),\hskip 8.5359ptp_{0}=\mathcal{P}(\rho_{0},\mathfrak{s}_{0};\mathbf{a}),(2.15)

or equivalently, cf.[38, Eq. (1.28), (1.32)],p~=𝒫​(ρ~,𝔰~;𝐚),p~0=𝒫​(ρ~0,𝔰~0;𝐚).\tilde{p}=\mathcal{P}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{\mathfrak{s}};\mathbf{a}),\hskip 8.5359pt\tilde{p}_{0}=\mathcal{P}(\tilde{\rho}_{0},\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}_{0};\mathbf{a}).(2.16)

∙\bulletDisplacement vector: Associated with a virtual particle𝐚\mathbf{a},
the Lagrangian displacementΔ~𝝌L\tilde{\Delta}^{L}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}}measures the difference between the two positions inEq.2.12, while the
difference in the particle velocities inEq.2.13are denoted byΔ~𝐯L\tilde{\Delta}^{L}_{\mathbf{v}}, see alsoFigure1,Δ~𝝌L​(𝐚,t):=𝝌​(𝐚,t)−𝝌0​(𝐚;t),Δ~𝐯L​(𝐚,t):=𝐯​(𝐚,t)−𝐯0​(𝐚,t).\tilde{\Delta}^{L}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}}(\mathbf{a},t):=\boldsymbol{\chi}(\mathbf{a},t)-\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}(\mathbf{a};t),\hskip 14.22636pt\tilde{\Delta}_{\mathbf{v}}^{L}(\mathbf{a},t):=\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{a},t)-\mathbf{v}_{0}(\mathbf{a},t).(2.17)

We introduce the quantity𝛏\boldsymbol{\upxi}defined at a fixed position (i.e., Eulerian) in the
observation frame to represent small amplitudeΔ~𝝌L\tilde{\Delta}^{L}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}}:Δ~𝝌L​(𝐚,t)=Δ𝝌L​(𝐱,t)=𝐱†−𝐱=ϵ​𝛏​(𝐱0,t)+𝖮​(ϵ2),where𝐚=𝝌0−1​(𝐱,t),𝐱†=𝝌​(𝐚,t).\begin{gathered}\tilde{\Delta}^{L}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}}(\mathbf{a},t)\,=\,\Delta^{L}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}}(\mathbf{x},t)\,=\,\mathbf{x}^{\dagger}-\mathbf{x}\,=\,\epsilon\,\boldsymbol{\upxi}(\mathbf{x}_{0},t)\,+\,\mathsf{O}(\epsilon^{2}),\\
\hskip 28.45274pt\text{ where }\quad\mathbf{a}=\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}^{-1}(\mathbf{x},t),\quad\mathbf{x}^{\dagger}=\boldsymbol{\chi}(\mathbf{a},t).\end{gathered}(2.18)

In the same manner, we introduce the small Lagrangian perturbation vector denoted byδ𝐯L\updelta^{L}_{\mathbf{v}},Δ~𝐯L​(𝐚,t)=Δ𝐯L​(𝐱,t)=ϵ​δ𝐯L​(𝐱,t)+𝖮​(ϵ2).\tilde{\Delta}_{\mathbf{v}}^{L}(\mathbf{a},t)=\Delta_{\mathbf{v}}^{L}(\mathbf{x},t)=\epsilon\updelta^{L}_{\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{x},t)+\mathsf{O}(\epsilon^{2}).(2.19)

We have the relation betweenδ𝐯L\updelta^{L}_{\mathbf{v}}and𝛏\boldsymbol{\upxi}as, cf.[38, Eq. (1.60)],δ𝐯L​(𝐱,t)=(∂t+(𝐯0⋅∇))​𝛏+𝖮​(ϵ).\updelta^{L}_{\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{x},t)=(\partial_{t}\,+\,(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla))\boldsymbol{\upxi}+\mathsf{O}(\epsilon).(2.20)

∙\bulletLagrangian perturbations:
The Lagrangian perturbation associated with a parameterffdescribes the change offfcompared to a referencef0f_{0}measured on the corresponding flows,ΔfL~(𝐚,t):=f(𝝌(𝐚,t)−f0(𝝌0(𝐚,t)).\widetilde{\Delta^{L}_{f}}(\mathbf{a},t):=f(\boldsymbol{\chi}(\mathbf{a},t)-f_{0}(\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}(\mathbf{a},t)).(2.21)

The small Lagrangian perturbation associated withffis denoted byδfL\updelta^{L}_{f}, cf.[61, Eq. (13.20)],ΔfL~​(𝐚,t)=ΔfL​(𝐱,t)=f​(𝐱,t)−f0​(𝐱0,t)=ϵ​δfL​(𝐱0,t)+𝖮​(ϵ2),where​𝐱0=𝝌0​(𝐚,t),𝐚=𝝌0−1​(𝐱0,t),𝐱=𝝌​(𝐚,t)=𝐱0+ϵ​𝝃+𝖮​(ϵ2).\begin{gathered}\widetilde{\Delta^{L}_{f}}(\mathbf{a},t)=\Delta^{L}_{f}(\mathbf{x},t)=f(\mathbf{x},t)-f_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{0},t)\,=\,\epsilon\,\delta^{L}_{f}(\mathbf{x}_{0},t)\,+\,\mathsf{O}(\epsilon^{2}),\\
\text{ where}\hskip 8.5359pt\mathbf{x}_{0}=\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}(\mathbf{a},t),\quad\mathbf{a}=\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}^{-1}(\mathbf{x}_{0},t),\quad\mathbf{x}=\boldsymbol{\chi}(\mathbf{a},t)=\mathbf{x}_{0}+\epsilon\,\boldsymbol{\xi}+\mathsf{O}(\epsilon^{2}).\end{gathered}(2.22)

∙\bulletEulerian perturbations:
For a quantityff, the Eulerian perturbation at position(𝐱0,t)(\mathbf{x}_{0},t)is denotedδfE\updelta^{E}_{f}, cf.[61, Eq. (13.19)],f​(𝐱0,t)=f0​(𝐱0,t)+ϵ​δfE​(𝐱0,t)+𝖮​(ϵ2).f(\mathbf{x}_{0},t)=f_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{0},t)\,+\,\epsilon\,\updelta^{E}_{f}(\mathbf{x}_{0},t)\,+\,\mathsf{O}(\epsilon^{2}).(2.23)

The relation between the small Eulerian and Lagrangian perturbations are given by,
cf.[38, Eq. (1.63)], or[61, Eq. (13.21)],δfL​(𝐱0,t)=δfE​(𝐱0,t)+𝛏​(𝐱0,t)⋅∇f0​(𝐱0,t)+𝖮​(ϵ).\updelta^{L}_{f}(\mathbf{x}_{0},t)=\updelta^{E}_{f}(\mathbf{x}_{0},t)\,+\,\boldsymbol{\upxi}(\mathbf{x}_{0},t)\cdot\nabla f_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{0},t)+\mathsf{O}(\epsilon).(2.24)

Use this relationEq.2.24andEq.2.20which gives
Lagrangian perturbationδ𝐯L\updelta^{L}_{\mathbf{v}}, we obtain an
expression of the Eulerian vector perturbationδ𝐯E\updelta^{E}_{\mathbf{v}}in terms of𝛏\boldsymbol{\upxi},δ𝐯E=(∂t+(𝐯0⋅∇))​𝛏−(𝝃⋅∇)​𝐯0.\updelta^{E}_{\mathbf{v}}=\left(\partial_{t}\,+\,(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\right)\boldsymbol{\upxi}\,-\,(\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{0}.(2.25)

## Perturbations of conservation laws

We carry out Eulerian perturbations of the momentum equations and then linearize.
For the remaining conservation laws, we follow the approach detailed in[38].
Therein, the conservation laws are rewritten in Lagrangian form and/or coordinates, the Lagrangian perturbations are introduced and linearization is performed on the resulting equations.
For the energy equation, the formalism of[38]is the same as[61], from where we cite the relation among the rates of change of the thermodynamical quantitiesρ\rho,ppand𝔰\mathfrak{s}.

∙\bulletEulerian perturbation of the momentum equation: We replace𝐯\mathbf{v},ρ\rho,ppand source𝔉\mathfrak{F}in expansion of small Eulerian perturbations, in
the conservation of momentumEq.2.7b,𝐯=𝐯0+ϵ​δ𝐯E+𝖮​(ϵ2),ρ=ρ0+ϵ​δρE+𝖮​(ϵ2),p=p0+ϵ​δpE+𝖮​(ϵ2),\displaystyle\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}_{0}+\epsilon\,\updelta^{E}_{\mathbf{v}}+\mathsf{O}(\epsilon^{2}),\hskip 14.22636pt\rho=\rho_{0}+\epsilon\,\updelta^{E}_{\rho}+\mathsf{O}(\epsilon^{2}),\hskip 14.22636ptp=p_{0}+\epsilon\,\updelta^{E}_{p}+\mathsf{O}(\epsilon^{2}),(2.26)𝔉​(𝐱,t)=𝐟ghost​(𝐱,t)+ϵ​𝖥​(𝐱,t)+𝖮​(ϵ2).\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}(\mathbf{x},t)\,=\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{ghost}}}(\mathbf{x},t)\,+\,\epsilon\,\mathsf{F}(\mathbf{x},t)+\mathsf{O}(\epsilon^{2}).(2.27)

By keeping only terms of orderϵ\epsilon, we obtain the following linear equation,ρ0​(∂tδ𝐯E+(𝐯0⋅∇)​δ𝐯E+(δ𝐯E⋅∇)​𝐯0+2​𝛀c×δ𝐯E)=−∇δpE−δρE​𝐘Ω+𝖥,\rho_{0}\left(\partial_{t}\updelta^{E}_{\mathbf{v}}\,+\,(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\updelta^{E}_{\mathbf{v}}+(\updelta^{E}_{\mathbf{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{0}\,+2\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\updelta^{E}_{\mathbf{v}}\right)\,=\,-\nabla\updelta^{E}_{p}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}-}\,\updelta^{E}_{\rho}\,\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}\,+\,\mathsf{F},(2.28)

where𝐘Ω\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}denotes𝐘Ω:=∇ϕ0+𝛀c×𝛀c×𝐱+(𝐯0⋅∇)​𝐯0+2​𝛀c×𝐯0.\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}\,:=\,\nabla\phi_{0}\,+\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{x}\,+\,(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{0}\,+\,2\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{v}_{0}\,.(2.29)

∙\bulletPerturbations of the conservation of mass:
Following[38], we first rewrite in Lagrangian form
the conservation of mass, currently listed in Eulerian form inEq.2.7aandEq.2.11a.
Denote byJJandJ0J_{0}the Jacobien of the diffeomorphisms𝝌\boldsymbol{\chi}and𝝌0\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}, cf.[38, Eq. (1.18)], we have𝐱=𝝌​(𝐚,t),F=(∂xi∂aα),α,i=1,2,3,J=det​F,\displaystyle\mathbf{x}=\boldsymbol{\chi}(\mathbf{a},t),\quad F=\left(\dfrac{\partial x_{i}}{\partial a_{\alpha}}\right),\,\,\alpha,i=1,2,3,\quad J=\mathrm{det}\,F,(2.30a)𝐱0=𝝌0​(𝐚,t),F0=(∂xi∂aα),α,i=1,2,3,J0=det​F0.\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{0}=\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}(\mathbf{a},t),\quad F_{0}=\left(\dfrac{\partial x_{i}}{\partial a_{\alpha}}\right),\,\,\alpha,i=1,2,3,\quad J_{0}=\mathrm{det}\,F_{0}.(2.30b)

Then, the conservation of mass can be written as in Lagrangian form,
cf.[38, Eq. (1.23), (1.33)]or[11, Section 1.1 p.11],J​ρ~=ρ𝐚~=J0​ρ~0,J\,\tilde{\rho}\,=\,\tilde{\rho_{\mathbf{a}}}\,=\,J_{0}\,\tilde{\rho}_{0},(2.31)

whereρ~\tilde{\rho}is the mass of the collection of particles𝐚\mathbf{a}at timett, andρ𝐚~\tilde{\rho_{\mathbf{a}}}at an initial time. We introduce the Lagrangian perturbation associated withρ\rhoand the JacobianJJ, to obtain,δρL~=−ρ0~​δJLJ0+𝖮​(ϵ),cf.[38, Eq. (1.45)]\displaystyle\widetilde{\updelta^{L}_{\rho}}=-\widetilde{\uprho_{0}}\dfrac{\updelta_{J}^{L}}{J_{0}}+\mathsf{O}(\epsilon),\hskip 8.5359pt\text{cf. \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{legendre2003rayonnement}{}{}, Eq. (1.45)]}}(2.32)⇒\displaystyle\RightarrowδρL=−ρ0​∇⋅𝛏+𝖮​(ϵ),cf.[38, Eq. (1.57)].\displaystyle\updelta_{\rho}^{L}=-\rho_{0}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\upxi}+\mathsf{O}(\epsilon),\hskip 8.5359pt\text{cf. \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{legendre2003rayonnement}{}{}, Eq. (1.57)]}}.

∙\bulletPerturbations of the conservation of energy:
The conservation of energy is written in Lagrangian coordinates(𝐚,t)(\mathbf{a},t)using⋅~\tilde{\cdot}notation with𝔰~​(𝐚,t)\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}(\mathbf{a},t),𝔰0~​(𝐚,t)\tilde{\mathfrak{s}_{0}}(\mathbf{a},t),∂t𝔰~​(𝐚,t)=0,\displaystyle\partial_{t}\,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}(\mathbf{a},t)=0,∂t𝔰~0​(𝐚,t)=0,cf.[38, Eqs (1.27, 1.31)]\displaystyle\partial_{t}\,\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}_{0}(\mathbf{a},t)=0,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{cf. \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{legendre2003rayonnement}{}{}, Eqs (1.27, 1.31)]} }(2.34)⇒∂tδ𝔰L~​(𝐚,t)=𝖮​(ϵ)\displaystyle\Rightarrow\hskip 5.69046pt\partial_{t}\,\widetilde{\delta^{L}_{\mathfrak{s}}}(\mathbf{a},t)=\mathsf{O}(\epsilon)⇒δ𝔰L=𝖮​(ϵ),cf.[38, Eqs (1.47, 1.48)].\displaystyle\Rightarrow\hskip 8.5359pt\delta^{L}_{\mathfrak{s}}=\mathsf{O}(\epsilon),\hskip 14.22636pt\text{cf. \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{legendre2003rayonnement}{}{}, Eqs (1.47, 1.48)]} }.

∙\bulletPerturbations of the equation of state: This computation is important in providing
the relation between the perturbations of pressure and density. Recall fromEq.2.16thatp~=𝒫​(ρ~,𝔰~;𝐚),p~0=𝒫​(ρ~0,𝔰~0;𝐚).\tilde{p}=\mathcal{P}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{\mathfrak{s}};\mathbf{a}),\hskip 14.22636pt\tilde{p}_{0}=\mathcal{P}(\tilde{\rho}_{0},\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}_{0};\mathbf{a}).

The derivation in[61]and[38, Eqs (1.48, 1.49)]lead to the same result.
The perturbations of the equation of state are carried out in terms of Lagrangian perturbation,
cf.[61, Section 13.4 p.98]. In particular, we have, cf.[61, Eq. (13.79)], modulo𝖮​(ϵ)\mathsf{O}(\epsilon),δρLρ0=1Γ1​δpLp0+∇adρ0​T0p0​δ𝔰L,\dfrac{\updelta^{L}_{\rho}}{\rho_{0}}=\dfrac{1}{\Gamma_{1}}\dfrac{\updelta^{L}_{p}}{p_{0}}+\nabla_{\mathrm{ad}}\dfrac{\rho_{0}T_{0}}{p_{0}}\updelta_{\mathfrak{s}}^{L},(2.35)

where the adiabatic gradient∇ad\nabla_{\mathrm{ad}}, the adiabatic indexΓ1\Gamma_{1}and sound speedc02c_{0}^{2}are defined as, cf[61, Eq (13.44)],∇ad=(∂ln⁡T0∂ln⁡p0)𝔰,Γ1=(∂ln⁡p0∂ln⁡ρ0)𝔰,c02:=Γ1​p0ρ0=∂ρ0𝒫.\nabla_{\mathrm{ad}}=\left(\dfrac{\partial\ln T_{0}}{\partial\ln p_{0}}\right)_{\mathfrak{s}},\hskip 20.00003pt\Gamma_{1}=\left(\dfrac{\partial\ln p_{0}}{\partial\ln\rho_{0}}\right)_{\mathfrak{s}},\hskip 20.00003ptc_{0}^{2}:=\dfrac{\Gamma_{1}p_{0}}{\rho_{0}}=\partial_{\rho_{0}}\mathcal{P}.(2.36)

The second expression ofc02c_{0}^{2}is given by[38, Eq. (149)]and agrees with the definitions in[61],c02:=∂ρ0𝒫=(∂p0∂ρ0)𝔰=(∂ln⁡p0∂ln⁡ρ0)𝔰​p0ρ0=Γ1​p0ρ0,with​Γ1:=(∂ln⁡p0∂ln⁡ρ0)𝔰.c_{0}^{2}:=\partial_{\rho_{0}}\mathcal{P}=\left(\dfrac{\partial p_{0}}{\partial\rho_{0}}\right)_{\mathfrak{s}}=\left(\dfrac{\partial\ln p_{0}}{\partial\ln\rho_{0}}\right)_{\mathfrak{s}}\dfrac{p_{0}}{\rho_{0}}=\Gamma_{1}\dfrac{p_{0}}{\rho_{0}},\quad\text{ with }\Gamma_{1}:=\left(\dfrac{\partial\ln p_{0}}{\partial\ln\rho_{0}}\right)_{\mathfrak{s}}.

If we assume that the perturbation does not introduce change in entropy for
each virtual particle, i.e.δ𝔰L=0\updelta^{L}_{\mathfrak{s}}=0,
which is given byEq.2.34, then we obtain a direct relation betweenδpL\delta^{L}_{p}andδpE\delta^{E}_{p},δ𝔰L=0⇒δρL=δpLc02.\updelta_{\mathfrak{s}}^{L}=0\hskip 11.38092pt\Rightarrow\hskip 17.07182pt\updelta^{L}_{\rho}\,=\,\frac{\updelta^{L}_{p}}{c_{0}^{2}}.(2.37)

In employingEq.2.32andEq.2.24, we obtain, cf.[38, Eq. (1.68)],δpE=δpL−(𝛏⋅∇)​p0=−ρ0​c02​∇⋅𝛏−(𝛏⋅∇)​p0.\updelta_{p}^{E}=\updelta_{p}^{L}-(\boldsymbol{\upxi}\cdot\nabla)p_{0}=-\rho_{0}c_{0}^{2}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\upxi}-(\boldsymbol{\upxi}\cdot\nabla)p_{0}.(2.38)

From the same identities, we can obtain the relationδpE=c02​δρE+𝛏⋅(−∇p0+c02​∇ρ0).\updelta^{E}_{p}=c_{0}^{2}\updelta^{E}_{\rho}+\boldsymbol{\upxi}\cdot\left(-\nabla p_{0}+c_{0}^{2}\nabla\rho_{0}\right).(2.39)

It can also be found in[61, Eq. (14.1)]and[51, Eq. (4.32)-(4.34)]for the radial symmetric background.

## Remark 3(No-resonance assumption).

As mentioned above, another approach
to arrive at the oscillation equations is to introduce the Eulerian perturbations
to the conservation laws, and then linearize, as was done in[27].
The small Eulerian perturbationsδρE\updelta^{E}_{\rho},δpE\updelta^{E}_{p}, andδ𝐯E\updelta_{\mathbf{v}}^{E}, satisfy the system of linear equations,{∂tδρE+∇⋅(δρE​𝐯0+δ𝐯E​ρ0)=0,ρ0​(∂tδ𝐯E+(𝐯0⋅∇)​δ𝐯E+(δ𝐯E⋅∇)​𝐯0+2​𝛀c×δ𝐯E)=−∇δpE−δρE​𝐘Ω+F.\begin{dcases}\partial_{t}\updelta_{\rho}^{E}+\nabla\cdot(\updelta_{\rho}^{E}\mathbf{v}_{0}+\updelta_{\mathbf{v}}^{E}\rho_{0})\,=\,0,\\[3.00003pt]
\rho_{0}\left(\partial_{t}\updelta_{\mathbf{v}}^{E}\,+\,(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\updelta_{\mathbf{v}}^{E}+(\updelta_{\mathbf{v}}^{E}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{0}\,+2\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\updelta_{\mathbf{v}}^{E}\right)\,=\,-\nabla\updelta_{p}^{E}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}-}\,\updelta_{\rho}^{E}\,\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}\,+\,F.\end{dcases}(2.40)

In takingDt=∂t+(𝐯0⋅∇)D_{t}=\partial_{t}+(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla),
the first equation can be written asρ0​Dt​(δρE+∇⋅(ρ0⋅𝝃)ρ0)=0.\rho_{0}D_{t}\left(\dfrac{\updelta_{\rho}^{E}+\nabla\cdot(\rho_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi})}{\rho_{0}}\right)=0\,.(2.41)

Employing the identity which gives the commutator
between the material derivative𝛏⋅∇\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nablaandDtD_{t},
cf.[27, Eq. (15)], we get,𝐰⋅∇Q=Dt​(𝝃⋅∇Q)−(𝝃⋅∇)​Dt​Q.\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla Q=D_{t}(\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla Q)-(\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla)D_{t}Q.(2.42)

FromEq.2.41, the relationEq.2.33is imposed as an assumption,
which is referred to as the no-resonance assumption in[31], cf. Assumption 27 therein.

Regarding the conservation of energy,
Eulerian perturbation was employed in[27],
to obtainDt​δ𝔰E+δ𝐯E⋅∇𝔰0=0D_{t}\delta^{E}_{\mathfrak{s}}+\delta_{\mathbf{v}}^{E}\cdot\nabla_{\mathfrak{s}_{0}}=0,
which was then rewritten as, cf.[27, Eq. (22)],Dt​(δ𝔰E+𝛏⋅𝔰0)=0.D_{t}\left(\delta^{E}_{\mathfrak{s}}+\boldsymbol{\upxi}\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{0}\right)=0.

From the above equation to the identityEq.2.34,δ𝔰E+𝛏⋅𝔰0=0\delta^{E}_{\mathfrak{s}}+\boldsymbol{\upxi}\cdot\mathfrak{s}_{0}=0was then taken as an assumption in[27, Eq. (22)]. It is here that the name ‘no resonance’ assumption was introduced,
as noted in[31].

## 2.3Time-harmonic equations of oscillations in Cowling’s approximation

We now employ equationEq.2.28and the relation between
the displacement and Eulerian perturbations to derive the oscillation equations.

## Adiabatic time-invariant background

We assume that the background is time-invariant, which includes the source:ρ0​(𝐱,t)=ρ0​(𝐱),p0​(𝐱,t)=p0​(𝐱),𝐯0​(𝐱,t)=𝐯0​(𝐱),ϕ0​(𝐱,t)=ϕ0​(𝐱),𝐟ghost​(𝐱,t)=𝐟ghost​(𝐱).\begin{gathered}\rho_{0}(\mathbf{x},t)=\rho_{0}(\mathbf{x}),\quad p_{0}(\mathbf{x},t)=p_{0}(\mathbf{x}),\quad\mathbf{v}_{0}(\mathbf{x},t)=\mathbf{v}_{0}(\mathbf{x}),\\
\phi_{0}(\mathbf{x},t)=\phi_{0}(\mathbf{x}),\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{ghost}}}(\mathbf{x},t)={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{ghost}}}(\mathbf{x}).\end{gathered}(2.43)

The conservation of mass and momentum inEq.2.11simplify to,∇⋅(ρ0​𝐯0)=0,\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\nabla\cdot(\rho_{0}\mathbf{v}_{0})=0\,,(2.44a)ρ0​(𝐯0⋅∇)​𝐯0+ρ0​∇ϕ0+2​ρ0​𝛀c×𝐯0+ρ0​𝛀c×𝛀c×𝐱=−∇p0+𝐟ghost.\displaystyle\rho_{0}\,(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{0}+\rho_{0}\nabla\phi_{0}+2\rho_{0}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{v}_{0}+\rho_{0}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{x}\,=\,-\nabla p_{0}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{ghost}}}.(2.44b)

The gravitational potentialϕ0\phi_{0}satisfies,Δ​ϕ0=4​π​G​ρ0,withϕ0→0​as​|𝐱|→∞.\Delta\phi_{0}=4\pi G\rho_{0},\quad\text{with }\quad\phi_{0}\rightarrow 0\text{ as }\lvert\mathbf{x}\rvert\rightarrow\infty.(2.45)

We also recall the adiabatic sound speedc02​(𝐱)c_{0}^{2}(\mathbf{x})and adiabatic indexΓ1​(𝐱)\Gamma_{1}(\mathbf{x}), fromEq.2.36,c02​ρ0=Γ1​p0.c_{0}^{2}\,\rho_{0}\,=\,\Gamma_{1}\,p_{0}.(2.46)

We have introduced𝐘Ω\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}inEq.2.29, which now denotes the left-hand side ofEq.2.44bdivided byρ0\rho_{0},𝐘Ω:=∇ϕ0+𝛀c×𝛀c×𝐱+(𝐯0⋅∇)​𝐯0+2​𝛀c×𝐯0.\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}\,:=\,\nabla\phi_{0}\,+\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{x}\,+\,(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{0}\,+\,2\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{v}_{0}\,.(2.47)

The background constraintEq.2.44bcan thus be written as,𝐘Ω=−∇p0ρ0+𝐟ghostρ0.\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}\,=\,-\dfrac{\nabla p_{0}}{\rho_{0}}\,+\,\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{ghost}}}}{\rho_{0}}\,.(2.48)

## Remark 4.

The value of𝐘Ω\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}and thus of both sides of background constraintEq.2.48are independent of the observation frame, specifically ofΩc\Omega_{c}.
We show this fact for backgrounds with only differential rotation
inSubsection3.1,Identity2.

We assume that the forces and solutions are time-harmonic,
following convention,𝖥​(𝐱,t)=F​(𝐱)​e−i​ω​t,𝛏​(𝐱,t)=𝝃​(𝐱)​e−i​ω​t,δ𝐯E​(𝐱,t)=δ𝐯E​(𝐱)​e−i​ω​t,δρE​(𝐱,t)=δρE​(𝐱)​e−i​ω​t,δpE​(𝐱,t)=δpE​(𝐱)​e−i​ω​t.\begin{gathered}\mathsf{F}(\mathbf{x},t)=F(\mathbf{x})\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t},\quad\boldsymbol{\upxi}(\mathbf{x},t)=\boldsymbol{\xi}(\mathbf{x})\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t},\quad\updelta^{E}_{\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{x},t)=\updelta^{E}_{\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{x})\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t},\\[3.00003pt]
\updelta^{E}_{\rho}(\mathbf{x},t)=\delta^{E}_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t},\quad\updelta^{E}_{p}(\mathbf{x},t)=\delta^{E}_{p}(\mathbf{x})\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}.\end{gathered}(2.49)

Recall fromEqs.2.28,2.39and2.33, one has obtained
the following relation between𝝃\boldsymbol{\xi}and the Eulerian perturbations
(listed in the frequency domain), cf.[38, p.30],[14],δ𝐯E=(−i​ω)​𝛏+(𝐯0⋅∇)​𝝃−(𝝃⋅∇)​𝐯0,\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\delta^{E}_{\mathbf{v}}=(-\mathrm{i}\omega)\boldsymbol{\upxi}+(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\xi}-(\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{0},(2.50a)δρE=−∇⋅(ρ0​𝝃),δpE=−ρ0​c02​∇⋅𝝃−(𝝃⋅∇)​p0.\displaystyle\delta^{E}_{\rho}=-\nabla\cdot(\rho_{0}\boldsymbol{\xi}),\hskip 28.45274pt\delta_{p}^{E}=-\rho_{0}c_{0}^{2}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}-(\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla)p_{0}.(2.50b)

We will employ these in the linearized Eulerian perturbation of momentumEq.2.28relisted below,ρ0​(−i​ω​δ𝐯E+(𝐯0⋅∇)​δ𝐯E+(δ𝐯E⋅∇)​𝐯0+2​𝛀0×δ𝐯E)=−∇δpE−δρE​𝐘Ω+F.\rho_{0}\left(-\mathrm{i}\omega\,\delta^{E}_{\mathbf{v}}\,+\,(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\delta^{E}_{\mathbf{v}}+(\delta^{E}_{\mathbf{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{0}\,+2\,\boldsymbol{\Upomega}_{0}\times\delta^{E}_{\mathbf{v}}\right)\,=\,-\nabla\delta^{E}_{p}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}-}\,\delta^{E}_{\rho}\,\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}\,+\,F.(2.51)

To obtain the oscillation equations, we
needIdentity1to rewrite
the left-hand sied ofEq.2.51in terms of𝝃\boldsymbol{\xi}, which
allows to rewriteEq.2.51asρ0​[(−i​ω+𝐯0⋅∇)2+2​𝛀c×(−i​ω+𝐯0⋅∇)−2​𝛀c×∇𝐯0−∇(𝐯0⋅∇𝐯0)]​𝝃=−∇δpE−δρE​𝐘Ω+F.\begin{gathered}\rho_{0}\bigg[\big(-\mathrm{i}\omega+\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\big)^{2}+2\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times(-\mathrm{i}\omega+\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)-2\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0}-\nabla(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0})\bigg]\boldsymbol{\xi}\\
\,=\,-\nabla\delta^{E}_{p}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}-}\,\delta^{E}_{\rho}\,\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}\,+\,F.\end{gathered}(2.52)

## Identity 1.

We have the following equality,Left-hand side ofEq.2.51(2.53)=ρ0​[(−i​ω+𝐯0⋅∇)2+2​𝛀c×(−i​ω+𝐯0⋅∇)−2​𝛀c×∇𝐯0−∇(𝐯0⋅∇𝐯0)]​𝝃.\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\rho_{0}\bigg[\big(-\mathrm{i}\omega+\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\big)^{2}+2\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times(-\mathrm{i}\omega+\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)-2\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0}-\nabla(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0})\bigg]\boldsymbol{\xi}.

Here, the last term is written as a matrix-vector product between the
matrix∇(𝐯0⋅∇𝐯0)\nabla(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0})and the
vector𝛏\boldsymbol{\xi}; this is equivalently written as two material derivatives,[∇(𝐯0⋅∇𝐯0)]​𝝃=(𝝃⋅∇)​(𝐯0⋅∇)​𝐯0.\big[\nabla(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0})\big]\boldsymbol{\xi}\,=\,(\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla)(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{0}.(2.54)

## Proof.

We need to compute the commutator of two material derivatives.
To simplify the notation in the derivations, the parenthesis in the notation of material derivatives is dropped where there is no ambiguity,(𝐯⋅∇𝐰)=(𝐯⋅∇)​𝐰.(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{w})=(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{w}.(2.55)

In Cartesian basis, we recall the(i,j)(i,j)-component of∇V\nabla Vfor vectorV=(vi)V=(v_{i}),(∇V)i​j=∂jvi,withithe row index andjthe column index.(\nabla V)_{ij}=\partial_{j}v_{i},\quad\text{ with $i$ the row index and $j$ the column index}.(2.56)

∙\bulletConsider the vectors𝐯\mathbf{v},𝐰\mathbf{w}andGGwith their Cartesian components𝐯=(vi)\mathbf{v}=(v_{i}),𝐰=(wi)\mathbf{w}=(w_{i}), andG=(gi)G=(g_{i}).
Two material derivatives acting on the vectorGG, first in the direction of𝐰\mathbf{w}then along𝐯\mathbf{v}, give((𝐯⋅∇)​(𝐰⋅∇)​G)i\displaystyle\big(\,(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla)(\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla)G\,\big)_{i}=∑kvk​∂k(∑jwj​∂jgi)=∑k,jvk​(∂kwj)​∂jgi+∑k,jwj​vk​∂j∂kgi\displaystyle\,\,=\,\,\sum_{k}v_{k}\partial_{k}\Big(\sum_{j}w_{j}\partial_{j}\,g_{i}\Big)\,\,=\,\,\sum_{k,j}v_{k}(\partial_{k}w_{j})\,\partial_{j}\,g_{i}\,+\,\sum_{k,j}w_{j}\,v_{k}\,\partial_{j}\,\partial_{k}g_{i}(2.57)=∑k,jvk​(∂kwj)​∂jgi+∑k,jwj​∂j(vk​∂kgi)−∑k,j(∂kgi)​(∂jvk)​wj.\displaystyle=\sum_{k,j}v_{k}(\partial_{k}w_{j})\,\partial_{j}g_{i}\,+\,\sum_{k,j}w_{j}\partial_{j}\,(v_{k}\partial_{k}\,g_{i})\,-\,\sum_{k,j}(\partial_{k}g_{i})(\partial_{j}v_{k})w_{j}.

From the above expressions, we obtain(𝐯⋅∇)​(𝐰⋅∇)​G=[(𝐯⋅∇𝐰)⋅∇]​G⏟(∇G)⋅(𝐯⋅∇𝐰)+(𝐰⋅∇)​(𝐯⋅∇)​G−[(𝐰⋅∇𝐯)⋅∇]​G⏟(∇G)⋅(𝐰⋅∇𝐯).(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla)(\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla)G=\underbrace{\big[(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{w})\,\cdot\,\nabla\big]G}_{(\nabla G)\,\cdot\,\left(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{w}\right)}\,\,+\,\,(\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla)(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla)G-\underbrace{\big[(\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v})\,\cdot\,\nabla\big]G}_{(\nabla G)\cdot\big(\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}\big)}.(2.58)

In the expressions under the under-braces, the first and third term are equivalently rewritten as matrix-vector products with matrix∇G\nabla G.

∙\bulletSecondly, from the first equality inEq.2.57, we can also rewrite two material derivatives as a matrix-vector product,(𝐯⋅∇)​(𝐰⋅∇)​F=[∇(𝐰⋅∇F)]⏟matrix given by gradientof vector(𝐰⋅∇)​F​𝐯.(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla)(\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla)F\,\,=\,\,\underbrace{\left[\nabla\left(\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla\,F\right)\right]}_{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{ matrix given by gradient}\\
\text{ of vector $(\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla)F$}\end{array}}\mathbf{v}.(2.59)

∙\bulletWe now return to left-hand side
ofEq.2.51.
Replacingδ𝐯E\delta_{\mathbf{v}}^{E}byEq.2.50a,
after some rearrangement, we obtainLhs ofEq.2.51=(−i​ω)​[(−i​ω)​𝝃+𝐯0⋅∇𝝃−𝝃⋅∇𝐯0]+𝐯0⋅∇[(−i​ω)​𝝃+𝐯0⋅∇𝝃−𝝃⋅∇𝐯0]\displaystyle=\,(-\mathrm{i}\omega)\left[(-\mathrm{i}\omega)\boldsymbol{\xi}+\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{\xi}-\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0}\right]\,+\,\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\left[(-\mathrm{i}\omega)\boldsymbol{\xi}+\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{\xi}-\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0}\right](2.60)+[(−i​ω)​𝝃+𝐯0⋅∇𝝃−𝝃⋅∇𝐯0]⋅∇𝐯0+2​𝛀0×[(−i​ω)​𝝃+𝐯0⋅∇𝝃−𝝃⋅∇𝐯0]\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\left[(-\mathrm{i}\omega)\boldsymbol{\xi}+\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{\xi}-\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0}\right]\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0}\,+2\,\boldsymbol{\Upomega}_{0}\times\left[(-\mathrm{i}\omega)\boldsymbol{\xi}\,\,+\,\,\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{\xi}\,\,-\,\,\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0}\right]=[(−i​ω)+𝐯0⋅∇]2​𝝃+2​𝛀0×[(−i​ω)​𝝃+𝐯0⋅∇𝝃−𝝃⋅∇𝐯0]\displaystyle=\left[(-\mathrm{i}\omega)+\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\right]^{2}\boldsymbol{\xi}\,\,+2\,\boldsymbol{\Upomega}_{0}\times\left[(-\mathrm{i}\omega)\boldsymbol{\xi}\,\,+\,\,\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{\xi}\,\,-\,\,\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0}\right]−(𝐯0⋅∇)​(𝝃⋅∇𝐯0)+[(𝐯0⋅∇𝝃)⋅∇]​𝐯0−[(𝝃⋅∇𝐯0)⋅∇]​𝐯0⏟−(𝝃⋅∇)​(𝐯0⋅∇)​𝐯0.\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\underbrace{-\,(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\left(\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0}\right)\,+\,\left[\left(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{\xi}\right)\,\cdot\,\nabla\right]\mathbf{v}_{0}\,-\,\left[\left(\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0}\right)\cdot\nabla\right]\mathbf{v}_{0}}_{-(\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla)(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{0}}.

The simplification of the last three terms is obtained by employing the identityEq.2.58,−(𝐯0⋅∇)​(𝝃⋅∇)​𝐯0=−[(𝐯0⋅∇)​𝝃⋅∇]​𝐯0−(𝝃⋅∇)​(𝐯0⋅∇)​𝐯0+[(𝝃⋅∇)​𝐯0⋅∇]​𝐯0.-(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)(\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{0}=-[(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\xi}\,\cdot\,\nabla]\mathbf{v}_{0}\,-\,(\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla)(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{0}\,+\,[(\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{0}\,\cdot\,\nabla]\mathbf{v}_{0}.(2.61)

To arrive at the final expressionEq.2.53, we use identityEq.2.59which leads toEq.2.54.

## Oscillation equations

The system of equations of motion are given byEq.2.52together with the relationsEq.2.50bbetween𝝃\boldsymbol{\xi}and
the Eulerian perturbations of pressure and density. We refer to this as the mixed formulation.
We also introduce an acoustic attenuation
termγatt\gamma_{\mathrm{att}}, as was done in[4].
The working system in terms of unknown(𝝃,δpE)(\boldsymbol{\xi},\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{E}})is given
on𝔹⊙\mathbb{B}_{\odot}as,ρ0​[(−i​ω+𝐯0⋅∇)2+2​𝛀c×(−i​ω+𝐯0⋅∇)−2​𝛀c×∇𝐯0−∇(𝐯0⋅∇𝐯0)−2​i​ω​γatt]​𝝃\displaystyle\rho_{0}\bigg[\big(-\mathrm{i}\omega+\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\big)^{2}+2\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times(-\mathrm{i}\omega+\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla)-2\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0}-\nabla(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0})\,-\,2\mathrm{i}\omega\gamma_{\mathrm{att}}\bigg]\boldsymbol{\xi}(2.62a)−𝐘Ω​∇⋅(ρ0​𝝃)+∇δpE=𝐠,\displaystyle\hskip 100.00015pt\,-\,\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}\,\nabla\cdot(\rho_{0}\boldsymbol{\xi})\,+\,\nabla\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{E}}\,=\,\mathbf{g}\,,\,δpE+𝝃⋅∇p0+ρ0​c02​∇⋅𝝃=0.\displaystyle\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{E}}\,+\,\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla p_{0}\,+\,\rho_{0}c_{0}^{2}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}\,=\,0\,.(2.62b)

The systemEq.2.62is closed by imposing on∂𝔹⊙\partial\mathbb{B}_{\odot}a vanishing
Lagrangian perturbation in pressure777The original form of the condition is,δpL=0\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{L}}\,=\,0. The working formEq.2.63is obtained by employing the relation between Lagrangian and Eulerian perturbation,δpL=−ρ​c02​∇⋅𝝃=δpE+𝝃⋅∇p0\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{L}}=-\rho c_{0}^{2}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}=\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{E}}+\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla p_{0}.,[14],δpE+𝝃⋅∇p0=0,on​∂𝔹⊙,Vacuum boundary condition.\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{E}}+\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla p_{0}\,=\,0\,,\quad\text{on }\partial\mathbb{B}_{\odot}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{Vacuum boundary condition.}(2.63)

Recall the term𝐯0⋅∇\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nablainEq.2.62ais a material derivative, cf.Eq.2.2, and[∇(𝐯0⋅∇𝐯0)]​𝝃[\nabla(\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0})]\boldsymbol{\xi}has a second interpretation inEq.2.54.

## Original-div formulationℒodiv\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{odiv}}

By using equationEq.2.62b,∇⋅𝝃\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}is expressed as a linear
combination of𝝃\boldsymbol{\xi}andδpE\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{E}}:∇⋅(ρ0​𝝃)=𝝃⋅(∇ρ0−∇p0c02)−δpEc02.\nabla\cdot(\rho_{0}\boldsymbol{\xi})=\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\left(\nabla\rho_{0}-\dfrac{\nabla p_{0}}{c_{0}^{2}}\right)-\dfrac{\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{E}}}{c_{0}^{2}}\,.(2.64)

By employing this identity to replace888This is seen as follows:

## Scalar Green’s kernel to scalar source

We are interested in the scalar response
given byδpE\delta^{E}_{p}to model helioseismic observables,
cf.Subsection7.1. We thus introduce the following Green’s kernel.

## Definition 1.

The scalar pressure perturbation Green’s kernel,Gp​(𝐱,𝐬)G_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s})is defined as follows,w​(⋅,𝐬):=Gp​(𝐱,𝐬)​is a distributional solution to​ℒodiv​(𝒖w)=(0δ​(𝐱−𝐬)).w(\cdot,\mathbf{s}):=G_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s})\,\,\text{ is a distributional solution to }\,\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{odiv}}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{u}\\
w\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s})\end{pmatrix}.(2.70)

The Green’s kernel provides the solution
toℒodiv\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{odiv}}with only a scalar sourcehh, i.e.,w=∫𝔹⊙Gp​(𝐱,𝐬)​h​(𝐬)​𝑑𝐬solvesℒodiv​(𝒖w)=(0h).w=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\odot}}G_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s})\,h(\mathbf{s})\,d\mathbf{s}\quad\text{ solves }\quad\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{odiv}}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{u}\\
w\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
h\end{pmatrix}\,.(2.71)

In the following remarks, we relate EquationEq.2.62to existing forms in literature.

EquationEq.2.62is the same as[30, (2.1)–(2.4)]by Gough & Thompson for differential rotation flow𝐯0\mathbf{v}_{0}, without magnetics’ effects and in Cowling’s approximation. Here the constraintEq.2.48with𝐟ghost=0\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{ghost}}=0is employed to yield𝐘Ω=−∇p0ρ0\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}=-\dfrac{\nabla p_{0}}{\rho_{0}}, and the equation of motionEq.2.62ais equivalently written as,Eq.2.62a⟺ρ0​𝑩0Ω​𝝃=−∇δpE+δρE​∇p0ρ0+𝐠\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\hskip 8.5359pt\rho_{0}\boldsymbol{B}_{0}^{\Omega}\boldsymbol{\xi}=-\nabla\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{E}}+\delta^{E}_{\rho}\dfrac{\nabla p_{0}}{\rho_{0}}+\mathbf{g}(2.72a)⟺ρ0​𝑩0Ω​𝝃=−∇δpE−(∇⋅𝝃)​∇p0−(𝝃⋅∇ρ0)​∇p0ρ0+𝐠.\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\hskip 8.5359pt\rho_{0}\boldsymbol{B}_{0}^{\Omega}\boldsymbol{\xi}=-\nabla\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{E}}-(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi})\nabla p_{0}-(\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla\rho_{0})\dfrac{\nabla p_{0}}{\rho_{0}}+\mathbf{g}.(2.72b)

In previous work[4,49], we employed

## 3Equations of oscillations with differential rotation

For the remaining of the investigation, we considerEq.2.62for backgrounds where the only nonzero contribution to the flow𝐯0\mathbf{v}_{0}is due to rotation. More specifically, we consider two types of backgrounds:
non-rotating backgrounds (Ωc=Ω=0\Omega_{c}=\Omega=0, i.e.,𝐯0=0\mathbf{v}_{0}=0),
and differentially rotating ones defined inDefinition2,
which yields axisymmetric systems.
As another property, for non-rotating background,𝑨0Ω=0\boldsymbol{A}_{0}^{\Omega=0}inEq.2.68is a multiplicative operator;
and it can be decomposed to such for differentially rotating backgrounds.
This allows to introduce the Liouville change of variable, which
yields the variants inSubsection3.2employed for the numerical computations.
These types of backgrounds also allow to relate the system of
equations with a second order scalar equation, which helps shed
light on the properties of solutions, cf.Subsection3.4.

## 3.1Differential rotation background

The cylindrical coordinate system used,(η,ϕ,z)(\eta,\phi,z),
is related to the Cartesian coordinates𝐱=(x,y,z)\mathbf{x}=(x,y,z),
and radial distancerr, asη2=x2+y2,x=η​cos⁡ϕ,y=η​sin⁡ϕ,r2=η2+z2.\eta^{2}=x^{2}+y^{2}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptx\,=\,\eta\cos\phi\,,\hskip 20.00003pty\,=\,\eta\sin\phi\,,\quad r^{2}=\eta^{2}+z^{2}.(3.1)

The corresponding orthonormal basis is(𝐞η,𝐞ϕ,𝐞z)(\mathbf{e}_{\eta},\mathbf{e}_{\phi},\mathbf{e}_{z}),
and we denote by𝐞r\mathbf{e}_{r}the radial vector.

## Definition 2(Differential flow backgrounds).By a differential flow background,
we refer to background parameters
satisfying the following properties:–In addition toEq.2.44, the background parameters,ρ0\rho_{0},p0p_{0},Γ1\Gamma_{1}andΦ0\Phi_{0}are independent
ofϕ\phi.–The body is in rotation around axis𝐞z\mathbf{e}_{z}with angular rateΩ​(r,θ)\Omega(r,\theta).–The background flow𝐯0\mathbf{v}_{0}inEq.2.44is only induced by rotation, which means in the observation
frame rotating rigidly at constant rateΩc\Omega_{c},𝐯0\mathbf{v}_{0}is
the difference between the full rotation and the frame rotation:𝐯0=(𝛀−𝛀c)×𝐱,where𝛀=Ω​(r,θ)​𝐞z,and𝛀c=Ωc​𝐞z.\mathbf{v}_{0}=(\boldsymbol{\Omega}-\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c})\times\mathbf{x},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 30.00005pt\boldsymbol{\Omega}=\Omega(r,\theta)\,\mathbf{e}_{z}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}=\Omega_{c}\mathbf{e}_{z}\,.(3.2)

In our investigation, we further distinguish cases depending on the constancy ofΩ\Omega:
- –

Differential rotation: WhenΩ\Omegais non-constant,
we refer to this case as the differential rotation.
- –

Uniform rotation: WhenΩ\Omegais constant and nonzero,Ω=Ωu\Omega=\Omega_{u}, withΩu≠0\Omega_{u}\neq 0, the fluid body is in uniform rotation.
In this case, the observation frame is usually chosen
to be co-rotating at the same rate, i.e.Ωc:=Ωu\Omega_{c}:=\Omega_{u},
in which choice, there is no background flow:Ω=Ωu​constant​and​Ωc:=Ωu⇒𝐯0=0.\Omega=\Omega_{u}\text{ constant }\hskip 5.69046pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69046pt\Omega_{c}:=\Omega_{u}\hskip 8.5359pt\Rightarrow\hskip 8.5359pt\mathbf{v}_{0}=0\,.(3.3)
- –

Non-rotating Sun in a rigidly co-rotating frame: In this case,Ω=0\Omega=0butΩc\Omega_{c}is not. It gives𝐯0=−𝛀c×𝐱\mathbf{v}_{0}=-\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{x}. This is used
in the experiments to validate the correct behaviour of our solver inSubsection7.3.

We next note the independence of the background constraintsEq.2.44bon the choice of the observation frame.

## Identity 2.

For the background flow𝐯0\mathbf{v}_{0}given inEq.3.2, we have the identity𝐯0=Ω~​𝐞z×𝐱​=Eq.A.6​η​Ω~​𝐞ϕ,whereΩ~:=Ω−Ωc;\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{0}\,=\,\tilde{\Omega}\,\mathbf{e}_{z}\times\mathbf{x}\,\,\overset{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{v0phi0_app}}{=}\,\,\eta\,\tilde{\Omega}\,\mathbf{e}_{\phi},\qquad\text{where }\quad\tilde{\Omega}:=\Omega-\Omega_{c};(3.4)and​𝐯0⋅∇𝐯0+2​𝛀c×𝐯0+𝛀c×𝛀c×𝐱=𝛀×𝛀×𝐱=−Ω2​η​𝐞η.\displaystyle\text{and}\hskip 8.5359pt\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0}\,+\,2\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{v}_{0}\,+\,\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{x}\,=\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}\times\mathbf{x}\,=-\Omega^{2}\,\eta\,\mathbf{e}_{\eta}\,.(3.5)

## Proof.

WithΩ~:=Ω−Ωc\tilde{\Omega}:=\Omega-\Omega_{c}, we have𝐯0⋅∇𝐯0​=Eq.A.19−η​Ω~2​𝐞η;2​𝛀c×𝐯0​=Eq.A.10−2​Ωc​Ω~​η​𝐞η;𝛀c×𝛀c×𝐱​=Eq.A.13−η​Ωc2​𝐞η.\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0}\overset{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{matder_cyl}}{=}-\eta\tilde{\Omega}^{2}\mathbf{e}_{\eta};\qquad 2\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{v}_{0}\overset{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{rotmatrix}}{=}-2\,\Omega_{c}\,\tilde{\Omega}\,\eta\,\mathbf{e}_{\eta};\qquad\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{x}\overset{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{phifullcyl}}{=}-\eta\Omega_{c}^{2}\mathbf{e}_{\eta}.(3.6)

Summing up these three equalities, we obtain,𝐯0⋅∇𝐯0+2​𝛀c×𝐯0+𝛀c×𝛀c×𝐱=−(Ω~+Ωc)2​η​𝐞η=−Ω2​η​𝐞η.\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v}_{0}\,+\,2\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{v}_{0}\,+\,\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times\mathbf{x}\,\,=\,\,-(\tilde{\Omega}+\Omega_{c})^{2}\eta\mathbf{e}_{\eta}=-\Omega^{2}\,\eta\,\mathbf{e}_{\eta}.(3.7)

∎

## Background constraints for differential rotation

As a result ofIdentity2,
for a differential flow background givenDefinition2,
we have another equivalent definition of𝐘Ω\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega},
explicitly independent ofΩc\Omega_{c}:𝐘Ω:=∇ϕ0+𝛀×𝛀×𝐱​=Eq.3.5​∇ϕ0−Ω2​η​𝐞η.\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}:=\nabla\phi_{0}\,+\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}\times\mathbf{x}\,\overset{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{phifullexp}}{=}\,\nabla\phi_{0}\,-\,\Omega^{2}\,\eta\,\mathbf{e}_{\eta}\,.(3.8)

The background constraintEq.2.48(orEq.2.44b)
can be written independently ofΩc\Omega_{c}:(𝐘Ω:=)∇ϕ0+𝛀×𝛀×𝐱=−∇p0ρ0+𝐟ghostρ0.\left(\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}\hskip 2.84544pt:=\right)\hskip 8.5359pt\nabla\phi_{0}\,+\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}\times\mathbf{x}\,=\,-\dfrac{\nabla p_{0}}{\rho_{0}}\,+\,\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{ghost}}}}{\rho_{0}}.(3.9)

The quantity𝐟ghost\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{ghost}}measures
the deviation from standard background states
which have𝐟ghost=0\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{ghost}}=0, with the
two following situations for the cases with and without
rotation.
- 1.

Rotational hydrostatics: For uniform rotation
in co-rotating frame, the constraintEq.3.9with𝐟ghost=0\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{ghost}}=0is referred to
asrotational hydrostatics:(𝐘Ω:=)∇ϕ0+𝛀×𝛀×𝐱=−∇p0ρ0,Rotational hydrostatics.\left(\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}\hskip 2.84544pt:=\right)\nabla\phi_{0}\,+\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}\times\mathbf{x}\,=\,-\dfrac{\nabla p_{0}}{\rho_{0}}\,,\qquad\text{Rotational hydrostatics.}(3.10)

It can also be written in terms of the centrifugal
potentialψs\psi^{s}defined inEq.2.9,
replacing the left-hand side by∇(ϕ0+ψS)\nabla(\phi_{0}+\psi^{S}).
- 2.

Hydrostatic equilibrium:
For non-rotating backgrounds with𝐯0=0\mathbf{v}_{0}=0,Ω=0\Omega=0and𝛀c=0\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}=0,
the relationEq.3.10with𝐟ghost=0\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{ghost}}=0is referred to ashydrostatic equilibrium. It writes as,∇p0=−ρ0​∇ϕ0⇔𝜶p0Γ1=∇ϕ0c02,Hydrostatic equilibrium.\nabla p_{0}\,=\,-\rho_{0}\nabla\phi_{0}\hskip 7.11317pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 7.11317pt\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p_{0}}}{\Gamma_{1}}\,=\,\dfrac{\nabla\phi_{0}}{c_{0}^{2}}\,,\hskip 28.45274pt\text{Hydrostatic equilibrium}\,.(3.11)

The definition of𝐘Ω\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}Eq.2.47reduces to,𝐘0:=𝐘𝛀=0=∇ϕ0.\mathbf{Y}_{0}:=\mathbf{Y}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}=0}\,=\,\nabla\phi_{0}\,.(3.12)

## Remark 7.

For numerical resolution of the equation of motionsEq.2.65,
one needs the values of the parameters𝛀,𝛀c,ρ0,ϕ0,c0,p0,Γ1,and​𝐘Ωsatisfying{adiabaticity constraintEq.2.36,background constraintEq.3.9,ϕ0​determined by​ρ0​viaEq.2.45,𝐘Ω​defined by​ϕ0​and​𝛀​byEq.3.8.\begin{array}[]{c}\boldsymbol{\Omega},\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c},\,\rho_{0},\,\phi_{0},\\
c_{0},\,p_{0},\,\Gamma_{1},\text{ and }\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}\end{array}\qquad\text{ satisfying }\qquad\begin{dcases}\quad\text{ adiabaticity constraint \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{adiaba}},\\
\quad\text{ background constraint }\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{backgroundcon_Y0difrot},\\
\quad\,\phi_{0}\text{ determined by }\rho_{0}\text{ via }\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{poisson_eqn_phi},\\
\quad\,\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}\text{ defined by }\phi_{0}\text{ and }\boldsymbol{\Omega}\text{ by }\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{phifull::deffullrot}.\end{dcases}(3.13)

The constraintEq.3.9is employed
either to obtain the value of∇p0\nabla p_{0}, or to
compensate for the deviation from using a standard
backgroundEq.3.10.
Specifically, from inputs𝛀\boldsymbol{\Omega},𝛀c\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c},ρ0\rho_{0}(which determinesϕ0\phi_{0}),
taking𝐟ghost=0\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{ghost}}=0means that the parameters must be constructed
to satisfy the rotational hydrostaticsEq.3.10.
This approach is taken, e.g., in[53, Equations (1–3)]with a
polytropic relation forρ0\rho_{0}andp0p_{0}in order to construct an axially
symmetrical background with rotation.

One way to obtain a complete set of parameters inEq.3.13,
for purpose of numerical resolution, is to employ
an existing background model forp0p_{0}andc0c_{0}. In this approach,
including rotation can result in a deviation from rotational hydrostatics given
by𝐟ghost\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{ghost}}.
For instance, in our experiments, we employp0p_{0}andc0c_{0}from the standard solar
Model-S,[12]that gives quantities depending only onrrand
verifying the hystrostatic equilibriumEq.3.11and adiabaticityEq.2.36.
Incorporating rotation results in𝐟ghost=ρ0​𝛀×𝛀×𝐱≠0\mathbf{f}_{\mathrm{ghost}}=\rho_{0}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}\times\boldsymbol{\Omega}\times\mathbf{x}\neq 0,
although this quantity is not directly implemented inEq.2.65.
In the solar case, the deviation is the greatest near the center and is at most 3%.

As an example, for the case of
uniform rotation in co-rotating frameEq.3.3,Ω=Ωu=Ωc=\Omega=\Omega_{u}=\Omega_{c}=constant and𝐯0=0\mathbf{v}_{0}=0,
the important terms inEq.2.65take the following values,UnderEq.3.3:​{ϕ0′​(r)c02​(r)=αp​(r)Γ1​(r),𝐘Ω:=c02​(r)​(−Ωu2​ηc02​(r)​𝐞η+ϕ0′​(r)c02​(r)​𝐞r),𝐘Ω⊗(𝜶ρ−𝜶pΓ1)=N2​(r)​𝐞r⊗𝐞r−Ωu2​η​(αρ​(r)−ϕ0′​(r)c02​(r))​𝐞η⊗𝐞r.\text{Under \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{unifrot_cotrot::def}:}\begin{dcases}&\dfrac{\phi_{0}^{\prime}(r)}{c_{0}^{2}(r)}=\dfrac{\alpha_{p}(r)}{\Gamma_{1}(r)},\hskip 19.91684pt\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}:=c_{0}^{2}(r)\left(-\Omega_{u}^{2}\dfrac{\eta}{c_{0}^{2}(r)}\mathbf{e}_{\eta}\,+\,\dfrac{\phi_{0}^{\prime}(r)}{c_{0}^{2}(r)}\mathbf{e}_{r}\right),\\
&\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega}\otimes(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\rho}-\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}}{\Gamma_{1}})=N^{2}(r)\mathbf{e}_{r}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{r}-\Omega_{u}^{2}\,\eta\left(\alpha_{\rho}(r)-\dfrac{\phi_{0}^{\prime}(r)}{c_{0}^{2}(r)}\right)\mathbf{e}_{\eta}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{r}.\end{dcases}(3.14)

Here the buoyancy frequencyNNis defined inEq.3.27,
withc0c_{0},ρ0\rho_{0},ϕ0\phi_{0}given by e.g. Model-S.

## 3.2Liouville variants of equations of motion

We next consider a change of variable defined by a
factor𝔣≠0\mathfrak{f}\neq 0, that commutes with
operator𝑨\boldsymbol{A}ofEq.2.68.
This property is satisfied by
the solar backgrounds described inSection3and for factor𝔣\mathfrak{f}independent ofϕ\phi.
We will need the following properties satisfied by the inverse scale height𝜶f\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{f}inEq.2.4: for constantγ∈ℝ\gamma\in\mathbb{R}, andff,g≠0g\neq 0:𝜶gγ=γ​𝜶g,𝜶f​g=𝜶f+𝜶g.\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{g^{\gamma}}=\gamma\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{g},\quad\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{fg}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{f}+\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{g}.(3.15)

In particular, we have𝜶ρ=𝜶ρ/2,𝜶ρ​c=𝜶ρ/2+𝜶c.\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\sqrt{\rho}}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\rho}/2,\quad\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\sqrt{\rho}c}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\rho}/2+\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{c}.(3.16)

## Identity 3.

For𝔣≠0\mathfrak{f}\neq 0,
that commutes with operator𝐀\boldsymbol{A}, i.e.,𝐀​𝔣=𝔣​𝐀\boldsymbol{A}\,\mathfrak{f}\,=\,\mathfrak{f}\boldsymbol{A}.
For(𝐮,w)(\boldsymbol{u},w), and(𝐮𝔣,w𝔣)(\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathfrak{f}},w_{\mathfrak{f}})related by𝐮𝔣:=𝔣​𝐮,w𝔣:=𝔣−1​w,\mathbf{u}_{\mathfrak{f}}:=\mathfrak{f}\,\mathbf{u},\hskip 40.00006ptw_{\mathfrak{f}}:=\mathfrak{f}^{-1}\,w\,,(3.17)

we have the equivalence,{𝑨​𝐮+𝜷1​w+∇w=𝐠,∇⋅𝐮+𝜷2⋅𝐮+ϱ​w=h,⟺Eq.3.17{𝑨𝔣2​𝐮𝔣+(𝜷1−𝜶𝔣)​w𝔣+∇w𝔣=𝐠𝔣,∇⋅𝐮𝔣+(𝜷2+𝜶𝔣)⋅𝐮𝔣+ϱ​𝔣2​w𝔣=𝔣​h.\left\{\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{A}\mathbf{u}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\,w+\nabla w=\mathbf{g},\\[1.99997pt]
\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{u}+\varrho\,w=h,\end{aligned}\right.\quad\overset{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{gencov}}{\Longleftrightarrow}\quad\left\{\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\boldsymbol{A}}{\mathfrak{f}^{2}}\,\mathbf{u}_{\mathfrak{f}}\,+\,\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{f}}\right)\,w_{\mathfrak{f}}\,+\,\nabla w_{\mathfrak{f}}\,=\,\dfrac{\mathbf{g}}{\mathfrak{f}},\\
\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}_{\mathfrak{f}}\,+\,\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\,+\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{f}}\right)\cdot\mathbf{u}_{\mathfrak{f}}\,+\,\varrho\,\mathfrak{f}^{2}\,w_{\mathfrak{f}}\,=\,\mathfrak{f}\,h.\end{aligned}\right.(3.18)

## Proof.

Employing∇w\displaystyle\nabla w=∇(𝔣​w𝔣)=𝔣​(∇w𝔣−w𝔣​𝜶𝔣),\displaystyle=\nabla(\mathfrak{f}w_{\mathfrak{f}})=\mathfrak{f}\left(\nabla w_{\mathfrak{f}}-w_{\mathfrak{f}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{f}}\right),∇⋅𝐮\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}=∇⋅(𝔣−1​𝐮𝔣)=𝔣−1​(∇⋅𝐮𝔣−𝐮𝔣⋅𝜶1/𝔣)=𝔣−1​(∇⋅𝐮𝔣+𝐮𝔣⋅𝜶𝔣),\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot(\mathfrak{f}^{-1}\mathbf{u}_{\mathfrak{f}})=\mathfrak{f}^{-1}\left(\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}_{\mathfrak{f}}-\mathbf{u}_{\mathfrak{f}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1/\mathfrak{f}}\right)=\mathfrak{f}^{-1}\left(\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}_{\mathfrak{f}}+\mathbf{u}_{\mathfrak{f}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{f}}\right)\,,

we can rewrite the left-hand sides of the system in variable(w,𝐮)(w,\mathbf{u})as𝑨​𝐮+𝜷1​w+∇w\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}\,\mathbf{u}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\,w+\nabla w=𝑨​(𝔣−1​𝐮𝔣)+𝜷1​(𝔣​w𝔣)+∇(𝔣​w𝔣)=𝔣​(𝑨𝔣2​𝝃𝔣+(𝜷1−𝜶𝔣)​w𝔣+∇w𝔣),\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{A}(\mathfrak{f}^{-1}\mathbf{u}_{\mathfrak{f}})+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\,(\mathfrak{f}w_{\mathfrak{f}})+\nabla(\mathfrak{f}w_{\mathfrak{f}})=\mathfrak{f}\left(\dfrac{\boldsymbol{A}}{\mathfrak{f}^{2}}\,\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathfrak{f}}+\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{f}}\right)\,w_{\mathfrak{f}}+\nabla w_{\mathfrak{f}}\right),(3.19)∇⋅𝐮+𝜷2⋅𝐮+ϱ​w\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{u}+\varrho w=∇⋅(𝔣−1​𝐮𝔣)+𝜷2⋅(𝔣−1​𝐮𝔣)+ϱ​𝔣​w𝔣=𝔣−1​(∇⋅𝐮𝔣+(𝜷2+𝜶𝔣)⋅𝐮𝔣+ϱ​𝔣2​w𝔣).\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot(\mathfrak{f}^{-1}\mathbf{u}_{\mathfrak{f}})+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\cdot(\mathfrak{f}^{-1}\mathbf{u}_{\mathfrak{f}})+\varrho\mathfrak{f}w_{\mathfrak{f}}=\mathfrak{f}^{-1}\left(\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}_{\mathfrak{f}}+\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}+\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{f}}\right)\cdot\mathbf{u}_{\mathfrak{f}}+\varrho\mathfrak{f}^{2}\,w_{\mathfrak{f}}\right).

The equivalent system in variable(𝐮𝔣,w𝔣)(\mathbf{u}_{\mathfrak{f}},w_{\mathfrak{f}})is obtained by multiplying the first equation by𝔣−1\mathfrak{f}^{-1}and second by𝔣\mathfrak{f}.
∎

## Liouville and Liouville-c change of variables

We employIdentity3with factors𝔣L=ρ0\mathfrak{f}_{\mathrm{L}}=\sqrt{\rho_{0}}and𝔣Lc=c0​ρ0\mathfrak{f}_{\mathrm{Lc}}=c_{0}\sqrt{\rho_{0}}inEq.3.17, to obtain variants,
respectively called the Liouville and Liouville-c variants,
equivalent to the original equationEq.2.68.
Specifically, we introduce the Liouville variables(𝝃L,wL)(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{L},w_{L}), and the
Liouville-c variables(𝝃c,wc)(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{c},w_{c}),Liouville unknowns:\displaystyle\text{Liouville unknowns}:\qquad𝝃L=ρ0​𝝃,wL=1ρ0​δpE;\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}_{L}\,=\,\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\boldsymbol{\xi}\,,\qquad w_{L}\,=\,\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\rho_{0}}}\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{E}};(3.20a)Liouville-c unknowns:\displaystyle\text{Liouville-c unknowns}:\qquad𝝃c=c0​ρ0​𝝃,wc=1c0​ρ0​δpE.\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}_{c}\,=\,c_{0}\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\boldsymbol{\xi}\,,\qquad w_{c}\,=\,\dfrac{1}{c_{0}\sqrt{\rho_{0}}}\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{E}}.(3.20b)

The operators of LiouvilleℒLiouville\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville}}and Liouville-c variantsℒLiouville−c\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville-c}}, are defined respectively asℒLiouville:=(𝐀LΩ𝜷a​LΩ+∇∇⋅+𝜷b​LΩ⋅ϱLΩ)andℒLiouville−c:=(𝐀LcΩ𝜷a​LcΩ+∇∇⋅+𝜷b​LcΩ⋅ϱLcΩ),\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville}}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{L}}^{\Omega}\quad&\quad\boldsymbol{\beta}_{a\mathrm{L}}^{\Omega}\,+\,\nabla\\[1.99997pt]
\nabla\cdot+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{b\mathrm{L}}^{\Omega}\cdot\quad&\quad\varrho_{\mathrm{L}}^{\Omega}\end{pmatrix}\hskip 15.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 15.00002pt\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville-c}}\,:=\,\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{Lc}}^{\Omega}\quad&\quad\boldsymbol{\beta}_{a\mathrm{Lc}}^{\Omega}\,+\,\nabla\\[1.99997pt]
\nabla\cdot+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{b\mathrm{Lc}}^{\Omega}\cdot\quad&\quad\varrho_{\mathrm{Lc}}^{\Omega}\end{pmatrix}\,,(3.21)

with their coefficients related to those ofℒodiv\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{odiv}}Eq.2.68as follows,𝑨LΩ=𝑨0Ω,𝜷a​LΩ=𝜷a​0Ω−𝜶ρ02,𝜷b​LΩ=𝜷b​0Ω+𝜶ρ02,ϱLΩ=ϱ0Ω​ρ0=1c02,𝑨LcΩ=𝑨0Ωc02,𝜷a​LcΩ=𝜷a​0Ω−𝜶c0−𝜶ρ02,𝜷b​LcΩ=𝜷b​0Ω+𝜶c0+𝜶ρ02,ϱLcΩ=ϱ0Ω​c02​ρ0=1.\begin{array}[]{l l l l }\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathrm{L}}^{\Omega}=\boldsymbol{A}_{0}^{\Omega},&\boldsymbol{\beta}_{a\mathrm{L}}^{\Omega}=\boldsymbol{\beta}_{a0}^{\Omega}-\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\rho_{0}}}{2},&\boldsymbol{\beta}_{b\mathrm{L}}^{\Omega}=\boldsymbol{\beta}_{b0}^{\Omega}+\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\rho_{0}}}{2},\quad&\varrho_{\mathrm{L}}^{\Omega}=\varrho^{\Omega}_{0}\rho_{0}=\dfrac{1}{c_{0}^{2}},\\
\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathrm{Lc}}^{\Omega}=\dfrac{\boldsymbol{A}_{0}^{\Omega}}{c_{0}^{2}},\quad&\boldsymbol{\beta}_{a\mathrm{Lc}}^{\Omega}=\boldsymbol{\beta}_{a0}^{\Omega}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{c_{0}}-\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\rho_{0}}}{2},\quad&\boldsymbol{\beta}_{b\mathrm{Lc}}^{\Omega}=\boldsymbol{\beta}_{b0}^{\Omega}+\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{c_{0}}+\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\rho_{0}}}{2},&\varrho_{\mathrm{Lc}}^{\Omega}=\varrho_{0}^{\Omega}c_{0}^{2}\rho_{0}=1.\end{array}(3.22)

\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{E}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{g}\\[1.99997pt]
h\end{pmatrix}\hskip 14.22636pt\Longleftrightarrow\hskip 14.22636pt\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville}}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{L}\\
w_{L}\end{pmatrix}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\rho_{0}}}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{g}\\[1.99997pt]
\rho_{0}\,h\end{pmatrix}(3.23)⟺ℒLiouville−c​(𝝃cwc)=1c0​ρ0​(𝐠c02​ρ0​h).\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\Longleftrightarrow\hskip 14.22636pt\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville-c}}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{c}\\

## Remark 8(Background models used depending on the formulation).

The motivation of the Liouville variantsℒLiouville\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville}}andℒLiouville−c\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville-c}}is that the background model densityρ0\rho_{0}does not appear explicitly
in the system of equations anymore, hence avoiding to numerically handle
the exponential decay in the atmosphere, cf.,[3,49,4].
Instead, the Liouville variants rely on the inverse density scale heightαρ\alpha_{\rho}which is a constant whenρ0\rho_{0}is exponentially decreasing.
Comparing the Liouville variants, we note thatℒLiouville−c\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville-c}}needs the inverse
velocity scale heightαc\alpha_{c}, hence the derivatives ofc0c_{0}, which might
complicate the consideration of non-radial velocity perturbations.
See furtherSubsection6.2for
numerical comparisons.

## Unification of variants

The three systems introduced above,ℒodiv\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{odiv}},ℒLiouville\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville}}andℒLiouville−c\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville-c}},
coupled with vanishingδpL\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{L}}boundary conditionEq.2.63are unified
in the following notation with unknowns(𝒖,w)(\boldsymbol{u},w),𝑨​𝒖+𝜷1​w+∇w=𝐠,in​𝔹⊙,\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}\,\boldsymbol{u}\,+\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\,w\,+\,\nabla w\,=\,\mathbf{g},\hskip 37.50005pt\text{ in }\mathbb{B}_{\odot},(3.24a)∇⋅𝒖+𝜷2⋅𝒖+ϱ​w=h,in​𝔹⊙,\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\,\boldsymbol{u}\,+\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\,\cdot\,\boldsymbol{u}\,+\,\varrho\,w\,=\,h,\hskip 25.00003pt\text{ in }\mathbb{B}_{\odot},(3.24b)w+𝜶bc⋅𝒖=0,on​∂𝔹⊙.\displaystyle w+\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{bc}}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}=0,\hskip 77.5001pt\text{ on }\partial\mathbb{B}_{\odot}.(3.24c)

We denote the operator in systemEq.3.24asℒ∙:=(𝑨𝜷1+∇∇⋅+𝜷2⋅ϱ).\mathcal{L}_{\bullet}:=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{A}\quad&\quad\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}+\nabla\\
\nabla\cdot+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\cdot\quad&\varrho\end{pmatrix}\,.(3.25)

The explicit definitions of the coefficient inEq.3.24vary depending on the variants,Eq.3.22.
The coefficient𝜶bc\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{bc}}of the
boundary conditionEq.3.24ctakes the following
values,ℒodivℒLiouvilleℒLiouville−c𝜶bc0=∇p0𝜶bcL=∇p0/ρ0𝜶bcLc=∇p0/(c02​ρ0)\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{odiv}}&\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville}}&\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville-c}}\\[1.99997pt]
\hline\cr&&\\[-7.5pt]
\makebox[70.0001pt][c]{$\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{bc}}^{0}\,=\,\nabla p_{0}$}&\makebox[90.00014pt][c]{$\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{bc}}^{\mathrm{L}}\,=\,\nabla p_{0}/\rho_{0}$}&\makebox[100.00015pt][c]{$\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{bc}}^{\mathrm{Lc}}\,=\,\nabla p_{0}/(c_{0}^{2}\,\rho_{0})$}\end{array}(3.26)

## 3.3Explicit expressions for non-rotating backgrounds in inertial frames

A model commonly employed for non-rotating backgrounds
in the standard solar
Model-S,[12]which is radially symmetric
and satisfies the
hydrostatic equilibriumEq.3.11.
When the background parameters only depend on the radial distancerr,
it is useful to work with the buoyancy frequencyN​(r)N(r),
with its squared defined as,[14,49,4],N2:=ϕ0′​(αρ0−αp0Γ1)Buoyancy frequency squared.N^{2}:=\phi_{0}^{\prime}\left(\alpha_{\rho_{0}}-\dfrac{\alpha_{p_{0}}}{\Gamma_{1}}\right)\,\qquad\text{Buoyancy frequency squared}.(3.27)

Hereα\alphais the radial inverse scale heightEq.2.5.

In non-rotating background in inertial frame,Ω=Ωc=0\Omega=\Omega_{c}=0,𝐯0=0\mathbf{v}_{0}=0, and we have,𝐘Ω=0:=∇ϕ0,𝑨0Ω=0=ρ0​[−ω2−2​i​ω​γatt+∇ϕ0⊗(𝜶ρ−𝜶pΓ1)].\mathbf{Y}_{\Omega=0}:=\nabla\phi_{0},\hskip 14.22636pt\boldsymbol{A}_{0}^{\Omega=0}=\rho_{0}\bigg[-\omega^{2}\,-\,2\mathrm{i}\omega\gamma_{\mathrm{att}}\,+\,\nabla\phi_{0}\otimes\Big(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\rho}\,-\,\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}}{\Gamma_{1}}\Big)\bigg].(3.28)

If we assume radial symmetry, it gives:Radial symmetry:{𝑨0Ω=0=𝑨00=ρ0​(−ω2−2​i​ω​γatt+N2​𝐞r⊗𝐞r),For the three variants:𝑨∙0=(𝑨∙0)t,𝜷1⁣∙0∥𝜷2⁣∙0∥𝐞r.\text{Radial symmetry}:\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\boldsymbol{A}_{0}^{\Omega=0}=\boldsymbol{A}_{0}^{0}=\rho_{0}\left(-\omega^{2}-2\mathrm{i}\omega\gamma_{\mathrm{att}}+N^{2}\,\mathbf{e}_{r}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{r}\right),\\[3.00003pt]
\text{For the three variants}:\quad\boldsymbol{A}_{\bullet}^{0}=(\boldsymbol{A}_{\bullet}^{0})^{t},\quad\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}_{1\bullet}\parallel\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}_{2\bullet}\parallel\mathbf{e}_{r}\end{array}\right.\,.(3.29)

Under hydrostatic equilibriumEq.3.11, and sufficient
regularity999The form of𝑨00\boldsymbol{A}_{0}^{0}inEq.3.34employs the gradient version ofEq.3.11.
