After some rearrangement, we thus obtain,Eq.3.11+regularity in​p0,ϕ0⇒Hϕ0+Hp0ρ0=c02​𝜶p0Γ1⊗𝜶ρ0=c02​𝜶ρ0⊙𝜶p0Γ1.\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{steq}+\text{ regularity in }p_{0},\phi_{0}\hskip 14.22636pt\Rightarrow\hskip 14.22636ptH_{\phi_{0}}+\dfrac{H_{p_{0}}}{\uprho_{0}}=c_{0}^{2}\,\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p_{0}}}{\Gamma_{1}}\otimes\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\rho_{0}}=c_{0}^{2}\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\rho_{0}}\odot\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p_{0}}}{\Gamma_{1}}.(3.32)Here⊙\odotdenotes the symmetric tensor product𝐮⊙𝐯=(𝐮⊗𝐯+𝐯⊗𝐮)/2\mathbf{u}\odot\mathbf{v}=(\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{v}\otimes\mathbf{u})/2. ## Remark 9(Mixed formulation obtained from Galbrun’s equation).

InRemark6, we have shown the relation between our working equation
and the equation by Lynden-Bell & Ostriker[42], referred
to as the LBO variant, cf.Eq.2.77.
Here we show that our mixed systemEq.2.65associated withEq.2.62a,
coincides with the mixed system directly derived from LBO variantEq.2.77.
Without rotation and flow, the LBO variantEq.2.77simplifies to,𝕷​𝝃=𝐠in​ℝ3,with𝕷​𝝃=−ρ0​(ω2+2​i​ω​γatt)​𝝃−∇[Γ1​p0​∇⋅𝝃]+(∇p0)​(∇⋅𝝃)\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{L}}\,\boldsymbol{\xi}=\mathbf{g}\,\quad\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{3}\,\,,\text{with}\quad\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{L}}\,\boldsymbol{\xi}\,=\,-\rho_{0}\big(\omega^{2}+2\,\mathrm{i}\omega\,\gamma_{\mathrm{att}}\big)\,\boldsymbol{\xi}\,-\,\nabla\big[\Gamma_{1}\,p_{0}\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}\big]+(\nabla\,p_{0})(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi})(3.35)−∇(𝝃⋅∇p0)+(𝝃⋅∇)​∇p0+ρ0​(𝝃⋅∇)​∇ϕ0.\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt-\nabla\,(\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla\,p_{0})\,+\,(\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla)\nabla\,p_{0}\,+\,\rho_{0}\,(\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\nabla)\nabla\,\phi_{0}\,.

This equation was employed in our previous works[4,49]and is also called Galbrun’s equation in aero-acoustics.
We next rewrite this as a mixed system,Eq.3.35⇔{ρ0​(𝖡−σ2)​𝝃+𝜶pΓ1​δpE+∇δpE=𝐠,δpEρ0​c02+𝝃⋅∇p0ρ0​c02+∇⋅𝝃=0,\displaystyle\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{main_eqn_Galbrun_unscaled}\hskip 14.22636pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{dcases}\rho_{0}\left(\mathsf{B}-\sigma^{2}\right)\boldsymbol{\xi}+\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}}{\Gamma_{1}}\,\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{E}}+\nabla\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{E}}=\mathbf{g},\\
\dfrac{\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{E}}}{\rho_{0}c_{0}^{2}}\,+\,\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{\dfrac{\nabla p_{0}}{\rho_{0}c_{0}^{2}}}}\,+\,\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}\,=\,0,\end{dcases}(3.36)where𝖡:=H𝗉0ρ0+Hϕ0−∇p0⊗∇p0Γ1​p0​ρ0=Hp0ρ0+Hϕ0−c02​𝜶pΓ1⊙𝜶pΓ1.\displaystyle\text{where}\hskip 14.22636pt\mathsf{B}\,:=\,\dfrac{\mathrm{H}_{\mathsf{p}_{0}}}{\rho_{0}}+\mathrm{H}_{\phi_{0}}\,-\,\dfrac{\nabla p_{0}\otimes\nabla p_{0}}{\Gamma_{1}p_{0}\rho_{0}}=\dfrac{\mathrm{H}_{p_{0}}}{\rho_{0}}+\mathrm{H}_{\phi_{0}}\,-\,c_{0}^{2}\,\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}}{\Gamma_{1}}\odot\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}}{\Gamma_{1}}\,.(3.37)

Employing identityEq.3.32, which replacesHϕ0+Hp0ρ0H_{\phi_{0}}+\tfrac{H_{p_{0}}}{\rho_{0}}byc02​𝛂ρ⊙𝛂pΓ1c_{0}^{2}\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\rho}\odot\tfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}}{\Gamma_{1}}, we can rewrite𝖡\mathsf{B}as,𝖡​=Eq.3.32​c02​𝜶ρ⊙𝜶pΓ1−c02​𝜶pΓ1⊙𝜶pΓ1=c02​𝜶pΓ1⊙(𝜶ρ−𝜶pΓ1)​=Eq.3.11​∇ϕ0⊙(𝜶ρ−𝜶pΓ1).\mathsf{B}\overset{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{steq:grad}}{=}c_{0}^{2}\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\rho}\odot\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}}{\Gamma_{1}}\,-\,c_{0}^{2}\,\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}}{\Gamma_{1}}\odot\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}}{\Gamma_{1}}=c_{0}^{2}\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}}{\Gamma_{1}}\odot\left(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\rho}-\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}}{\Gamma_{1}}\right)\overset{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{steq}}{=}\nabla\phi_{0}\odot\left(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\rho}-\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}}{\Gamma_{1}}\right).(3.38)

This leads to the coefficients in this work, cf.Eq.3.34.

## 3.4Relation to scalar PDEs

The relation of the mixed systemEq.3.24with a scalar PDE is established below.
We focus on the backgrounds described inSection3,
in which operator𝑨\boldsymbol{A}is either a multiplication
operator without rotation, or can be reduced
to one with differential rotation.
The details for the latter case are
further developed inSubsection4.2.
The equivalence with a scalar PDE is obtained readily
when there is only a scalar source, cf.Identity5.
Here we limit our discussion to a one-way relation for vector source.

## With scalar source

We considerEq.3.24when
there is only a scalar source input, i.e.𝐠=0\mathbf{g}=0.
We introduce a scalar differential operator of anisotropic
convected Helmholtz type,LΩ​w:=−∇⋅𝑨−1​∇w−(𝑨−t​𝜷2+𝑨−1​𝜷1)⋅∇w+w​[ϱ−𝜷2⋅𝑨−1⋅𝜷1−∇⋅(𝑨−1​𝜷1)].L^{\Omega}w:=-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\nabla w-(\boldsymbol{A}^{-t}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}+\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1})\cdot\nabla w+w\left[\varrho-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}-\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1})\right]\,.(3.39)

In the caseΩ=Ωc=0\Omega=\Omega_{c}=0, the above operator simplifies to,L​w:=−∇⋅𝑨−1​∇w+(ϱ+𝜸⋅𝑨−1⋅𝜸+∇⋅(𝑨−1​𝜸))​w;𝜸:=𝜷20=−𝜷10.Lw:=-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\nabla w+\left(\varrho+\boldsymbol{\gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\gamma})\right)w;\quad\boldsymbol{\gamma}:=\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}^{0}=-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}^{0}.(3.40)

## Identity 4.

For generic invertible matrix-valued𝐀\boldsymbol{A}, vector-valued𝛃\boldsymbol{\beta}and scalar-valuedϱ\varrhofunctions, we have the following equivalence,𝑨​𝐮+𝜷1​w+∇w\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}\mathbf{u}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}w+\nabla w=0,\displaystyle=0\,,(3.41a)∇⋅𝐮+𝜷2⋅𝐮+ϱ​w\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{u}+\varrho w=h.\displaystyle=h\,.(3.41b)⟺LΩ​w=h.\Longleftrightarrow\hskip 17.07182ptL^{\Omega}w\,=\,h.(3.42)

## Proof.

We employEq.3.41aas the defining expression for𝐮=−A−1​(𝜷1​w+∇w)\mathbf{u}=-A^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}w+\nabla w).
Substitute this in the second equationEq.3.41b, employing∇⋅(A−1​𝜷1​w)=w​∇⋅(A−1​𝜷1)+(A−1​𝜷1)⋅∇w\nabla\cdot(A^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}w)=w\nabla\cdot(A^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1})+(A^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1})\cdot\nabla w, we get∇⋅𝐮\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}+𝜷2⋅𝐮+ϱ​w=−∇⋅[𝑨−1​(𝜷1​w+∇w)]−𝜷2⋅𝑨−1​(𝜷1​w+∇w)+ϱ​w\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{u}+\varrho\,w=-\nabla\cdot[\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}w+\nabla w)]-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}w+\nabla w)+\varrho\,w(3.43)=−∇⋅𝑨−1​∇w+w​[ϱ−𝜷2⋅𝑨−1⋅𝜷1−∇⋅(𝑨−1​𝜷1)]−(𝑨−t​𝜷2+𝑨−1​𝜷1)⋅∇w.\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\nabla w+w\left[\varrho-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}-\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1})\right]-(\boldsymbol{A}^{-t}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}+\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1})\cdot\nabla w\,.

∎

As a corollary, we obtain the following identity for the case without rotation.

## Identity 5.

Without rotation and flow, i.e.,Ω=Ωc=0\Omega=\Omega_{c}=0, we have for all three variants,𝑨0=(𝑨0)t,𝜷20=−𝜷10=:𝜸.\boldsymbol{A}^{0}=(\boldsymbol{A}^{0})^{t},\quad\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}^{0}=-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}^{0}=:\boldsymbol{\gamma}\,.(3.44)

The systemEq.3.24with𝐠=0\mathbf{g}=0is equivalent to an anisotropic Helmholtz equation,{𝑨0​𝐮−𝜸​w+∇w=0∇⋅𝐮+𝜸⋅𝐮+ϱ​w=h⟺L​w=h,with​L​defined inEq.3.40.\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\Longleftrightarrow\hskip 14.22636ptLw=h,\hskip 5.69046pt\text{ with }L\text{ defined in }\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{Lscalar_op::def}.(3.45)

## With vector source

Employing essentially the same proof, withLΩL^{\Omega}defined inEq.3.39, for a source𝐠\mathbf{g}with enough regularity, we can still arrive at a scalar equation (here without declaring an equivalence),ℒ(𝒖w)=(𝐠h)⟹ℒΩw=h−gscalar,wheregscalar:=(∇⋅+𝜷2⋅)𝐀−1𝐠.\mathcal{L}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{u}\\
w\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{g}\\
h\end{pmatrix}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathcal{L}^{\Omega}w=h-g_{\mathrm{scalar}},\quad\text{where }\hskip 5.69046ptg_{\mathrm{scalar}}:=(\nabla\cdot+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\cdot)\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{g}.(3.46)

## 3.5Elliptic and hyperbolic regions for radial backgrounds at zero attenuation

The equivalence inEq.3.45helps give insight in the nature of the vector operator.
Since the nature of the PDE is the same for all three variants, for convenience, we consider the Liouville variantℒLiouville\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville}}.
For a radially symmetric background, fromEq.3.29, we have𝑨L0=−σ2​𝕀​𝕕+N2​𝐞r⊗𝐞r,σ2:=ω2+2​i​ω​γatt\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}_{L}^{0}=-\sigma^{2}\mathbb{Id}+N^{2}\,\mathbf{e}_{r}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{r},\quad\sigma^{2}:=\omega^{2}+2\mathrm{i}\omega\gamma_{\mathrm{att}}⇒(𝑨L0)−1=1N2−σ2​𝐞r⊗𝐞r−1σ2​(𝐞θ⊗𝐞θ+𝐞ϕ⊗𝐞ϕ).\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad(\boldsymbol{A}_{L}^{0})^{-1}=\dfrac{1}{N^{2}-\sigma^{2}}\mathbf{e}_{r}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{r}-\dfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\left(\mathbf{e}_{\theta}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{\theta}+\mathbf{e}_{\phi}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{\phi}\right)\,.(3.47)

The highest-order term inEq.3.45takes the following form
without attenuation (γatt=0\gamma_{\mathrm{att}}=0):∇⋅(𝑨L0)−1​∇=1r2​∂rr2N2−ω2​∂r−1ω2​r2​Δ𝕊2.\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{A}_{L}^{0})^{-1}\nabla\,\,=\,\,\dfrac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}\dfrac{r^{2}}{N^{2}-\omega^{2}}\partial_{r}-\dfrac{1}{\omega^{2}r^{2}}\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\,.(3.48)

Then, the operatorLLEq.3.40is elliptic when the coefficient of∂r2\partial_{r}^{2}andΔ𝕊2\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}have the same sign, and is of hyperbolic type when
they are of opposite signs.
This observation leads to the following definitions,𝔹ell:={ω2>N2​(r)}(Elliptic region),\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}=\{\omega^{2}>N^{2}(r)\}\hskip 32.72049pt\text{(Elliptic region)},(3.49)𝔹hyp:={ω2<N2​(r)}(Hyperbolic region).\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}=\{\omega^{2}<N^{2}(r)\}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{(Hyperbolic region)}.

## Remark 10.

Throughout the work, we work with nonzero-attenuation, which guarantees
the ellipticity of the problem. However, at small levels of attenuation, at frequencyω\omegaand position
of Dirac source𝐬\mathbf{s}such that𝐬∈𝔹hyp\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}, we will see
that the solutions manifest behaviors reminiscent of those of wave operators,
in particular propagation of singularities along line-cone structures,
cf.Subsection6.3.
These notionsEq.3.49will also be employed to provide
interpretations for the choice of the HDG stabilization
inSubsection5.5.

## 4Cylindrical decomposition for axially symmetric backgrounds

In this section, we exploit the axial symmetry
of the background parameters to derive the system in so-called 2.5D.
We recall the generic system of equationsEq.3.24with unknowns(𝒖,w)(\boldsymbol{u},w)and backgrounds that verifyDefinition2,𝑨​𝒖+𝜷1​w+∇w=𝐠,\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}\,\boldsymbol{u}\,+\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\,w\,+\,\nabla w\,=\,\mathbf{g},(4.1a)∇⋅𝒖+𝜷2⋅𝒖+ϱ​w=h,\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\,\boldsymbol{u}\,+\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\,\cdot\,\boldsymbol{u}\,+\,\varrho\,w\,=\,h,(4.1b)

with𝐠\mathbf{g}andhhsupported away from the rotation axis𝐞z\mathbf{e}_{z}.
For simplification in terms of boundary conditionEq.2.63, we assume that,∇p0\nabla p_{0}is parallel to the (outward-pointing) normal vector of𝔹⊙\mathbb{B}_{\odot}denoted
by𝐧𝔹⊙\mathbf{n}_{\mathbb{B}_{\odot}}, i.e.∇p0∥𝐧𝔹⊙,on​∂𝔹⊙.\nabla p_{0}\parallel\mathbf{n}_{\mathbb{B}_{\odot}},\quad\text{on }\partial\mathbb{B}_{\odot}.(4.2)

We will decouple the above system which is axially symmetric
with respect to the z-axis𝐞z\mathbf{e}_{z}into modal problems
defined for each cylindrical/azimuthal modemm(or equivalently Fourier series inϕ\phi)
inSubsection4.1.
InSubsection4.2, the cylindrical expansion of the
scalar Green’s kernelGpG_{p}is introduced.

## 4.1Azimuthal modal problem

Due to the symmetry, we work in cylindrical coordinate system(η,ϕ,z)(\eta,\phi,z), in
which the coefficients of operator𝑨\boldsymbol{A},𝜷1\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},𝜷2\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}andϱ\varrhoare independent ofϕ\phi.
We list some general notations required to state the problem:
- –

We denote by the square bracket[⋅][\cdot]the components of a tensor in the basis(𝐞η,𝐞ϕ,𝐞z)(\mathbf{e}_{\eta},\mathbf{e}_{\phi},\mathbf{e}_{z}), specifically for a vector𝒗\boldsymbol{v},[𝒗]=(vη,vϕ,vz)t,for​𝒗=vη​𝐞η+vϕ​𝐞ϕ+vz​𝐞z.[\boldsymbol{v}]=(v_{\eta},v_{\phi},v_{z})^{t},\quad\text{ for }\boldsymbol{v}=v_{\eta}\mathbf{e}_{\eta}+v_{\phi}\mathbf{e}_{\phi}+v_{z}\mathbf{e}_{z}.(4.3)
- –

The modal problem is defined on the restriction of𝔹⊙\mathbb{B}_{\odot}to the meridional half-plane. For simplicity we called this geometry
the meridional half-diskDD, illustrated inFigure2.
- –

We denote the boundary of the meridional half-diskDDby∂D\partial D, and partition this boundary into the
exterior one, denoted by∂bD\partial^{b}D, and the portion
lying in the rotation axis𝐞z\mathbf{e}_{z},
denoted by∂aD\partial^{a}D, seeFigure2.
In terms of(η,ϕ,z)(\eta,\phi,z),DDand its boundary
are parametrized by(η,z)∈ℝ+×ℝ(\eta,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R},D={(η,z)∈ℝ+×ℝ|(η,z,ϕ)∈𝔹⊙}∂D=∂Da∪∂Db,\displaystyle D=\{(\eta,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}\,\big|\,(\eta,z,\phi)\in\mathbb{B}_{\odot}\}\hskip 28.45274pt\partial D=\partial D^{a}\cup\partial D^{b},(4.4)where​∂Da={(0,z)∈D},∂Db=∂D∖∂Da.\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\text{where}\,\,\partial D^{a}=\{(0,z)\in D\},\quad\partial D^{b}=\partial D\setminus\partial D^{a}\,.
- –

We denote the cylindrical components of the outward pointing normal vector ofDDin the meridional
plane by𝒏~\tilde{\boldsymbol{n}}, in relation to the normal vector𝐧𝔹⊙\mathbf{n}_{\mathbb{B}_{\odot}}of𝔹⊙\mathbb{B}_{\odot}in 3D, we have𝒏~=(nη,nz)t,𝒏:=[𝒏𝔹⊙]=(nη,0,nz)t.\tilde{\boldsymbol{n}}\,=\,(n_{\eta},n_{z})^{t},\quad\boldsymbol{n}:=[\boldsymbol{n}_{\mathbb{B}_{\odot}}]=(n_{\eta},0,n_{z})^{t}\,.(4.5)Figure 2:Illustration of the numerical domain for axisymmetry
which corresponds to the meridional half-disk.
The boundary is separated between∂Da\partial D^{a}on
axis𝐞z\mathbf{e}_{z}, and∂Db\partial D^{b}for the exterior.

We note that the discussion in this section works for
a general geometry of𝔹⊙\mathbb{B}_{\odot}that is
rotationally invariant, such as an oblate spheroid.

## Remark 11.

When𝔹⊙\mathbb{B}_{\odot}is a sphere,𝔹⊙={𝐱∈ℝ3||𝐱|≤rmax}\mathbb{B}_{\odot}=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\,|\,\lvert\mathbf{x}\rvert\leq r_{\max}\}, the domainDDand its boundary can be more explicitly described as,D={(η,z)∈ℝ+×ℝ|η2+z2≤rmax2},∂D=∂Da∪∂Db,\displaystyle D=\{(\eta,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}\,\big|\,\eta^{2}+z^{2}\leq r_{\max}^{2}\},\hskip 28.45274pt\partial D=\partial D^{a}\cup\partial D^{b},(4.6)where​∂Da={(0,z)||z|≤rmax},∂Db={(η,z)∈ℝ+×ℝ|η2+z2=rmax2}.\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\text{where}\,\,\partial D^{a}=\{(0,z)\,|\,\lvert z\rvert\leq r_{\max}\},\quad\partial D^{b}=\{(\eta,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}\,\big|\,\eta^{2}+z^{2}=r_{\max}^{2}\}\,.

## Cylindrical expansion of the unknowns and sources

For𝐱\mathbf{x}away from the rotation axis𝐞z\mathbf{e}_{z},
we write the scalar unknownwwand scalar sourcehhin cylindrical expansion, which is the Fourier series expansion inϕ\phi:w​(η,ϕ,z)=∑m∈ℤwm​(η,z)​ei​m​ϕ,h​(η,ϕ,z)=∑m∈ℤhm​(η,z)​ei​m​ϕ.w(\eta,\phi,z)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}w_{m}(\eta,z)\,e^{\mathrm{i}m\phi},\hskip 20.00003pth(\eta,\phi,z)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}h_{m}(\eta,z)\,e^{\mathrm{i}m\phi}\,.(4.7)

For the vector quantities, i.e., the source𝐠\mathbf{g}and unknown𝒖\boldsymbol{u},
the cylindrical expansion is applied to their components in(𝐞η,𝐞ϕ,𝐞z)(\mathbf{e}_{\eta},\mathbf{e}_{\phi},\mathbf{e}_{z})in cylindrical expansion, such that(η,ϕ,z)\displaystyle(\eta,\phi,z)=∑m∈ℤ𝒖m​(η,z)​ei​m​ϕ,where​𝒖m​(η,z)=(uη​m,uϕ​m,uz​m)t,\displaystyle=\,\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\boldsymbol{u}_{m}(\eta,z)\,e^{\mathrm{i}m\phi}\,,\quad\text{where}\,\,\boldsymbol{u}_{m}(\eta,z)=(u_{\eta m},u_{\phi m},u_{zm})^{t},(4.8)[𝐠]​(η,ϕ,z)\displaystyle[\mathbf{g}](\eta,\phi,z)=∑m∈ℤ𝐠m​(η,z)​ei​m​ϕ,where​𝐠m​(η,z)=(gη​m,gϕ​m,gz​m)t.\displaystyle=\,\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{g}_{m}(\eta,z)\,e^{\mathrm{i}m\phi}\,,\quad\text{where}\,\,\mathbf{g}_{m}(\eta,z)=(g_{\eta m},g_{\phi m},g_{zm})^{t}\,.

We also denote the restriction to the(η,z)(\eta,z)component by𝒖m~=(um​η,um​z)t.\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}_{m}}\,=\,(u_{m\eta},u_{mz})^{\mathrm{t}}.(4.9)

## Useful identities

Below are some identities needed to derive the modal problem.
- 1.

Introducing the operator[∇]m[\nabla]_{m}acting on a scalar functionww,
and[∇⋅]m[\nabla\cdot]_{m}acting on a vector𝒗=(v1,v2,v3)t\boldsymbol{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})^{t}, we have,[∇]mw:=(∂ηw,i​mηw,∂zw)t,[∇⋅]m𝒗=(∂η+1η)v1+i​mηv2+∂zv3.[\nabla]_{m}w:=(\partial_{\eta}w,\tfrac{\mathrm{i}\,m}{\eta}w,\partial_{z}w)^{t}\,,\qquad[\nabla\cdot]_{m}\boldsymbol{v}=(\partial_{\eta}+\tfrac{1}{\eta})v_{1}\,+\,\tfrac{\mathrm{i}\,m}{\eta}v_{2}\,+\,\partial_{z}v_{3}\,.(4.10)

They describe the action of the gradient
and divergence operators on each azimuthal
modemm:∇w(η,ϕ,z)=∑m∈ℤ[∇]mwm(η,z)ei​m​ϕ,∇⋅𝒗(η,ϕ,z)=∑m∈ℤ[∇⋅]m𝒗mei​m​ϕ.\nabla w(\eta,\phi,z)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}[\nabla]_{m}w_{m}(\eta,z)\,e^{\mathrm{i}\,m\,\phi},\quad\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v}(\eta,\phi,z)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}[\nabla\cdot]_{m}\boldsymbol{v}_{m}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\,m\,\phi}\,.(4.11)
- 2.

Due to the assumption on the rotation,
the differential operator𝑨\boldsymbol{A}is reduced on each modemmto a
multiplication operator with3×33\times 3matrix-valued functions
denoted by𝑨m​(η,z)\boldsymbol{A}_{m}(\eta,z),[𝑨​𝒖]​(η,ϕ,z)=∑m∈ℤ𝑨m​(η,z)​𝒖m​(η,z)​ei​m​ϕ.[\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{u}](\eta,\phi,z)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\boldsymbol{A}_{m}(\eta,z)\boldsymbol{u}_{m}(\eta,z)\,e^{\mathrm{i}\,m\,\phi}\,.(4.12)

The explicit expression of𝑨m​(η,z)\boldsymbol{A}_{m}(\eta,z)with differential rotation is further given inAppendixA.
- 3.

Under assumptionEq.4.2,
the boundary condition becomes:w+αbc​𝒖⋅𝒏𝔹⊙=0,whereαbc:=𝜶bc⋅𝒏𝔹⊙.w+\alpha_{\mathrm{bc}}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\mathbb{B}_{\odot}}=0,\quad\text{ where }\quad\alpha_{\mathrm{bc}}:=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{bc}}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\mathbb{B}_{\odot}}\,.(4.13)

We also note that𝒏𝔹⊙=𝐞r\boldsymbol{n}_{\mathbb{B}_{\odot}}=\mathbf{e}_{r},𝒖⋅𝒏𝔹⊙=[𝒖]⋅𝒏=∑m∈ℤ𝒖m~⋅𝒏~​ei​m​ϕ=∑m∈ℤ𝒖m⋅𝒏​ei​m​ϕ.\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\mathbb{B}_{\odot}}=[\boldsymbol{u}]\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}_{m}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{n}}\,e^{\mathrm{i}m\phi}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\boldsymbol{u}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\,e^{\mathrm{i}m\phi}.(4.14)

The cylindrical decomposition ofEq.4.13readily gives, for each modemm,wm+αbc​𝒖m⋅𝒏=0,on​∂Db.w_{m}+\alpha_{\mathrm{bc}}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0,\quad\text{ on }\partial D^{b}.(4.15)

## Boundary value problems on azimuthal modemm

By employing the above identities and upon equating coefficients
ofei​m​ϕe^{\mathrm{i}m\phi}on both sides ofEq.4.1,
we obtain an equation for each modemm.
With assumptionEq.4.2, in terms of
unknowns(𝒖m​(η,z),wm​(η,z))(\boldsymbol{u}_{m}(\eta,z),w_{m}(\eta,z))defined onDD, we have,𝑨m​𝒖m+[𝜷1]​wm+[∇]m​wm=𝐠m,\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}_{m}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{m}\,+\,[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}]\,w_{m}\,+\,[\nabla]_{m}w_{m}\,=\,\mathbf{g}_{m},(4.16a)[∇⋅]m𝒖m+[𝜷2]⋅𝒖m+ϱwm=hm.\displaystyle[\nabla\cdot]_{m}\boldsymbol{u}_{m}\,+\,[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}]\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{m}\,+\,\varrho\,w_{m}\,=\,h_{m}.(4.16b)

The system is coupled with vanishing Lagrangian pressure
on the exterior boundary∂Db\partial D^{b}and symmetry
condition on the rotation axis∂Da\partial D^{a}:𝒖m⋅𝒏=0,on​∂Da;wm+αbc​𝒖m⋅𝒏=0,on​∂Db.\boldsymbol{u}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\,=\,0\,,\quad\text{on }\partial D^{a}\,;\hskip 30.00005ptw_{m}+\alpha_{\mathrm{bc}}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\,=\,0\,,\quad\text{on }\partial D^{b}\,.(4.17)

We denote the operator in the modal problemEq.4.16asℒm\mathcal{L}_{m}:ℒm​(𝒖mwm)=(𝑨m[𝜷1]+[∇]m[∇⋅]m+[𝜷2]⋅+ϱ)​(𝒖mwm).\mathcal{L}_{m}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{u}_{m}\\
w_{m}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{A}_{m}\,\,&\,\,[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}]+[\nabla]_{m}\\
[\nabla\cdot]_{m}\,+\,[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}]\cdot\,\,&\,\,+\varrho\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{u}_{m}\\
w_{m}\end{pmatrix}\,.(4.18)

## Weak solution

Denote the cylindrical components(η,z)(\eta,z)by𝐱~\tilde{\mathbf{x}},𝐱~:=(η,z),d​𝐱~=d​η​d​z.\tilde{\mathbf{x}}:=(\eta,z),\quad d\tilde{\mathbf{x}}=d\eta dz\,.(4.19)

## Proof.

w\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\

w_{m}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\

FromEq.4.28and the definition ofGpG_{p}ofEq.2.71, we have,wm​(𝐱~)=∫DGpm​(𝐱~,𝐱~′)​hm​(𝐱~′)​η​𝑑𝐱~′,w=∫𝔹⊙Gp​(𝐱,𝐬)​h​(𝐬)​𝑑𝐬.w_{m}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}})=\int_{D}G^{m}_{p}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}},\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime})\,h_{m}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime})\eta\,d\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},\qquad w=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\odot}}G_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s})\,h(\mathbf{s})\,d\mathbf{s}\,.(4.32)

Replacing the first equality in the expansion ofww, we obtain,w\displaystyle w=∑m∈ℤwm​(𝐱~)​ei​m​ϕ=∑m∈ℤ(∫DGpm​(𝐱~,𝐱~′)​hm​(𝐱~′)​η​𝑑𝐱~′)​ei​m​ϕ\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}w_{m}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}})\,e^{\mathrm{i}m\phi}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\int_{D}G^{m}_{p}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}},\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime})\,h_{m}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime})\eta\,d\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}\right)e^{\mathrm{i}m\phi}(4.33)=∑m∈ℤ∫DGpm​(𝐱~,𝐱~′)​ei​m​ϕ​(12​π​∫02​πh​(𝐱~′,ϕ′)​e−i​m​ϕ′​𝑑ϕ′)​η′​𝑑𝐱~′\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{D}G^{m}_{p}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}},\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime})\,e^{\mathrm{i}m\phi}\,\left(\dfrac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}h(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\,e^{-\mathrm{i}m\phi^{\prime}}\,d\phi^{\prime}\right)\eta^{\prime}\,d\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}=12​π​∫02​π∫D(∑m∈ℤGpm​(𝐱~,𝐱~′)​ei​m​(ϕ−ϕ′))​h​(𝐱~′,ϕ′)​η′​𝑑𝐱~′​𝑑ϕ′.\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{D}\left(\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}G^{m}_{p}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}},\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime})\,e^{\mathrm{i}m(\phi-\phi^{\prime})}\right)h(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\eta^{\prime}\,d\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}\,.

## 5HDG formulation of the cylindrical modal equations

InSection4, we have derived the modal boundary value
problemEqs.4.16and4.17for each azimuthal modemmon the meridional half-diskDD,Figure2.
For the remaining of the section, the subscriptmmis dropped
from the unknowns and sources, such that we consider the system,{𝑨m​𝒖+𝜷1​w+[∇]m​w=𝐠,[∇⋅]m𝒖+𝜷2⋅𝒖+ϱw=h.​on​D,with boundary conditions​{𝒖⋅𝒏=0,on​∂Da,w+αbc​𝒖⋅𝒏=0,on​∂Db.\begin{dcases}\boldsymbol{A}_{m}\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}w+[\nabla]_{m}w=\mathbf{g},\\
[\nabla\cdot]_{m}\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}+\varrho\,w=h.\end{dcases}\text{ on }D,\hskip 2.84544pt\text{ with boundary conditions }\begin{dcases}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0,\hskip 38.99998pt\text{on }\partial D^{a},\\
w+\alpha_{\mathrm{bc}}\,\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0,\,\,\text{on }\partial D^{b}.\end{dcases}(5.1)

Recall that𝒖​(η,z)=(uη​(η,z),uϕ​(η,z),uz​(η,z))t\boldsymbol{u}(\eta,z)=(u_{\eta}(\eta,z),u_{\phi}(\eta,z),u_{z}(\eta,z))^{t}and𝒏\boldsymbol{n}is the normal vector defined from𝒏~\tilde{\boldsymbol{n}}of∂D=∂Da∪∂Db\partial D=\partial D^{a}\cup\partial D^{b}, cf.Eq.4.5.

We solve this problem with the Hybridizable Discontinuous Galerkin
method (HDG,[15]), following the perspective of[48]for notations.
In the HDG formulation, one distinguishes between the value of
an unknown on the union of the interior of mesh cells and its
trace on the skeleton of the mesh, here corresponding to a set
of edges.
Specifically, in addition to the volume unknowns(𝒖,w)(\boldsymbol{u},w)approximating the value (of the corresponding quantities)
in the interior of the cells, another unknown that we referred to as the
HDG numerical traceλ\lambdais introduced and defined on the set of
edges, to represent the Dirichlet trace ofwwthere.
With this variable, the global systemEq.4.16onDDis written as a union of boundary value problems (local BVP)
each defined on a mesh cell with boundary dataλ\lambda.

These local BVPs are decoupled from one another, i.e., independent per cell,
forming block-diagonal structures in the global linear system
(in terms of unknowns(𝒖,w,λ)(\boldsymbol{u},w,\lambda)).
The link between cells is carried out by the constraints on the
boundary dataλ\lambda, and imposes a certain degree of regularity
of the solution across the interfaces.
The aforementioned block-diagonal structure allows to employ
static condensation to yield a linear system solely in terms
ofλ\lambda, which forms the essence of the HDG method in
the DG family.
Another specificity of a HDG method is the choice of stabilization
of the error in the Neumann trace, which in implementation provides
an expression for the numerical trace𝒖⋅𝒏^\yhwidehat{\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}}(which approximates the trace of𝒖⋅𝒏=𝒖~⋅𝒏~\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{n}},
see below), in terms ofλ\lambdaand the volume unknowns.

## 5.1Domain discretization

We discretizeDDwith a non-overlapping
conforming mesh denoted by𝒯h\mathcal{T}_{h},
which consists ofncelln_{\mathrm{cell}}elementsKK.
The skeleton of the mesh is denoted byΣ\Sigmaand
is composed ofnfacen_{\mathrm{face}}faces, which are divided intoΣi\Sigma^{i}the set of interfaces,Σb\Sigma^{b}discretizing∂Db\partial D^{b}andΣa\Sigma^{a}discretizing∂Da\partial D^{a}. We have𝒯h=⋃e=1ncellKe,Σ=⋃e=1ncell∂Ke=Σb∪Σa∪Σi=⋃k=1nface𝔣k.\mathcal{T}_{h}=\bigcup_{e=1}^{n_{\mathrm{cell}}}K_{e}\,,\qquad\qquad\Sigma=\bigcup_{e=1}^{n_{\mathrm{cell}}}\partial K_{e}=\Sigma^{b}\cup\Sigma^{a}\cup\Sigma^{i}\,=\,\bigcup_{k=1}^{n_{\mathrm{face}}}\mathfrak{f}_{k}\,.(5.2)

## Normal vectors and jump notation

For a cellKK, we write its outward-pointing normal vector
in the meridional plane as𝒏~\tilde{\boldsymbol{n}}defined on∂K\partial K;
we also introduce its extension𝒏\boldsymbol{n}as inEq.4.5,𝒏~:=(nη,nz)t,𝒏:=(nη,0,nz)t.\tilde{\boldsymbol{n}}:=(n_{\eta},n_{z})^{t},\qquad\boldsymbol{n}:=(n_{\eta},0,n_{z})^{t}\,.(5.3)

We employ𝒏\boldsymbol{n}to limit the use of⋅~\tilde{\cdot}, particularly in expressions with numerical traces, by using interchangeably the left-hand side
and right-hand side of the following identity:𝒏~⋅𝐯~=𝒏⋅𝐯,for​𝐯=(vη,vϕ,vz)t,𝐯~=(vη,vz)t.\tilde{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{v}}=\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\mathbf{v},\quad\text{ for }\mathbf{v}=(v_{\eta},v_{\phi},v_{z})^{t},\,\,\,\,\tilde{\mathbf{v}}=(v_{\eta},v_{z})^{t}.(5.4)

The normal jump of a vector along an interior face𝔣∈Σi\mathfrak{f}\in\Sigma^{i}between cellK+K^{+}andK−K^{-},𝔣=K+¯∩K−¯\mathfrak{f}=\overline{K^{+}}\cap\overline{K^{-}}(followingEq.5.4), is denoted by⟦⋅⟧\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket:⟦𝒖⋅𝒏⟧=𝒖+⋅𝒏++𝒖−⋅𝒏−.\llbracket\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\rrbracket\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}^{+}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{+}\,+\,\boldsymbol{u}^{-}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{-}.(5.5)

Here, the vectors𝒏±\boldsymbol{n}^{\pm}are defined from the outward pointing
normal vectors𝒏~±\tilde{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\pm}ofK±K^{\pm}byEq.5.3,
and𝒖±\boldsymbol{u}^{\pm}are the interior Dirichlet traces of𝒖|K±\boldsymbol{u}|_{K^{\pm}}on∂K±\partial K^{\pm}.

## Local and global indexes of a face

A face𝔣∈Σ\mathfrak{f}\in\Sigmabears two systems of indexing, first as a member ofΣ\Sigma, and secondly as a member of the boundary of an elementK∈𝒯hK\in\mathcal{T}_{h},Face Global index:𝔣k,1≤k≤nface,Local index as a face ofKe:𝔣(e,ℓ),1≤e≤ncell,1≤ℓ≤nfacee,such that​𝔣k=𝔣(e,ℓ)​(as a set).\begin{array}[]{c c c}\text{Face Global index:}&\mathfrak{f}_{k},&1\leq k\leq n_{\mathrm{face}}\,,\\[5.0pt]
\text{Local index as a face of $K_{e}$:}&\mathfrak{f}^{(e,\ell)},&1\leq e\leq n_{\mathrm{cell}},\,\,1\leq\ell\leq n_{\mathrm{face}}^{e}\,,\end{array}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ such that }\mathfrak{f}_{k}=\mathfrak{f}^{(e,\ell)}\text{ (as a set).}(5.6)

We denote bynfaceen_{\mathrm{face}}^{e}the number of faces in a cellKK,∂Ke=⋃ℓ=1nfacee𝔣(e,ℓ),for simplex cell in 2D (triangle),nfacee=3.\partial K_{e}=\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{n_{\mathrm{face}}^{e}}\mathfrak{f}^{(e,\ell)},\qquad\text{for simplex cell in 2D (triangle), $n_{\mathrm{face}}^{e}=3$.}(5.7)

## 5.2Statement of the HDG formulation

The weak problem is stated in broken Lagrange finite elements, denoted byℙr\mathbb{P}_{r}indicating polynomials of orderrr:Wh={wh∈L2(D):wh∣Ke∈ℙre(Ke),∀Ke∈𝒯h};𝑾h=[Wh]3={𝒘h=(wη​hwϕ​hwz​h)∈L2(D)3:𝒘h∣Ke∈[ℙre(Ke)]3,∀Ke∈𝒯h};Vh={vh∈L2(Σ):vh∣𝔣k∈ℙr~k(𝔣k),𝔣k∈Σ}.\left.\qquad\begin{aligned} W_{h}&=\left\{w_{h}\in L^{2}(D):\hskip 60.00009ptw_{h}\!\mid_{K_{e}}\in\mathbb{P}_{r_{e}}(K_{e}),\hskip 5.69046pt\forall K_{e}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}\right\};\\[3.00003pt]
\boldsymbol{W}_{h}=[W_{h}]^{3}&=\left\{\boldsymbol{w}_{h}=\begin{pmatrix}w_{\eta h}\\[3.00003pt]
w_{\phi h}\\[3.00003pt]
w_{zh}\end{pmatrix}\in L^{2}(D)^{3}:\hskip 10.00002pt\boldsymbol{w}_{h}\!\mid_{K_{e}}\in[\mathbb{P}_{r_{e}}(K_{e})]^{3},\hskip 5.69046pt\forall K_{e}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}\right\};\\[3.00003pt]
V_{h}&=\left\{v_{h}\in L^{2}(\Sigma)\,:\hskip 60.00009ptv_{h}\mid_{\mathfrak{f}_{k}}\in\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{r}_{k}}(\mathfrak{f}_{k}),\hskip 5.69046pt\mathfrak{f}_{k}\in\Sigma\right\}.\end{aligned}\right.(5.8)

Note that the definitions allow forpp-adaptivity, i.e. different orders of approximation between cells.

## HDG formulation ofEq.5.1:find(wh,𝒖h,λh)∈Wh×𝑾h×Vh(w_{h},\boldsymbol{u}_{h},\lambda_{h})\in W_{h}\times\boldsymbol{W}_{h}\times V_{h}such that:1.for each cellK∈𝒯hK\in\mathcal{T}_{h},

the set of facesΣh\Sigma_{h}, which consists of the
continuity in the normal jump of𝒖h\boldsymbol{u}_{h}along the interfacesΣi\Sigma^{i},⟦𝒖h⋅𝒏^⟧=0onΣi,\llbracket\yhwidehat{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}}\rrbracket=0\,\quad\text{ on }\Sigma^{i},(5.10)and boundary conditionsEq.4.17on exterior faces,Σb∪Σa\Sigma^{b}\cup\Sigma^{a},𝒖h⋅𝒏^=0,on​Σa;1αbc​λh+𝒖h⋅𝒏^=0,on​Σb.\yhwidehat{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}}=0\,,\quad\text{on }\Sigma^{a}\,;\hskip 30.00005pt\dfrac{1}{\alpha_{\mathrm{bc}}}\lambda_{h}\,+\,\yhwidehat{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}}\,=\,0\,,\quad\text{on }\Sigma^{b}\,.(5.11)The above problems are supplemented with

## Remark 12.

The definitions of the numerical traces can be summarized as follows: on boundary edges,wh^=λh,and​𝒖h⋅𝒏^=𝒖h⋅𝐧−τ​(wh−λh),on​Σ∖Σi;\widehat{w_{h}}=\lambda_{h},\hskip 8.5359pt\text{and}\hskip 8.5359pt\yhwidehat{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}}=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\mathbf{n}\,-\,\tau\Big(w_{h}-\lambda_{h}\Big),\hskip 14.22636pt\text{ on }\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{i};(5.13)

for an interior face𝔣∈Σi\mathfrak{f}\in\Sigma^{i}, with𝔣=K+¯∩K−¯\mathfrak{f}=\overline{K^{+}}\cap\overline{K^{-}}, we havewh^±=λh,and​𝒖h⋅𝐧^±=𝒖h±⋅𝐧±−τ±​(wh±−λh),on​∂K±.\widehat{w_{h}}^{\pm}=\lambda_{h},\hskip 8.5359pt\text{and}\hskip 8.5359pt\yhwidehat{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\mathbf{n}}^{\pm}=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\pm}\cdot\mathbf{n}^{\pm}\,-\,\tau^{\pm}\Big(w_{h}^{\pm}-\lambda_{h}\Big),\hskip 14.22636pt\text{ on }\partial K^{\pm}\,.(5.14)

Here, the values associated with cellsK±K^{\pm}are distinguished by±\pm.

## Remark 13.

In the second boundary condition ofEq.5.11, we have employed that the numerical trace ofwhw_{h}is given byλh\lambda_{h}, i.e.wh^=λh\yhwidehat{w_{h}}=\lambda_{h}. By substituting the definition of𝐮h⋅𝐧^\yhwidehat{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}}fromEq.5.13, we can further rewrite the left-hand side
of this condition as,1αbc​λh+𝒖h⋅𝒏^=1αbc​λh+𝒖h⋅𝐧−τ​(wh−λh)=𝒖h⋅𝐧−τ​wh+(τ+1αbc)​λh.\dfrac{1}{\alpha_{\mathrm{bc}}}\lambda_{h}+\yhwidehat{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}}=\dfrac{1}{\alpha_{\mathrm{bc}}}\lambda_{h}+\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\mathbf{n}\,-\,\tau\Big(w_{h}-\lambda_{h}\Big)=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\mathbf{n}-\tau w_{h}+\left(\tau+\dfrac{1}{\alpha_{\mathrm{bc}}}\right)\lambda_{h}.(5.15)

## 5.3Statement of the HDG problem in weak form

we make appear the role ofλh\lambda_{h}:∫K𝝍~⋅([∇]~​wh)​η​𝑑𝐱~\displaystyle\int_{K}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\cdot(\widetilde{[\nabla]}w_{h})\eta\,d\tilde{\mathbf{x}}=−∫Kwh​([∇⋅]~​𝝍~)​η​𝑑𝐱~+∫∂Kwh^​𝒏⋅𝝍​η​𝑑s𝐱~\displaystyle=-\int_{K}w_{h}(\widetilde{[\nabla\cdot]}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}})\,\eta\,d\tilde{\mathbf{x}}+\int_{\partial K}\widehat{w_{h}}\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\eta\,ds_{\tilde{\mathbf{x}}}=−∫Kwh​([∇⋅]~​𝝍~)​η​𝑑𝐱~+∫∂Kλh​𝒏⋅𝝍​η​𝑑s𝐱~,\displaystyle=-\int_{K}w_{h}(\widetilde{[\nabla\cdot]}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}})\,\eta\,d\tilde{\mathbf{x}}+\int_{\partial K}\lambda_{h}\,\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\eta\,ds_{\tilde{\mathbf{x}}},(5.28a)and​∫Kφ​([∇⋅]~​𝒖h~)​η​𝑑𝐱~\displaystyle\text{and }\hskip 2.84544pt\int_{K}\!\varphi\,(\widetilde{[\nabla\cdot]}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}})\eta\,d\tilde{\mathbf{x}}=−∫K𝒖h​([∇]~​φ)​η​𝑑𝐱~+∫∂K𝒖h⋅𝒏^​φ​η​𝑑s𝐱~\displaystyle=-\int_{K}\!\boldsymbol{u}_{h}(\widetilde{[\nabla]}\varphi)\,\eta\,d\tilde{\mathbf{x}}+\int_{\partial K}\yhwidehat{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}}\varphi\,\eta ds_{\tilde{\mathbf{x}}}=−∫K𝒖h​([∇]~​φ)​η​𝑑𝐱~+∫∂K(𝒖h⋅𝒏−τ​(wh−λh))​φ​η​𝑑s𝐱~\displaystyle\hskip-30.00005pt=-\int_{K}\!\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\,(\widetilde{[\nabla]}\varphi)\,\eta\,d\tilde{\mathbf{x}}+\int_{\partial K}\Big(\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-\tau(w_{h}-\lambda_{h})\Big)\varphi\,\eta\,ds_{\tilde{\mathbf{x}}}=(−∫K𝒖h​([∇]~​φ)​η​𝑑𝐱~+∫∂K𝒖h⋅𝒏​φ​η​𝑑s𝐱~)−∫∂Kτ​(wh−λh)​φ​η​𝑑s𝐱~.\displaystyle\hskip-30.00005pt=\left(-\int_{K}\!\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\,(\widetilde{[\nabla]}\,\varphi)\,\eta\,d\tilde{\mathbf{x}}+\int_{\partial K}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\,\varphi\,\eta\,ds_{\tilde{\mathbf{x}}}\right)-\int_{\partial K}\tau(w_{h}-\lambda_{h})\varphi\,\eta\,ds_{\tilde{\mathbf{x}}}\,.(5.28b)

In carrying out a reverse integration by parts,
the term in parentheses ofEq.5.28bsimplifies,
with that the derivative falling back to𝒖~h\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{h}, i.e.,∫Kφ​([∇⋅]~​𝒖h~)​η​𝑑𝐱~=∫Kφ​([∇⋅]~​𝒖h~)​η​𝑑𝐱~−∫∂Kτ​(wh−λh)​φ​η​𝑑s𝐱~.\int_{K}\!\varphi\,(\widetilde{[\nabla\cdot]}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}})\,\eta\,d\tilde{\mathbf{x}}=\int_{K}\!\varphi\,(\widetilde{[\nabla\cdot]}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}})\,\eta\,d\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\int_{\partial K}\tau(w_{h}-\lambda_{h})\varphi\,\eta\,ds_{\tilde{\mathbf{x}}}\,.(5.29)

SubstitutingEqs.5.29and5.28ainEq.5.26to replace the terms with derivatives,
we arrive at the problem statedEq.5.22.

We gather the coefficientspj[∙]\mathrm{p}_{j}^{[\bullet]}associated with a cellKeK_{e}into the vector𝖴e\mathsf{U}^{e}, containing four blocks,𝖴e=(𝕡[uη]𝕡[uϕ]𝕡[uz]𝕡[w])t,with​𝕡[∙]=(pj[∙])j=1ndof.\mathsf{U}^{e}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{p}_{[u_{\eta}]}&\mathbb{p}_{[u_{\phi}]}&\mathbb{p}_{[u_{z}]}&\mathbb{p}_{[w]}\end{pmatrix}^{t},\hskip 40.00006pt\text{ with }\hskip 2.84544pt\mathbb{p}_{[\bullet]}\,=\,\left(\mathrm{p}^{[\bullet]}_{j}\right)_{j=1}^{n_{\mathrm{dof}}}.(5.33)

## Edge discrete unknowns

The HDG trace unknownλh\lambda_{h}is represented on each face𝔣k∈Σ\mathfrak{f}_{k}\in\Sigmain the basis functionsζjk\zeta_{j}^{k}Eq.5.30with coefficientsqjk\mathrm{q}_{j}^{k}(also listed in local indexingEq.5.6),λh∣𝔣k=∑j=1ndof𝔣qjk​ζjk,with​qjk=qj(e,ℓ)​where​𝔣k=𝔣(e,ℓ).\lambda_{h}\!\mid_{\mathfrak{f}_{k}}\,=\,\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\mathrm{dof}}^{\mathfrak{f}}}\,\mathrm{q}_{j}^{k}\,\zeta^{k}_{j},\quad\text{with }\mathrm{q}_{j}^{k}=\mathrm{q}_{j}^{(e,\ell)}\hskip 2.84544pt\text{ where }\hskip 2.84544pt\mathfrak{f}_{k}=\mathfrak{f}^{(e,\ell)}.(5.34)

We gatherqjk\mathrm{q}_{j}^{k}into the global vectorΛ\Lambdaof lengthNdofN_{\mathrm{dof}}which is the unknown of the global HDG problem,Λ=(𝕢k)k=1nface,with​𝕢k=(qjk)j=1ndof𝔣.\Lambda=\left(\mathbb{q}^{k}\right)_{k=1}^{n_{\mathrm{face}}},\hskip 5.69046pt\text{ with }\hskip 5.69046pt\mathbb{q}^{k}=(\mathrm{q}_{j}^{k})_{j=1}^{n_{\mathrm{dof}}^{\mathfrak{f}}}.(5.35)

Its restriction to an elementKeK_{e}is given byℝe\mathbb{R}_{e}, which gathers the
coefficients from each face𝔣(e,ℓ)∈Ke\mathfrak{f}^{(e,\ell)}\in K_{e},1≤ℓ≤nface1\leq\ell\leq n_{\mathrm{face}}of
an elementKeK_{e},Λe:=ℝe​Λ=(𝕢(e,ℓ))ℓ=1nface,with𝕢(e,ℓ)=(qj(e,ℓ))j=1ndof𝔣.\Lambda^{e}:=\mathbb{R}_{e}\Lambda\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{q}^{(e,\ell)}\end{pmatrix}_{\ell=1}^{n_{\mathrm{face}}},\quad\text{ with }\quad\mathbb{q}^{(e,\ell)}=\left(\mathrm{q}_{j}^{(e,\ell)}\right)_{j=1}^{n_{\mathrm{dof}}^{\mathfrak{f}}}.(5.36)

We also note thatℝet\mathbb{R}_{e}^{t}assigns contribution
defined on an elementKeK_{e}to the global vector,[48, Equation (3.39)].

## 5.4.1Local problems

The local problemEq.5.22is discretized and
written in matrix form such as, for eachKeK_{e},1≤e≤ncell1\leq e\leq n_{\mathrm{cell}}:𝔸e​𝖴e+ℂe​ℝe​Λ=𝖦e.\mathbb{A}^{e}\,\mathsf{U}^{e}\,+\,\mathbb{C}^{e}\,\mathbb{R}_{e}\Lambda\,=\,\mathsf{G}^{e}\,.(5.37)

The matrix𝔸e\mathbb{A}^{e}has a4×44\times 4block structure,
while the matrixℂe\mathbb{C}^{e}has a4×nfacee4\times n_{\mathrm{face}}^{e}block structure,𝔸e\displaystyle\mathbb{A}^{e}=(𝔸e​[I,J])I,J=1,…,4=(𝔸e​[1,1]…𝔸e​[1,4]⋮⋱⋮𝔸e​[4,1]…𝔸e​[4,4]),\displaystyle=\left(\mathbb{A}^{e}[I,J]\right)_{I,J=1,\ldots,4}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{A}^{e}[1,1]&\ldots&\mathbb{A}^{e}[1,4]\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\[3.00003pt]
\mathbb{A}^{e}[4,1]&\ldots&\mathbb{A}^{e}[4,4]\end{pmatrix},(5.38a)ℂe\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{e}=(ℂe​[I,ℓ])I=1,…,4;ℓ=1,…,nfacee=(ℂe​[1,1]…ℂe​[1,nfacee]⋮⋱⋮ℂe​[4,1]…ℂe​[4,nfacee]).\displaystyle=\left(\mathbb{C}^{e}[I,\ell]\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}I=1,\ldots,4;\\[2.45836pt]
\ell=1,\ldots,n_{\mathrm{face}}^{e}\end{subarray}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{C}^{e}[1,1]&\ldots&\mathbb{C}^{e}[1,n_{\mathrm{face}}^{e}]\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\mathbb{C}^{e}[4,1]&\ldots&\mathbb{C}^{e}[4,n_{\mathrm{face}}^{e}]\end{pmatrix}.(5.38b)

The first three row-blocks,𝔸e[1:3,:]\mathbb{A}^{e}[1:3,:]andℂe[1:3,:]\mathbb{C}^{e}[1:3,:],
correspond to the volume integrals inEq.5.22aand
with test functions containing non-zero components

to the volume integrals inEq.5.22b.

The vector source has a length4×ndof4\times n_{\mathrm{dof}},
we list its components for the case of a Dirac source
on the scalar equation at position𝐬~=(sη,sz)\tilde{\mathbf{s}}=(s_{\eta},s_{z}):𝖦e=(𝖦e​[I])I=1,…,4,with​{𝐬~∉Ke:𝖦e​[:]=𝟎ndof×1,𝐬~∈Ke:𝖦e[1:3]=𝟎ndof×1;𝖦e[4]=(φi(𝐬~))i=1ndof.\mathsf{G}^{e}=(\mathsf{G}^{e}[I])_{I=1,\ldots,4},\text{ with }\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}\notin K^{e}:&\hskip 8.5359pt\mathsf{G}^{e}[:]=\boldsymbol{0}_{n_{\mathrm{dof}}\times 1},\\[3.00003pt]
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}\in K^{e}:&\hskip 8.5359pt\mathsf{G}^{e}[1:3]=\boldsymbol{0}_{n_{\mathrm{dof}}\times 1}\,;\quad\mathsf{G}^{e}[4]=\big(\varphi_{i}(\tilde{\mathbf{s}})\big)_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{dof}}}\,.\end{array}\right.(5.39)

## Components of𝔸e\mathbb{A}^{e}

Since each component of𝐮h\mathbf{u}_{h}andwhw_{h}is discretized
with the same basis functions{φje}\{\varphi_{j}^{e}\}Eq.5.30,
we describe all together the elements(i,j)(i,j)of each
sub-block listed inEq.5.38a.
For this, we introduce the vector-valued differential operator𝒟\mathcal{D}acting on a scalar functionφ\varphi,𝒟m​φ:=(∂ηφ+1η​φ,i​mη​φ,∂zφ)t,𝒟m¯​φ:=(∂ηφ+1η​φ,−i​mη​φ,∂zφ)t.\mathcal{D}_{m}\varphi:=\left(\partial_{\eta}\varphi+\dfrac{1}{\eta}\varphi\,,\,\,\mathrm{i}\dfrac{m}{\eta}\varphi\,,\,\partial_{z}\varphi\right)^{t},\quad\overline{\mathcal{D}_{m}}\varphi:=\left(\partial_{\eta}\varphi+\dfrac{1}{\eta}\varphi\,,\,-\mathrm{i}\dfrac{m}{\eta}\varphi\,,\,\partial_{z}\varphi\right)^{t}.(5.40)

The first three row-blocks of𝔸e\mathbb{A}^{e}are listed as3×43\times 4matrices: fori,ji,j,1≤i,j≤ndof1\leq i,j\leq n_{\mathrm{dof}},(𝔸e​[I,J]​(i,j))I=1,…,3,J=1,…,4\displaystyle\Big(\mathbb{A}^{e}[I,J](i,j)\Big)_{\begin{subarray}{c}I=1,\ldots,3,\\
J=1,\ldots,4\end{subarray}}=∫Kη​φj​(φi​𝑨m,φi​[𝜷1]−𝒟m¯​φi)​𝑑𝐱~,\displaystyle=\int_{K}\eta\,\varphi_{j}\Big(\varphi_{i}\,\boldsymbol{A}_{m}\hskip 5.69046pt,\hskip 5.69046pt\varphi_{i}\,[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}]\,-\,\overline{\mathcal{D}_{m}}\,\varphi_{i}\,\Big)\,d\tilde{\mathbf{x}},(5.41)(𝔸e​[I,J]​(i,j))I=4,J=1,…​4\displaystyle\Big(\mathbb{A}^{e}[I,J](i,j)\Big)_{\begin{subarray}{c}I=4,\\
J=1,\ldots 4\end{subarray}}=∫Kη​φi​(φj​[𝜷2]t+𝒟mt​φj,ρ)​𝑑𝐱~−∫∂Kη​(𝟎,τ)​φj​𝑑s𝐱~.\displaystyle=\int_{K}\eta\,\varphi_{i}\Big(\varphi_{j}\,[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}]^{t}\,+\,\mathcal{D}_{m}^{t}\,\varphi_{j}\hskip 5.69046pt,\hskip 5.69046pt\rho\Big)\,d\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\int_{\partial K}\eta\,\Big(\boldsymbol{0}\,,\,\tau\Big)\varphi_{j}\,ds_{\tilde{\mathbf{x}}}.

Here the integration is carried out component-wise.
Recall that the matrix-valued function𝑨m\boldsymbol{A}_{m}has size3×33\times 3,
while the vector-valued functions[𝜷1][\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}]and[𝜷2][\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}]are of size3×13\times 1;𝟎\boldsymbol{0}is the zero row vector of length 3.

Forℓ=1,…,nfacee\ell=1,\ldots,n_{\mathrm{face}}^{e}, we describe the components for each column-blockℓ\ell,
within which
the(i,j)(i,j)-th element of the four blocks are listed as a column vector of length 4,(ℂe​[I,ℓ]​(i,j))I=14=∫𝔣(e,ℓ)φie​ζj(e,ℓ)​(nη,0,nz,τ)t​η​𝑑s𝐱~.\Big(\,\mathbb{C}^{e}[I,\ell](i,j)\,\Big)_{I=1}^{4}=\int_{\mathfrak{f}^{(e,\ell)}}\varphi^{e}_{i}\,\zeta_{j}^{(e,\ell)}\,\ \big(n_{\eta}\,,\,0\,,\,n_{z}\,,\,\tau\big)^{t}\,\eta\,ds_{\tilde{\mathbf{x}}}.(5.42)

## 5.4.2Problems on edges

The discretization of the edge problemsEq.5.23–Eq.5.25onΣ\Sigmatakes the following form,∑e=1ncellℝet​(𝔹e​𝖴e+𝕃e​ℝe​Λ)=𝟎,\sum_{e=1}^{n_{\mathrm{cell}}}\mathbb{R}_{e}^{t}\left(\mathbb{B}^{e}\,\mathsf{U}^{e}\,+\,\mathbb{L}^{e}\,\mathbb{R}_{e}\,\Lambda\right)=\boldsymbol{0},(5.43)

where the matrix𝕃e\mathbb{L}^{e}associated with the
trace unknownΛ\Lambdais block-diagonal containingnfaceen_{\mathrm{face}}^{e}blocks,𝕃e=Diag​(𝕃e​[1],…,𝕃e​[nfacee]).\mathbb{L}^{e}=\mathrm{Diag}\big(\mathbb{L}^{e}[1],\ldots,\mathbb{L}^{e}[n_{\mathrm{face}}^{e}]\big)\,.(5.44)

Each sub-block𝕃e​[ℓ]\mathbb{L}^{e}[\ell],1≤ℓ≤nfacee1\leq\ell\leq n_{\mathrm{face}}^{e}, is of sizendof𝔣×ndof𝔣n_{\mathrm{dof}}^{\mathfrak{f}}\times n_{\mathrm{dof}}^{\mathfrak{f}}representing contribution from face(e,ℓ)(e,\ell).
The matrix𝔹e\mathbb{B}^{e}has a block structurenfacee×4n_{\mathrm{face}}^{e}\times 4,𝔹e=(𝔹e​[ℓ,J])J=1,…,4;ℓ=1,…,nfacee=(𝔹e​[1,1]…𝔹e​[1,4]⋮⋱⋮𝔹e​[nfacee,1]…𝔹e​[nfacee,4]),\mathbb{B}^{e}=\left(\mathbb{B}^{e}[\ell,J]\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}J=1,\ldots,4;\\[2.45836pt]
\ell=1,\ldots,n_{\mathrm{face}}^{e}\end{subarray}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{B}^{e}[1,1]&\ldots&\mathbb{B}^{e}[1,4]\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\mathbb{B}^{e}[n_{\mathrm{face}}^{e},1]&\ldots&\mathbb{B}^{e}[n_{\mathrm{face}}^{e},4]\end{pmatrix},(5.45)

with each column-block associated with the volume unknown𝖴e\mathsf{U}^{e},
and each row-block representing contribution (of all volume unknowns)
from the face(e,ℓ)(e,\ell).

We next describe the components of the sub-blocks inℓ\ell-th column block:ℓ=1,…,nfacee\ell=1,\ldots,n_{\mathrm{face}}^{e},

For matrix𝕃e\mathbb{L}^{e}, the(i,j)(i,j)-th element
of theℓ\ell-th block: fori,j=1,…,ndof𝔣i,j=1,\ldots,n_{\mathrm{dof}}^{\mathfrak{f}}, is,𝕃e​[ℓ]​(i,j)={∫𝔣(e,ℓ)τ​ζi(e,ℓ)​ζj(e,ℓ)​ds𝐱~,𝔣(e,ℓ)∈Σi∪Σa,∫𝔣(e,ℓ)ζi(e,ℓ)​ζj(e,ℓ)​(τ+1αbc)​ds𝐱~,𝔣(e,ℓ)∈Σb.\mathbb{L}^{e}[\ell](i,j)\hskip 0.0pt=\hskip 1.00006pt\begin{dcases}\int_{\mathfrak{f}^{(e,\ell)}}\tau\,\zeta^{(e,\ell)}_{i}\,\zeta^{(e,\ell)}_{j}\,\mathrm{d}s_{\tilde{\mathbf{x}}},&\hskip 5.69046pt\mathfrak{f}^{(e,\ell)}\in\Sigma^{i}\cup\Sigma^{a},\\[-1.00006pt]
\int_{\mathfrak{f}^{(e,\ell)}}\zeta^{(e,\ell)}_{i}\,\zeta_{j}^{(e,\ell)}\left(\tau+\dfrac{1}{\alpha_{\mathrm{bc}}}\right)\mathrm{d}s_{\tilde{\mathbf{x}}}\,,&\hskip 5.69046pt\mathfrak{f}^{(e,\ell)}\in\Sigma^{b}.\end{dcases}(5.46)

For matrix𝔹e\mathbb{B}^{e}, we group the(i,j)(i,j)-th element from the four sub-blocks in this row-block: fori=1,…,ndoffi=1,\ldots,n_{\mathrm{dof}}^{f}, andj=1,…,ndofj=1,\ldots,n_{\mathrm{dof}},(𝔹e​[I,ℓ]​(i,j))I=14=∫𝔣(e,ℓ)ζi(e,ℓ)​φje​(nη,0,nz,−τ)​η​𝑑s𝐱~.\Big(\mathbb{B}^{e}[I,\ell](i,j)\Big)_{I=1}^{4}=\int_{\mathfrak{f}^{(e,\ell)}}\zeta^{(e,\ell)}_{i}\,\varphi^{e}_{j}\,\,\big(n_{\eta},0,n_{z},-\tau\big)\,\eta\,ds_{\tilde{\mathbf{x}}}.(5.47)

## 5.4.3Summary

We can now put the discretized local and global problems together,
taking the generic form of the HDG method showing the two-level characteristics.
The description follows notation of[48, Section 3.3.4](where the HDG method is used for elastic anisotropic waves),𝔸e​𝖴e+ℂe​ℝe​Λ=𝖦e,∀e=1​…,ncell,\displaystyle\hskip 19.91684pt\mathbb{A}^{e}\,\mathsf{U}^{e}\,\,+\,\,\mathbb{C}^{e}\,\mathbb{R}_{e}\,\Lambda=\,\mathsf{G}^{e}\,\,,\hskip 28.45274pt\forall\,\,e=1\,\ldots,n_{\mathrm{cell}}\,,(5.48a)∑e=1ncellℝet​(𝔹e​𝖴e+𝕃e​ℝe​Λ)=0Ndof×1.\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{e=1}^{n_{\mathrm{cell}}}\,\,\mathbb{R}^{t}_{e}\,\Big(\,\mathbb{B}^{e}\,\mathsf{U}^{e}\,\,+\,\,\mathbb{L}^{e}\,\mathbb{R}_{e}\,\Lambda\,\Big)\,=\,\boldsymbol{0}_{N_{\mathrm{dof}}\times 1}.(5.48b)

With the local problemsEq.5.48adecoupled from one another,
and assuming the invertibility of𝔸e\mathbb{A}^{e}, one can solve for𝖴e\mathsf{U}^{e}in terms ofΛ\Lambda,𝖴e=(𝔸e)−1​(𝖦e−ℂe​ℝe​Λ),∀e=1,…,ncell.\mathsf{U}^{e}\,=\,\left(\mathbb{A}^{e}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathsf{G}^{e}\,-\,\mathbb{C}^{e}\,\mathbb{R}_{e}\,\Lambda\right)\,,\hskip 28.45274pt\forall e=1,\ldots,n_{\mathrm{cell}}.(5.49)

Inserting this intoEq.5.48b, we obtain a problem only in terms ofΛ\Lambda, called the global problem,𝕂​Λ=𝖲where​{𝖲:=−∑e=1ncellℝet​𝔹e​(𝔸e)−1​𝖦e,𝕂:=∑e=1ncellℝet​𝕂e​ℝe,with​𝕂e:=𝕃e−𝔹e​(𝔸e)−1​ℂe.\mathbb{K}\,\Lambda\,=\,\mathsf{S}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where }\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{S}:=-\displaystyle\sum_{e=1}^{n_{\mathrm{cell}}}\mathbb{R}^{t}_{e}\,\mathbb{B}^{e}\,(\mathbb{A}^{e})^{-1}\,\mathsf{G}^{e},\\[15.00002pt]
\mathbb{K}:=\hskip 8.5359pt\displaystyle\sum_{e=1}^{n_{\mathrm{cell}}}\,\mathbb{R}^{t}_{e}\,\mathbb{K}^{e}\,\mathbb{R}_{e}\,,\hskip 8.5359pt\text{with}\hskip 5.69046pt\mathbb{K}^{e}:=\mathbb{L}^{e}\,-\,\mathbb{B}^{e}\,(\mathbb{A}^{e})^{-1}\,\mathbb{C}^{e}.\end{array}\right.(5.50)

## Remark 14.

Implementation is carried out in the open-source softwareHawen,[19](https://ffaucher.gitlab.io/hawen-website/)
which usesMPIandOpenMPparallelism.
Once the global sparse linear systemEq.5.50is assembled, it is solved with the direct solver MUMPS,[2,1],
allowing for the fast resolution with multiple right-hand sides.
Once the discrete solutionΛ\Lambdais obtained, the local systemEq.5.49are solved. This step is embarrassingly parallel on each cell.

## 5.5Stabilization operator

We devise a strategy to select the stabilization operatorτ\tauappearing in the numerical trace of𝒖⋅𝒏\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}Eq.5.12,𝒖⋅𝒏^=𝒖h⋅𝐧−τ​(wh−λ),on​∂K​for eachK∈𝒯h.\yhwidehat{\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}}=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\mathbf{n}\,-\,\tau\Big(w_{h}-\lambda\Big),\quad\text{ on }\partial K\text{ for each $K$ }\in\mathcal{T}_{h}\,.(5.51)

Recall this relation was employed for the problem defined on edgesΣ\Sigmaas well as in the local volume problem,Eq.5.22–Eq.5.25.
With our systemEq.5.1bearing similarities to that of the linear convection-diffusion equation considered in[45,55], we adapt the strategy of[45]to selectτ\tau.

## Derivation ofEq.5.12

We first show that the general formEq.5.12is obtained containing a more specific decomposition ofτ\tau.
Consider an edge𝔣∈Σ\mathfrak{f}\in\Sigma, where
we assume that𝑨m\boldsymbol{A}_{m}is invertible, and that the
support of source𝐠m\mathbf{g}_{m}does not intersect with𝔣\mathfrak{f}.
Let us introduce the following quantity,η𝜷:=𝒏⋅𝑨m−1⋅𝜷1,η𝒏:=𝒏⋅𝑨m−1⋅𝒏.\eta_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:=\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{-1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\qquad\eta_{\boldsymbol{n}}:=\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{-1}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}.(5.52)

Their meanings are investigated for radially symmetric with rotation backgrounds inRemark15.
- –

Under the current assumption, from relationEq.5.9a, we have𝒖h=−𝑨m−1​(𝜷1​wh+[∇]m​wh)⇒𝒖h⋅𝒏=−η𝜷​wh−𝒏⋅𝑨m−1⋅[∇]m​wh.\boldsymbol{u}_{h}=-\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\,w_{h}\,+\,[\nabla]_{m}w_{h}\right)\quad\Rightarrow\quad\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=-\eta_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}w_{h}-\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{-1}\cdot[\nabla]_{m}w_{h}.(5.53)

which distinguishes the term of convection type,ηβ​wh^\yhwidehat{\eta_{\beta}\,w_{h}},

we defineη𝜷​wh^\displaystyle\yhwidehat{\eta_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}w_{h}}:=η𝜷​λ+τc​(wh−λ),\displaystyle:=\hskip 5.69046pt\eta_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\lambda\hskip 8.5359pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}+}\,\,\tau_{c}(w_{h}-\lambda),(5.55a)𝐧⋅𝑨m−1⋅[∇]m​wh^\displaystyle\yhwidehat{\mathbf{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{-1}\cdot[\nabla]_{m}w_{h}}:=𝐧⋅Am−1⋅[∇]m​wh+τd​(wh−λ).\displaystyle:=\mathbf{n}\cdot A_{m}^{-1}\cdot[\nabla]_{m}w_{h}\,\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}+}\,\,\tau_{d}(w_{h}-\lambda).(5.55b)

In substituting the definitionsEq.5.55inEq.5.54, we obtain111111We also add and subtractηβ​wh\eta_{\beta}w_{h}, to make appear𝒖h⋅𝒏\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}in employing expressionEq.5.53𝒖h⋅𝐧^=−ηβ​λ−τc​(wh−λ)−𝐧⋅𝑨−1⋅∇wh−τd​(wh−λ)=𝒖h⋅𝒏+ηβ​(wh−λ)−(τc+τd)​(wh−λ).\yhwidehat{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\mathbf{n}}=-\eta_{\beta}\lambda\,\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}-}\,\,\tau_{c}(w_{h}-\lambda)-\mathbf{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\cdot\nabla w_{h}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}-}\tau_{d}(w_{h}-\lambda)=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+\eta_{\beta}(w_{h}-\lambda)-(\tau_{c}+\tau_{d})(w_{h}-\lambda).(5.56)𝒖⋅𝒏^=𝒖h⋅𝒏−(τc−ηβ+τd)​(wh−λ)\boxed{\yhwidehat{\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}}=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\,\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}-}\,\,(\tau_{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}-}\eta_{\beta}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}+}\,\,\tau_{d})(w_{h}-\lambda)}(5.57)

## Choice of stabilization

We chooseτc\tau_{c}andτd\tau_{d}inEq.5.57as follows:τc:=|η𝜷|+η𝜷,i.e.​τc−η𝜷=|η𝜷|,\tau_{c}:=\lvert\eta_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\rvert+\eta_{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\hskip 28.45274pt\text{ i.e. }\,\,\tau_{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}-}\eta_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\lvert\eta_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\rvert,(5.58)

We further allow for some tuning in the stabilization with a scaling parameterαtune\alpha_{\text{tune}}such that,τd:=i​ω​αtune​|η𝒏|.\tau_{d}:=\mathrm{i}\omega\,\alpha_{\text{tune}}\,\lvert\eta_{\boldsymbol{n}}\rvert\,.(5.59)

## Remark 15(Interpretation of HDG stabilization in radially symmetric backgrounds).

Here, we provide some interpretation for stabilizationEqs.5.58and5.59for radially symmetric backgrounds.
We first recall that without rotation and
with radially symmetric background, fromEq.3.29, we haveη𝜷=nr​β1N2−σ2,η𝒏=c02N2−σ2​nr2−c02σ2​n∥2.\eta_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\dfrac{n_{r}\beta_{1}}{N^{2}-\sigma^{2}},\qquad\eta_{\boldsymbol{n}}=\dfrac{c_{0}^{2}}{N^{2}-\sigma^{2}}n_{r}^{2}-\dfrac{c_{0}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}n_{\parallel}^{2}\,.(5.60)

We also recall fromEq.3.49the distinction between the elliptic region and the hyperbolic region, defined respectively as,𝔹ell:={ω2>N2​(r)}​(Elliptic region),𝔹hyp:={ω2<N2​(r)}​(Hyperbolic region).\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}:=\{\omega^{2}>N^{2}(r)\}\hskip 8.5359pt\text{(Elliptic region)},\quad\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}:=\{\omega^{2}<N^{2}(r)\}\hskip 8.5359pt\text{(Hyperbolic region)}.(5.61)

We introduce some working notions in the hyperbolic region. Compared to a classical wave operator∂t2−Δx\partial_{t}^{2}-\Delta_{x}, the radial direction here is analogous to the time direction, while the tangential direction to the spatial direction. Faces are named based on the sign ofη𝐧\eta_{\boldsymbol{n}}at zero attenuation, i.e. withσ2=ω2>0\sigma^{2}=\omega^{2}>0,𝔣={is space-like,η𝒏>0,is time-like,η𝒏<0,for​𝔣∈𝔹hyp.\mathfrak{f}=\begin{dcases}\text{ is space-like},&\quad\eta_{\boldsymbol{n}}>0,\\
\text{ is time-like},&\quad\eta_{\boldsymbol{n}}<0\end{dcases},\quad\text{for }\mathfrak{f}\in\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}.(5.62)

In particular whennr=0n_{r}=0on𝔣\mathfrak{f},η𝐧<0\eta_{\boldsymbol{n}}<0and𝔣\mathfrak{f}is space-like. We also define speed𝔠\mathfrak{c}as,𝔠=N2−ω2ω2>0,on​𝔹hyp.\mathfrak{c}=\sqrt{\dfrac{N^{2}-\omega^{2}}{\omega^{2}}}>0,\quad\text{ on }\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}.(5.63)

We can writeη𝐧\eta_{\boldsymbol{n}}as (at zero attenuation),η𝒏=c02N2−ω2​(nr2−𝔠2​n∥2),on​𝔹hyp.\eta_{\boldsymbol{n}}=\dfrac{c_{0}^{2}}{N^{2}-\omega^{2}}\left(n_{r}^{2}-\mathfrak{c}^{2}n_{\parallel}^{2}\right),\quad\text{ on }\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}.(5.64)

We next observe the different nature ofτd\tau_{d}(diffusion-type stabilization) andτc\tau_{c}(convection-type stabilization) at zero attenuation. The definition ofτc\tau_{c}which depends onη𝐧\eta_{\boldsymbol{n}}reflects the nature of the PDE on𝔣\mathfrak{f}, whileτd\tau_{d}which depends onη𝛃\eta_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}only signals the direction of𝐧~\tilde{\mathbf{n}}.
- –

Diffusion-type: with the sign ofη𝒏\eta_{\boldsymbol{n}}given byη𝒏={<0,elliptic or hyperbolic time-like,>0,hyperbolic space-like,\eta_{\boldsymbol{n}}=\begin{dcases}<0,&\quad\text{ elliptic or hyperbolic time-like},\\
>0,&\quad\text{ hyperbolic space-like},\end{dcases}(5.65)

the stabilizationτd\tau_{d}takes the following form,τd=i​ω​αtune​η𝒏×{−1,elliptic or hyperbolic time-like face,1,hyperbolic space-like.\tau_{d}=\mathrm{i}\omega\,\alpha_{\text{tune}}\,\eta_{\boldsymbol{n}}\times\begin{dcases}-1,&\quad\text{ elliptic or hyperbolic time-like face},\\
1,&\quad\text{ hyperbolic space-like}\,.\quad\end{dcases}(5.66)
- –

Convection-type: Note thatτc=0\tau_{c}=0whenη𝜷<0\eta_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}<0andτc=2​|η𝜷|\tau_{c}=2\lvert\eta_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\rvertwhenη𝜷>0\eta_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}>0.
Both cases can happen on each region, depending on the sign ofnr​β1n_{r}\beta_{1}.
The total contribution from the convective-type stabilization inEq.5.57is thusτc−η𝜷=|η𝜷|=η𝜷×{−sgn​(nr​β1),elliptic,sgn​(nr​β1),hyperbolic.\tau_{c}-\eta_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\lvert\eta_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\rvert=\eta_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\times\begin{dcases}-\mathrm{sgn}\,(n_{r}\beta_{1}),&\quad\text{ elliptic},\\
\mathrm{sgn}\,(n_{r}\beta_{1}),&\quad\text{ hyperbolic }.\end{dcases}(5.67)

## Remark 16.

We note that solar gravity affects differently the numerical resolution in 1.5D
than in 2.5D although within the same perspective of discretization (HDG) method.
For the same radially symmetric background, the current working equations lead to a different 1.5D problem having as variables, radial displacement and pressure perturbation,
in contrast to the 1.5D system considered in[48]which works with radial displacement and its derivative.
The interior singularity (in the context of scalar ODE theory) encountered in[48]as analyzed in[4]is related to the Lamb frequencies,
cf. discussion in[48,4]. Secondly,
the 1.5D problem can employ mesh refinement more freely than in 2.5D,
which allows for larger leeway with HDG stabilization.
We note that[49]studies effects of gravity however in the context of devising absorbing boundary conditions.

## 6Qualitative wavefield comparisons with solar background

In this section we conduct a qualitative investigation of the
behaviour of the axisymmetric HDG solver using the solar
background parameters.
The solar background presents a challenge due to the
decrease of the parameters near-surface, and the non-zero
buoyancy frequency, cf.Subsection6.1.
InSubsection6.2, we compare
the variant formulations introduced inSubsection3.2in terms of amplitudes of the numerical solutions.
Subsequently, we investigate the choice of HDG stabilization inSubsection6.3.
In particular, we emphasize that for frequencies below the cut-off
and a source position in the region𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}(whereN2>ω2N^{2}>\omega^{2})
a propagation of singularity occurs and the choice of stabilization
plays an important role to maintain the accuracy.
Note that such a source position corresponds to the actual
situation for creating helioseismic observables, as detailed inSection7. The numerical experiments are carried out with the
open-source softwareHawen,[19](https://ffaucher.gitlab.io/hawen-website/)
employing direct solver MUMPS,[1]for linear
system resolution. The setups to reproduce the numerical experiments
are provided in the dedicated Zenodo repository,https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.21297984.

## Remark 17(Rescaling).

The model parameters are adimensionalized with respect to a
reference length which is taken to be the solar radiusR⊙R_{\odot}.
The scaled background quantities are then plugged into the
equations,[17],[4, Section 2].
By denoting with subscriptS\mathrm{S}the unscaled solar
background models, we have the scaled quantities as follow,[17,4]ρ0:=ρ0S;c0:=c0S/R⊙;Φ0=Φ0S/R⊙2;p0=p0S/R⊙2.\displaystyle\rho_{0}\,:=\,\rho_{0}^{\mathrm{S}}\,;\hskip 20.00003ptc_{0}\,:=\,c_{0}^{\mathrm{S}}/R_{\odot}\,;\hskip 20.00003pt\Phi_{0}\,=\,\Phi_{0}^{\mathrm{S}}/R_{\odot}^{2}\,;\hskip 20.00003ptp_{0}\,=\,p_{0}^{\mathrm{S}}/R_{\odot}^{2}\,.(6.1a)

Other quantities such as rotation and frequency remain
in their original units. Therefore, in the numerical
experiments the scaled positionr=1r=1corresponds to the
actual solar positionR⊙=696 000kmR_{\odot}=$696\,000$$\mathrm{km}$.

## 6.1Solar background radial model

InFigure3, we show the radial solar
background models that correspond to the standardmodel-Sin the interior,[12], and extension
in the atmosphere,[17].
The densityρ0\rho_{0}is further plotted on a logarithmic axis
which highlights its drastic decrease, with about twelve
orders of magnitude, and the different Sun’s layer are indicated.
InFigure4, the
buoyancy (or Brunt-Väisälä) frequencyEq.3.27is plotted together with
Lamb frequencies for a few harmonic degreesℓ\ell.
Using the radial backgrounds, the buoyancy
frequencyN2N^{2}is readily used to define
regions𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}and𝔹ell\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}ofEq.3.49.
Note that we picture the ordinary frequency
(i.e.,Re​(N)/(2​π)\mathrm{Re}(N)/(2\pi)) inFigure4aandN2N^{2}inFigure4b.Figure 3:Radial solar background models for the densityρ0\rho_{0}(top),
wave-speedc0c_{0}(bottom left) and
gravity potentialϕ0′\phi_{0}^{\prime}(bottom right)
corresponding to the standardmodel-S,[12,17](all units in SI).
The plot of the density also highlights the different
layers of the Sun.
The positionr=1r=1corresponds to the solar
radiusR⊙R_{\odot}, numerical experiments are

The horizontal dashed lines indicate the three representative

The right panel showsN2N^{2}crossing withω2\omega^{2}near the
surface at 0.2mHz\mathrm{mHz}(inr=r=0.999 9490.999\,949) and
at 3mHz\mathrm{mHz}(inr=r=0.999 999 50.999\,999\,5).

Regarding the solar buoyancy frequency, we see that:
- –

N2N^{2}becomes negative close to the surface, as depicted
inFigure4b,
leading to convective instabilities, a difficulty for time-domain simulations[57,46].
- –

The buoyancy frequency is positive in the interior,
up until a maximum of0.46640.4664mHz\mathrm{mHz},
which corresponds to the maximal frequency at which internal
gravity waves can be excited,[49].

In the experiments of this section, we will focus on three frequencies:0.20.2,33and66mHz\mathrm{mHz}. These are representative
of the different configurations betweenω2\omega^{2}andN2N^{2}:
- –

For frequencies above the cut-off
(∼5.2mHz\sim$5.2$$\mathrm{mHz}$,[49])N2<ω2N^{2}<\omega^{2}for allrr. Therefore, in this case𝔹ell\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}corresponds to the entire domain and𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}is empty,Eq.3.49.
- –

For frequencies below the cut-off, there is at least one
portion of the domain whereN2>ω2N^{2}>\omega^{2}, which means
that𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}is not empty,Eq.3.49.
We further distinguish two situations:
- –

For frequencies above0.46640.4664mHz\mathrm{mHz}𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}corresponds only to an area stating in the
near-surface/solar atmosphere. For instance at
3mHz\mathrm{mHz},𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}corresponds to the zoner>0.999 999 5r>$0.999\,999\,5$,Figure4b.
This corresponds to about350350m\mathrm{m}below
the surface.
- –

For frequencies below0.46640.4664mHz\mathrm{mHz}𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}is also non-empty in the interior, with
at least three crossings betweenN2N^{2}andω2\omega^{2},Figure4a.
For instance at 0.20mHz\mathrm{mHz},𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}corresponds to0.022 606<r<0.659 452$0.022\,606$<r<$0.659\,452$andr>0.999 949r>$0.999\,949$(35.535.5km\mathrm{km}below surface).

Furthermore, a non-zero attenuationγatt\gamma_{\mathrm{att}}is always used to avoid the coefficient𝑨∙\boldsymbol{A}_{\bullet}ofEq.1.1to be zero at the position whereN2=ω2N^{2}=\omega^{2}.
Specifically, constant attenuationγatt=10​µ​Hz\gamma_{\mathrm{att}}=10$\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro Hz}$is used for experiments at 6 and 3mHz\mathrm{mHz}below,
whileγatt=2​µ​Hz\gamma_{\mathrm{att}}=2$\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro Hz}$is used
at0.20.2mHz\mathrm{mHz}.
Despite having non-zero attenuation, in the following
we still refer to𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}and𝔹ell\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}according to their definitionEq.3.49.

For solar applications, the main source of excitation of acoustic waves is due to granulation and the source height is thus located a few hundred kms below the surface. The receiver height corresponds to the observation height which slightly depends on the position on the disk and the instrument but is located around 100-200 kilometers above the surface.
To generate helioseismic observables inSection7,
we choosersrc=0.999 86r_{\mathrm{src}}=$0.999\,86$(about 97km\mathrm{km}below surface) andrrcv=1.000 21r_{\mathrm{rcv}}=$1.000\,21$(about 146km\mathrm{km}above surface).

## Discretization meshes

The discretization meshes are created by first imposing the
radial layers, using the wavelength computed from the solar
background and potential associated to the spherical wave
equation[4].
Next, the tangential edge size is selected to be
similar to the layer size. Once the set of node is fixed,
the softwareTriangle121212www.cs.cmu.edu/~quake/triangle.html[58]is used to assemble the mesh.
The meshes are thus more and more refined near the surface according
to the variation of the background parameters and reduction of the wavelength.
In the numerical experiments, two choices of maximal
radiusrmaxr_{\max}are used to investigate the effect of
the position of the outer boundary, withrmax=1.001r_{\max}=$1.001$andrmax=1.01r_{\max}=$1.01$. These correspond to about696696and69606960km\mathrm{km}above surface, respectively.
- –

rmax=1.001r_{\max}=$1.001$; three meshes used:
- –

Mesh30k001{}^{001}_{\text{30k}}with24 94924\,949cells, used for the
low-frequency0.20.2mHz\mathrm{mHz}.
- –

Mesh175k001{}^{001}_{\text{175k}}with175 919175\,919cells, it is
the main mesh used in simulations, created
such that there are at least 10 points per
wavelength at frequency 6mHz\mathrm{mHz}with polynomial order 4.
- –

Mesh600k001{}^{001}_{\text{600k}}with594 350594\,350cells is a refined
version ofMesh175k001{}^{001}_{\text{175k}}and is used to obtain reference solutions.
- –

rmax=1.01r_{\max}=$1.01$; the extended domain usesMesh1200k01{}^{01}_{\text{1200k}}that has1 183 7001\,183\,700cells.
It is used to illustrate the effect of
moving the boundary further out, and it
is based uponMesh600k001{}^{001}_{\text{600k}}up until1.0011.001.

## Relative difference

To evaluate the accuracy in the absence of analytic solution,
we introduce the relative difference𝔢\mathfrak{e}between a reference
solutionwrefw_{\mathrm{ref}}and a simulationwτw_{\tau}.
We further introduce quantities𝔢D\mathfrak{e}_{D}and𝔢surf\mathfrak{e}_{\mathrm{surf}}that evaluate
the norm of𝔢\mathfrak{e}on different subdomains. We have,𝔢​(η,z)=|wref​(η,z)−wτ​(η,z)|‖wref‖2,\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}(\eta,z)\,=\,\dfrac{|w_{\mathrm{ref}}(\eta,z)-w_{\tau}(\eta,z)|}{\|w_{\mathrm{ref}}\|_{2}}\,,(6.2)𝔢D=‖𝔢​(η,z)‖,η∈[0:​10−3:rmax],z∈[−rmax:​10−3:rmax]withη2+z2≤rmax;\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}_{D}=\|\mathfrak{e}(\eta,z)\|\,,\quad\text{$\eta\in[0:${10}^{-3}$:r_{\max}]$, $z\in[-r_{\max}:${10}^{-3}$:r_{\max}]$\hskip 15.00002pt with
$\sqrt{\eta^{2}+z^{2}}\leq r_{\max}$}\,;𝔢surf=‖𝔢​(η,z)‖,η∈[0.99:​10−5:rmax],z∈[−0.005:​10−5:0.02]withη2+z2≤rmax.\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}_{\mathrm{surf}}=\|\mathfrak{e}(\eta,z)\|\,,\,\,\text{$\eta\in[0.99:${10}^{-5}$:r_{\max}]$, $z\in[-0.005:${10}^{-5}$:0.02]$ with
$\sqrt{\eta^{2}+z^{2}}\leq r_{\max}$}\,.

InEq.6.2,𝔢D\mathfrak{e}_{D}corresponds to the norm of the
relative difference on the entire computational interval, while𝔢surf\mathfrak{e}_{\mathrm{surf}}focuses on a near-surface area close to the equator, and we use a structured
grid to evaluate the functions.
As the experiments below consider the source on the equator,𝔢surf\mathfrak{e}_{\mathrm{surf}}also
emphasises the relative difference near the source position. However, for
the computation of𝔢surf\mathfrak{e}_{\mathrm{surf}}, we exclude the actual position of the source
in order to avoid instabilities at this position.
In the following,wrefw_{\mathrm{ref}}is typically computed with the
refined meshMesh600k001{}^{001}_{\text{600k}}, and we compare its difference with solutions
computed withMesh175k001{}^{001}_{\text{175k}}.

## 6.2Comparison of formulations

We first compare the different formulations of the equations
that have been introduced, namelyℒLiouville\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville}},ℒLiouville−c\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville-c}}andℒodiv\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{odiv}}, with their coefficients given inSubsection3.2.
Their corresponding unknowns are denoted(𝝃L0,wL0)(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathrm{L0}},w_{\mathrm{L0}}),(𝝃Lc,wLc)(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathrm{Lc}},w_{\mathrm{Lc}}), and(𝝃og,wog)(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathrm{og}},w_{\mathrm{og}})respectively,
with relations given inEq.3.20.
The solutionsw∙w_{\bullet}are pictured inFigure5for
a source positioned in(1,0)(1,0), at azimuthal
modem=0m=0and frequency66mHz\mathrm{mHz}.
Note that at this frequency,𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}is
empty and𝔹ell\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}is the entire domain,
i.e.,N2<ω2N^{2}<\omega^{2}, cf.Figure4.(a)SolutionwL0w_{\mathrm{L0}}solvingℒLiouville\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville}}.(b)SolutionwLcw_{\mathrm{Lc}}solvingℒLiouville−c\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville-c}}.(c)Solutionwogw_{\mathrm{og}}solvingℒodiv\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{odiv}}.(d)Normalized solutions|w∙|/max⁡(|w∙|)|w_{\bullet}|/\max(|w_{\bullet}|)inz=0z=0.Figure 5:Comparison of the solutionsRe​(w∙)\mathrm{Re}(w_{\bullet})for the different
formulations for a source positioned in(1,0)(1,0), at modem=0m=0and frequency66mHz\mathrm{mHz}.
The zoom near the source corresponds to the zone(0.99,1.001)η×(−0.005,0.02)z($0.99$,$1.001$)_{\eta}\times($-0.005$,$0.02$)_{z},
and employs a different scaling for better visualization.

The original formulationℒodiv\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{odiv}}shows strong variation
in amplitude between the center and the surface
with a decrease of 4 orders in magnitude betweenr=0r=0andr=1r=1inFigure5d.
The discrepancy in scale renders difficult the simultaneous
visualization the oscillation patterns in the interior and
outer layers, as seen inFigure5cshowing saturation in the interior but indistinguishable
amplitude at higher height.
The latter affects in particular the near-surface layers
where solar observables are generated, and persists even
in the zoom ofFigure5c.
On the other hand, the Liouville variantsℒLiouville\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville}}andℒLiouville−c\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville-c}}do not suffer from this
contrast in amplitude between the interior and the surface,
with only 2 orders of magnitude difference forℒLiouville−c\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville-c}},
and about 1 forℒLiouville\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville}}.
In view of the whole domain,ℒLiouville\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville}}yields solutions
of more bounded variation compared toℒLiouville−c\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville-c}}.

To supplement these qualitative observations, the condition numbers
of the global HDG matrices corresponding to the different formulations
are given inTable2for frequencies between11and77mHz\mathrm{mHz}. The condition numbers are obtained with the direct
sparse solver MUMPS also employed for resolution, cf.[44].
We see that for both frequencies, the two Liouville variants
yield similar condition numbers, both of which are approximately one order
of magnitude lower than that of the original formulation.
They therefore
improve the conditioning of the global matrix,
and in this way enhance numerical robustness.
On the other hand, we observe a general increase of the condition numbers
with frequencies, except between 5 and 6mHz\mathrm{mHz}where
it decreases. This can be explained by the absence of𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}at frequencies above cut-off, cf.Subsection6.1.
In the rest of our investigation, we only present the results
obtained with the variantℒLiouville−c\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville-c}}.Table 2:Conditioning of the global HDG matrix depending

## 6.3Empirical study of the HDG stabilization

The HDG discretization involves a stabilization parameterτ\tau,Subsection5.5, which is critical for
the accuracy of the method (e.g.,[48]).
Here we use the Liouville-c formulation of the equation
and solves for unknowns(𝝃c,wc)(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{c},w_{c}), such that the
relation for the numerical traces is written as𝝃c​m^=𝝃c​m−τ​(wc​m−λ)​𝒏,HDG Numerical trace relation.\widehat{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{c\,m}}\,=\,\boldsymbol{\xi}_{c\,m}\,-\,\tau\big(w_{c\,m}\,-\,\lambda)\,\boldsymbol{n}\,,\qquad\quad\text{HDG Numerical trace relation.}(6.3)

Following the derivation carried out inSubsection5.5, the
stabilization termτ\tauis of the formτ=|𝑨−1​𝜷1⋅𝒏|+αtune​|𝒏t​𝑨−1​𝒏|,\tau\,=\,|\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}|\,+\,\,\alpha_{\text{tune}}\,|\boldsymbol{n}^{\mathrm{t}}\,\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\,\boldsymbol{n}|\,,(6.4)

withαtune\alpha_{\text{tune}}a scaling factor.
For the heterogeneous solar backgrounds considered, analytical solutions
do not exist, therefore two criteria are set up to decide of an acceptable
stabilization:
(1) The absence of artifact in the solution,
and (2) Comparison with a reference solution based on a refined mesh.

We have performed a comparative study for values ofαtune\alpha_{\text{tune}}in the complex plane, with amplitude±ω​​106\pm\omega${10}^{6}$.
For the sake of conciseness, we do not provide all the choices
of stabilization that have been experimented, and only work
with the following illustrative choices:τa\displaystyle\tau_{\mathrm{a}}=1,\displaystyle\,=\,1\,,(6.5a)τb\displaystyle\tau_{\mathrm{b}}=|𝑨−1​𝜷1⋅𝒏|−​106​i​ω​|𝒏t​𝑨−1​𝒏|,\displaystyle\,=\,|\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}|\,-\,${10}^{6}$\mathrm{i}\omega\,|\boldsymbol{n}^{\mathrm{t}}\,\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\,\boldsymbol{n}|\,,(6.5b)τc\displaystyle\tau_{\mathrm{c}}=|𝑨−1​𝜷1⋅𝒏|+​106​ω​|𝒏t​𝑨−1​𝒏|.\displaystyle\,=\,|\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}|\,+\,${10}^{6}$\omega\,|\boldsymbol{n}^{\mathrm{t}}\,\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\,\boldsymbol{n}|\,.(6.5c)

In particular, we will see that the stabilization is critical mostly when
the source is in or close to𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}, i.e., when there are parts of the
domain in whichN2>ω2N^{2}>\omega^{2}.

With the source close to the surface, we see
waves propagating in the interior, in a similar
pattern for the three positions investigated inFigure7.
We notice the impact of the source position when
we zoom near its position and in𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}.
With the source in𝔹ell\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}(left ofFigure7),
we observe that waves are evanescent in𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}and
thus have small amplitudes. In this case all stabilization
gives comparable results visually, and have similar relative
difference comparing with the refined solution usingMesh600k001{}^{001}_{\text{600k}}.
When the source is near or in𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}(middle and right ofFigure7, respectively),
we observe a propagation of singularity from the source position, which
then reach the surface and is reflected in the layer𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}.
It creates a zigzag pattern of waves reflecting back and forth
in this layer.
We further notice that this pattern is located near the source,
as solutions in the interior look similar for all three sources,
and the zooms in the atmosphere away from the source always
show a smooth pattern (cf. upper right zoom of each panel inFigure7).

The relative difference ofFigure8bconfirms our observations:
The solutions are accurate for all choices of stabilizations in
the interior𝔹ell\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}, as well as away from the source in𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}.
The difference is mostly located near the source area, more
specifically restricted to the region𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}.
This zone of𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}is not accurate forτa\tau_{\mathrm{a}}andτc\tau_{\mathrm{c}}, and the
difference is only on the rays of the zigzag pattern forτb\tau_{\mathrm{b}}.
Therefore, when the source is positioned close (or in)𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}},
waves are reflected back and forth in the solar atmosphere in a
zigzag pattern; This effect ismostly localized in𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}and
near the source position: the solution in the interior or in the
region further away from the source do not have this pattern as
waves are rapidly attenuated from the source position.
In addition, this effect of propagation of singularity is not
triggered when the source is in𝔹ell\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}.

The solutions at different heights inFigure9show that the difference is mostly located near the source position, in the first few degrees.
the difference increases with the height of the receivers, and is stronger for the source
in(1,0)(1,0)compared to the source in(0.99986,0)(0.99986,0), as expected from the zoom provided
inFigure7.
Comparing the stabilization, we see that the difference withτb\tau_{\mathrm{b}}is
smaller thanτ\tau, especially for the source in(1,0)(1,0)and the highest
heights. Nonetheless, the difference is concentrated on the first few degrees
only, for positions below 2°\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}.
