## 6.4Ignoring gravity effects

To ignore the effects of gravity in the problem, one can
take the gradient of the gravity potential to zero (∇Φ0=0\nabla\Phi_{0}=0)
in the equations.
This assumption is reasonable when one
focuses on the solar acoustic p-modes, cf.[3].
In this case, the buoyancy frequencyN2N^{2}defined inEq.3.27is zero, hence𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}is empty.
Furthermore, the vacuum boundary conditionEq.2.63simplifies
to a Dirichlet boundary conditionδpE=0\delta_{p}^{\mathrm{E}}=0.
InFigure12, we picture the solutions with
and without gravity effects, for frequencies33and66mHz\mathrm{mHz}.
The source is positioned in(0.999 86,0)($0.999\,86$,0)and
the attenuation is set to 10µ​Hz\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro Hz}.
We also compare the solutions at the heightr=1.000 21r=$1.000\,21$.(a)3mHz\mathrm{mHz}solutions
without (left)
and with (right) gravity.(b)6mHz\mathrm{mHz}solutions
without (left)
and with (right) gravity.(c)Comparisons of the solutions at heightr=1.000 21r=$1.000\,21$for the cases without and with gravity at
3mHz\mathrm{mHz}(left) and ;
6mHz\mathrm{mHz}(right).
For 3mHz\mathrm{mHz}, we show from 0 to 15°\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}, but
separate the case without gravity (top left) which
has very small amplitude compared to the case with (bottom left).
For 6mHz\mathrm{mHz}, we shown separately for angles
from 0 to 3.5°\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}(top right)
and 5 to 9°\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}(bottom right).Figure 12:Comparisons of the solutions with and without
gravity effects for a source positioned in(0.999 86,0)($0.999\,86$,0).
In the case without gravity,N2=0N^{2}=0and𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}is always empty.
The solutions on the entire domain are
complemented with a zoom near source
position on interval(0.9975,1.001)×(−0.005,0.01)($0.9975$,$1.001$)\times(-$0.005$,$0.01$),
and a zoom in the area(0.705,0.71)2(0.705,0.71)^{2}.
The attenuation is set to 10µ​Hz\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro Hz}.
The white dashed line on the zoom corresponds
to the heightr=1.00021r=1.00021.

We see that the solutions are very different between the cases
with and without gravity. For the frequency below cut-off, 3mHz\mathrm{mHz},
we do not have the multiple reflections back and forth as𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}is empty
in the case without gravity. For the frequency 6mHz\mathrm{mHz},
we also see a different pattern in the region
close to the surface: ignoring the gravity, the waves decrease in
amplitude rapidly while when considering the gravity, we see waves
propagating in this region and particularly small wavelength.
We also notice that the amplitudes of the wavefields are different.
Consequently, the signals are hardly comparable at
the height1.000 211.000\,21(Figure12c)
with both amplitude and phase being different.
This experiment illustrates that incorporating the gravity induces drastic
changes in the pattern of the solutions, in particular near the surface
which is where the helioseismic observables are generated.

## 6.5Summary

In this section, we have shown the behaviour of the wavefield
with solar background for three representative frequencies.
The difficulty of working with solar background is the non-zero
buoyancy frequency, which possibly leads to the existence of
the region𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}.
- –

In the case where the frequency is above cut-off frequency,𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}is empty and the solutions are stable with respect
to the choice of the HDG stabilization, as depicted inFigure6.
- –

For frequencies below cut-off,𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}is not empty.
The behaviour of the solution is affected
by the position of the source:
if the source is in𝔹ell\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}, the solutions are evanescent
in𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}, and the HDG stabilizations behave well
(left ofFigure7).
If the source is in𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}(or even close to),
propagation of singularity arises, with back and forth
reflections in the region𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}in a zigzag pattern.
In this case the choice of stabilization plays a role
in avoiding artifacts near the source position
(right of7).
- –

Ignoring the gravity effects, the buoyancy frequency
is zero in the entire domain, hence𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}is empty.
This avoids the propagation of singularity for frequencies
below cut-off. However, ignoring gravity drastically
changes the solutions, which are not comparable in terms
of phase and amplitude,Figure12.

In the context of helioseismology, the sources and receivers
are taken near the surface and frequencies below cut-off are
studied. This corresponds precisely to the zone of𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}.
Note that here we have only shown the results for the azimuthal
modem=0m=0, but the behaviour is the same for non-zero azimuthal
modes, which are not included for the sake of conciseness.

## Remark 18(Including rotation and differential flow).

Due to the small values of the solar rotation, belowµ​Hz\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro Hz},
there are little visual differences in the solutions when incorporating
the rotation for the frequencies used above. However, these differences
are important as they allow to infer the rotation,
cf.Sections7and15.
Differences may be expected when studying extremely
low frequencies with magnitude comparable to the solar
rotation, which is the case for inertial modes[26].
Nonetheless, this is out of the scope of the current paper
and it will be the focus of future works.

## 7Comparisons with helioseismic products

The objective of this section is to validate our
axisymmetric solver by comparing with synthetic
helioseismic data. We first derive inSubsection7.1expressions to compute synthetic
helioseismic products, such as power spectraEq.7.16and expected values of cross-covarianceEq.7.22from the azimuthal Green’s kernels.
We illustrate with the synthetic power spectrum associated
with the standard solar model inSubsection7.2,
and compare different models of solar rotation inSubsection7.3.
We will show that the synthetic power spectrum for standardmodel-Scontains peaks of energy that are in agreement with observations, and
with the eigenfrequencies computed with softwareGyreinSubsection7.2.
The power spectrum computed from the solar background containing rotation
is shown to produce the observed effects on p-modes inSubsection7.3,
namely a shift in the p-mode depending on the azimuthal ordermm.
Since the axisymmetric solver constructed in this work takes into account gravity
effect and allows for uniform and differential rotation, they provide
more realistic simulations of the helioseismic products, compared to the
1.5D solvers that ignore rotation in[3,49]and the axisymmetric formulation that ignore gravity in[22].

## 7.1Derivation of synthetic helioseismic data

Helioseismic data correspond to line-of-sight Doppler
velocities and are obtained from instruments on board of
satellites (such as the Helioseismic and Magnetic Imager, HMI,
of the Solar Dynamics Observatory mission), or from
ground-based telescopes.
Here we show how to model helioseismic data with the azimuthal
Green’s kernelGpmG_{p}^{m}givenDefinition3, solved
with the HDG discretization method inSection5.

## Synthetic Doppler signal

In this work, in assuming single-height

## Azimuthal Green’s kernel

RecallIdentity6which states the cylindrical expansion ofGp​(𝐫,𝐬;ω)G_{p}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s};\omega)with coefficients given by azimuthal Green’s kernelGpmG^{m}_{p},Gp​(𝐱,𝐬;ω)=12​π​∑m∈ℤGpm​(𝐱~,𝐬~′;ω)​ei​m​(ϕ−ϕ′),𝐬∉z-axis.G_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s};\omega)=\dfrac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}G^{m}_{p}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}},\tilde{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime};\omega)\,e^{\mathrm{i}m(\phi-\phi^{\prime})},\quad\mathbf{s}\notin\text{ z-axis}.(7.5)

When the position of the Dirac source is on the axis, there is only the term associated withm=0m=0in the above summand,Gp​(𝐱,𝐬;ω)=12​π​Gp0​(𝐱~,0,z′;ω),𝐬∈z-axis.G_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s};\omega)=\dfrac{1}{2\pi}G^{0}_{p}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}},0,z^{\prime};\omega),\hskip 28.45274pt\mathbf{s}\in\text{ z-axis}.(7.6)

variantℒLiouville−c\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Liouville-c}}, which yieldsGcmG_{c}^{m}related toGpmG^{m}_{p}byGpm​(η,z,η′,z′)=Gcm​(η,z,η′,z′)​c0​(η,z)​ρ0​(η,z)​c0​(ηsrc,zsrc)​ρ0​(ηsrc,zsrc).G_{p}^{m}(\eta,z,\eta^{\prime},z^{\prime})\,=\,G_{c}^{m}(\eta,z,\eta^{\prime},z^{\prime})\,\,c_{0}(\eta,z)\sqrt{\rho_{0}(\eta,z)}\,\,c_{0}(\eta_{\mathrm{src}},z_{\mathrm{src}})\sqrt{\rho_{0}(\eta_{\mathrm{src}},z_{\mathrm{src}})}.(7.7)

We denote the representation of the source𝐬\mathbf{s}in cylindrical and spherical coordinates by𝐬↔(η′,z′,ϕ′)↔(s,θ′,ϕ′)whereη′=scos(θ′),z′=ssin(θ′).\mathbf{s}\leftrightarrow(\eta^{\prime},z^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\leftrightarrow(s,\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\quad\text{ where }\eta^{\prime}=s\cos(\theta^{\prime}),\quad z^{\prime}=s\sin(\theta^{\prime})\,.(7.9)

From the definitions of𝒦pl​m\mathcal{K}_{p}^{lm}Eq.7.4and spherical harmonicsYℓm​(θ,ϕ)=c​(ℓ,m)​Pℓ|m|​(cos⁡θ)​ei​m​ϕY^{m}_{\ell}(\theta,\phi)=c(\ell,m)P^{\lvert m\rvert}_{\ell}(\cos\theta)e^{\mathrm{i}m\phi},
we have,𝒦pl​m(robs,𝐬;ω)=𝒦pl​m(robs,;ω)=𝒦pl​m(robs,s,θ′,ϕ′;ω)\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{p}^{lm}(r_{\rm obs},\mathbf{s};\omega)=\mathcal{K}_{p}^{lm}(r_{\rm obs},;\omega)=\mathcal{K}_{p}^{lm}(r_{\rm obs},s,\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime};\omega)=∫02​π∫0πGp​(robs,θobs,ϕobs,𝐬;ω)​Ylm(θobs,ϕobs)¯​sin⁡θobs​d​θobs​d​ϕobs\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}G_{p}(r_{\rm obs},\theta_{\rm obs},\phi_{\rm obs},\mathbf{s};\omega)\,\overline{Y_{l}^{m}(\theta_{\rm obs},\phi_{\rm obs)}}\,\sin\theta_{\rm obs}\mathrm{d}\theta_{\rm obs}\mathrm{d}\phi_{\rm obs}=12​π​∫02​π∫0π(∑m′Gpm′​(robs,θobs,η′,z′;ω)​ei​m′​(ϕobs−ϕ′))​c​(ℓ,m)​Pℓ|m|​(cos⁡θobs)¯​e−i​m​ϕobs​dϕobs​sin⁡θobs​d​θobs\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\hskip-5.69046pt\int_{0}^{\pi}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sum_{m^{\prime}}G^{m^{\prime}}_{p}(r_{\rm obs},\theta_{\rm obs},\eta^{\prime},z^{\prime};\omega)e^{\mathrm{i}m^{\prime}(\phi_{\rm obs}-\phi^{\prime})}\right)c(\ell,m)\overline{P^{\lvert m\rvert}_{\ell}(\cos\theta_{\rm obs})}e^{-\mathrm{i}m\phi_{\rm obs}}\,\mathrm{d}\phi_{\rm obs}\sin\theta_{\rm obs}\mathrm{d}\theta_{\rm obs}=c​(ℓ,m)​e−i​m​ϕ′​∫0πGp−m​(robs,θobs,η′,z′;ω)​Pℓ|m|​(cos⁡θobs)¯​sin⁡θobs​d​θobs.\displaystyle=c(\ell,m)e^{-\mathrm{i}m\phi^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{\pi}G^{-m}_{p}(r_{\rm obs},\theta_{\rm obs},\eta^{\prime},z^{\prime};\omega)\,\overline{P^{\lvert m\rvert}_{\ell}(\cos\theta_{\rm obs})}\sin\theta_{\rm obs}\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{\rm obs}\,.

We thus obtain the following result.

## Identity 7.

The Green’s kernel corresponding to synthetic observablesEq.7.4is related to the azimuthal Green’s kernel as𝒦pl​m​(robs,s,θ′,ϕ′;ω)=e−i​m​ϕ​𝒦~pl​m​(robs,s,θ′;ω).\mathcal{K}_{p}^{lm}(r_{\rm obs},s,\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime};\omega)=e^{-\mathrm{i}m\phi}\,\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{p}^{lm}(r_{\rm obs},s,\theta^{\prime};\omega)\,.(7.10)

where the quantity𝒦~pl​m\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{p}^{lm}defined as𝒦~pl​m​(robs,s,θ′;ω)=c​(ℓ,m)​∫0πGp−m​(robs,θobs,η′,z′;ω)​Pℓ|m|​(cos⁡θobs)¯​sin⁡θobs​d​θobs\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{p}^{lm}(r_{\rm obs},s,\theta^{\prime};\omega)=c(\ell,m)\int_{0}^{\pi}G^{-m}_{p}(r_{\rm obs},\theta_{\rm obs},\eta^{\prime},z^{\prime};\omega)\,\,\,\overline{P^{\lvert m\rvert}_{\ell}(\cos\theta_{\rm obs})}\sin\theta_{\rm obs}\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{\rm obs}(7.11)

is independent ofϕ′\phi^{\prime}.
We also have the independence of|𝒦pl​m|2\lvert\mathcal{K}_{p}^{lm}\rvert^{2}with respect toϕ′\phi^{\prime}:|𝒦pl​m​(robs,s,θ′,ϕ′;ω)|2=|𝒦~pl​m​(robs,s,θ′;ω)|2.\lvert\mathcal{K}_{p}^{lm}(r_{\rm obs},s,\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime};\omega)\rvert^{2}=\lvert\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{p}^{lm}(r_{\rm obs},s,\theta^{\prime};\omega)\rvert^{2}.(7.12)

## 7.1.1Synthetic power spectrum

The power spectrum is defined with the expected
value of the componentsψl​m\psi^{lm}such that:𝒫lm​(ω):=𝔼​[|ψl​m​(ω)|2].\mathcal{P}_{l}^{m}(\omega):=\mathbb{E}[|\psi^{lm}(\omega)|^{2}].(7.13)

We further assume that the random (realization) sources
are spatially uncorrelated and from a single depthrsrcr_{\rm src}such that𝔼​[s​(𝐫,ω)​s​(𝐫′,ω)¯]=S​(ω)​δ​(𝐫−𝐫′)​δ​(r−rsrc),\mathbb{E}[s(\mathbf{r},\omega)\overline{s(\mathbf{r}^{\prime},\omega)}]=S(\omega)\delta(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime})\delta(r-r_{\mathrm{src}}),(7.14)

whereSSrepresents the power distribution of the sources with frequencies.
In this case, in terms of the quantities𝒦~pl​m\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{p}^{lm}fromEq.7.11, the power spectrum becomes𝒫lm​(ω)\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{l}^{m}(\omega)=∫𝔹⊙∫𝔹⊙𝒦pl​m​(robs,𝐫,ω)​𝒦pl​m​(robs,𝐫′,ω)¯​𝔼​[s​(𝐫)​s​(𝐫′)¯]​d𝐫​d𝐫′\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\odot}}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\odot}}\mathcal{K}_{p}^{lm}(r_{\mathrm{obs}},\mathbf{r},\omega)\overline{\mathcal{K}_{p}^{lm}(r_{\mathrm{obs}},\mathbf{r}^{\prime},\omega)}\mathbb{E}[s(\mathbf{r})\overline{s(\mathbf{r}^{\prime})}]\mathrm{d}\mathbf{r}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{r}^{\prime}(7.15)=∫𝔹⊙|𝒦pl​m​(robs,𝐫,ω)|2​𝔼​[s​(𝐫)​s​(𝐫)¯]​d𝐫\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\odot}}|\mathcal{K}_{p}^{lm}(r_{\mathrm{obs}},\mathbf{r},\omega)|^{2}\mathbb{E}[s(\mathbf{r})\overline{s(\mathbf{r})}]\mathrm{d}\mathbf{r}=S​(ω)​∫𝕊2|𝒦pl​m​(robs,rsrc,𝐫^,ω)|2​d𝐫^\displaystyle=S(\omega)\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|\mathcal{K}_{p}^{lm}(r_{\mathrm{obs}},r_{\mathrm{src}},\hat{\mathbf{r}},\omega)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{r}}=S​(ω)​∫02​π∫0π|𝒦pl​m​(robs,rsrc,θ,ϕ,ω)|2​sin⁡θ​d​θ​d​ϕ.\displaystyle=S(\omega)\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}|\mathcal{K}_{p}^{lm}(r_{\mathrm{obs}},r_{\mathrm{src}},\theta,\phi,\omega)|^{2}\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\phi.

By applyingEq.7.12, we obtain𝒫lm​(ω)=2​π​S​(ω)​∫0π|𝒦~pl​m​(robs,rsrc,θ,ω)|2​sin⁡θ​d​θ.\mathcal{P}_{l}^{m}(\omega)=2\pi S(\omega)\int_{0}^{\pi}|\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{p}^{lm}(r_{\mathrm{obs}},r_{\mathrm{src}},\theta,\omega)|^{2}\sin\theta\mathrm{d}\theta.(7.16)

Therefore, for every azimuthal modemm, the power spectrum
can be computed from the simulations for sources at fixedrsrcr_{\mathrm{src}}and at manyθ\thetalocations to perform the integration. We will see inSubsection7.3that the dependency of the power spectrum on the azimuthal ordermmis directly related to the rotation.

## 7.1.2Synthetic cross-covariance

In addition to the power spectrumEq.7.16,
another useful helioseismic product is the cross-covariance
between the wavefields at two positions𝐱^1\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1}and𝐱^2\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2}on the visible disk.
It is typically computed in the frequency domain asC​(𝐱^1,𝐱^2,ω)=ψ​(𝐱^1,ω)​ψ​(𝐱^2,ω)¯.C(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2},\omega)=\psi(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\omega)\overline{\psi(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2},\omega)}.(7.17)

The expected value𝒞\mathcal{C}of the cross-covariance is given by𝒞​(𝐱^1,𝐱^2,ω)\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2},\omega)=𝔼​[ψ​(𝐱^1,ω)​ψ​(𝐱^2,ω)¯]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\psi(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\omega)\overline{\psi(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2},\omega)}](7.18)=∫𝔹⊙∫𝔹⊙Gp​(robs,𝐱^1,𝐫,ω)​Gp​(robs,𝐱^2,𝐫′,ω)¯​𝔼​[s​(𝐫)​s​(𝐫′)¯]​d𝐫​d𝐫′\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\odot}}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\odot}}G_{p}(r_{\mathrm{obs}},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\mathbf{r},\omega)\overline{G_{p}(r_{\mathrm{obs}},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2},\mathbf{r}^{\prime},\omega)}\mathbb{E}[s(\mathbf{r})\overline{s(\mathbf{r}^{\prime})}]\mathrm{d}\mathbf{r}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{r}^{\prime}(7.19)=∫VGp​(robs,𝐱^1,𝐫,ω)​Gp​(robs,𝐱^2,𝐫,ω)¯​𝔼​[s​(𝐫)​s​(𝐫)¯]​d𝐫.\displaystyle=\int_{V}G_{p}(r_{\mathrm{obs}},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\mathbf{r},\omega)\overline{G_{p}(r_{\mathrm{obs}},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2},\mathbf{r},\omega)}\mathbb{E}[s(\mathbf{r})\overline{s(\mathbf{r})}]\mathrm{d}\mathbf{r}.(7.20)

Writing the decomposition ofGpG_{p}fromIdentity6,Gp​(robs,𝐱^1,𝐫,ω)=12​π​∑mGpm​(robs,θ1,r,θ,ω)​ei​m​(ϕ1−ϕ),G_{p}(r_{\mathrm{obs}},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\mathbf{r},\omega)=\dfrac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{m}G_{p}^{m}(r_{\mathrm{obs}},\theta_{1},r,\theta,\omega)\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}m(\phi_{1}-\phi)},(7.21)

we obtain𝒞​(𝐱^1,𝐱^2,ω)=S​(ω)2​π​∑mei​m​(ϕ1−ϕ2)​∫0πGpm​(robs,θ1,rsrc,θ,ω)​Gpm​(robs,θ2,rsrc,θ,ω)¯​sin⁡θ​d​θ.\mathcal{C}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2},\omega)=\dfrac{S(\omega)}{2\pi}\sum_{m}\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}m(\phi_{1}-\phi_{2})}\int_{0}^{\pi}G_{p}^{m}(r_{\mathrm{obs}},\theta_{1},r_{\mathrm{src}},\theta,\omega)\overline{G_{p}^{m}(r_{\mathrm{obs}},\theta_{2},r_{\mathrm{src}},\theta,\omega)}\sin\theta\mathrm{d}\theta.(7.22)

## 7.1.3Simplification for the case without rotation

Without rotation, it is convenient to work in the spherical harmonic basis. In terms of the quantity introduced inEq.7.4, the Green’s function for a source at depthrsrcr_{\rm src}and horizontal position𝐱^s=(θs,ϕs)\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{s}=(\theta_{s},\phi_{s})(or equivalently(ηs,zs)(\eta_{s},z_{s})) is written as,Gp​(robs,𝐱^1,rsrc,𝐱^s,ω)=∑ℓ=0∞∑m=−ℓℓ𝒦pℓ​m​(robs,rsrc,𝐱^s,ω)​Yℓm​(θ1,ϕ1).G_{p}(r_{\rm obs},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},r_{\rm src},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{s},\omega)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\mathcal{K}_{p}^{\ell m}(r_{\rm obs},r_{\rm src},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{s},\omega)Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta_{1},\phi_{1}).(7.23)

If there is no background flow, then the Green’s function for a source located at𝐱^0=(0,zsrc)\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{0}=(0,z_{\rm src})on thez−z-axis depends only on the modem=0m=0and can be written asGp​(robs,𝐱^1,rsrc,𝐱^0,ω)=12​π​∑ℓ=0∞𝒦pℓ​0​(robs,rsrc,𝐱^0,ω)​Pℓ​(cos⁡θ1),G_{p}(r_{\rm obs},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},r_{\rm src},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{0},\omega)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{K}_{p}^{\ell 0}(r_{\rm obs},r_{\rm src},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{0},\omega)P_{\ell}(\cos\theta_{1}),(7.24)

wherePℓP_{\ell}represents the Legendre polynomial of degreeℓ\ell.

Using the rotation formula of spherical harmonics,𝒦pℓ​m​(robs,rsrc,𝐱^s,ω)\mathcal{K}_{p}^{\ell m}(r_{\rm obs},r_{\rm src},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{s},\omega)can be written as a function of𝒦pℓ​0\mathcal{K}_{p}^{\ell 0}such that𝒦pℓ​m​(robs,rsrc,𝐱^s,ω)=4​π2​ℓ+1​𝒦pℓ​0​(robs,rsrc,𝐱^0,ω)​Yℓm​(θs,ϕs).\mathcal{K}_{p}^{\ell m}(r_{\rm obs},r_{\rm src},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{s},\omega)=\sqrt{\frac{4\pi}{2\ell+1}}\mathcal{K}_{p}^{\ell 0}(r_{\rm obs},r_{\rm src},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{0},\omega)Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta_{s},\phi_{s}).(7.25)

InsertingEq.7.25intoEq.7.23leads toGp​(robs,𝐱^1,rsrc,𝐱^s,ω)=∑l=0∞𝒦pl​0​(robs,rsrc,𝐱^0,ω)​4​π2​ℓ+1​∑m=−ℓℓYℓm​(θs,ϕs)​Yℓm​(θ1,ϕ1).G_{p}(r_{\rm obs},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},r_{\rm src},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{s},\omega)=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{K}_{p}^{l0}(r_{\rm obs},r_{\rm src},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{0},\omega)\sqrt{\frac{4\pi}{2\ell+1}}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta_{s},\phi_{s})Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta_{1},\phi_{1}).(7.26)

Using this expression in the expectation value
of the cross-covarianceEq.7.20leads toC¯​(𝐱^1,𝐱^2,ω)\displaystyle\overline{C}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2},\omega)=S​(ω)​∫𝕊2Gp​(robs,𝐱^1,rsrc,𝐱^s,ω)​Gp​(robs,𝐱^2,rsrc,𝐱^s,ω)¯​sin⁡θs​d​θs​d​ϕs,\displaystyle=S(\omega)\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}G_{p}(r_{\rm obs},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},r_{\rm src},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{s},\omega)\overline{G_{p}(r_{\rm obs},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2},r_{\rm src},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{s},\omega)}\sin\theta_{s}d\theta_{s}d\phi_{s},(7.27)=S​(ω)​∑ℓ4​π2​ℓ+1​|𝒦pℓ​0​(robs,rsrc,𝐱^0,ω)|2​∑m=−ℓℓYℓm​(θ1,ϕ1)​Yℓm​(θ2,ϕ2)¯.\displaystyle=S(\omega)\sum_{\ell}\frac{4\pi}{2\ell+1}|\mathcal{K}_{p}^{\ell 0}(r_{\rm obs},r_{\rm src},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{0},\omega)|^{2}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta_{1},\phi_{1})\overline{Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta_{2},\phi_{2})}.(7.28)

Employ the addition theorem of spherical harmonics to the last expression,
we obtain the following identity.

## Identity 8.

For background without flow, the (synthetic) cross-covariance depends only on the angular distance between
the two observations points𝐱^1\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1}and𝐱^2\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2}and is given byC¯​(𝐱^1,𝐱^2,ω)=S​(ω)​∑ℓ=0∞|𝒦pℓ​0​(robs,rsrc,𝐱^0,ω)|2​Pℓ​(𝐱^1⋅𝐱^2).\overline{C}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2},\omega)=S(\omega)\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}|\mathcal{K}_{p}^{\ell 0}(r_{\rm obs},r_{\rm src},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{0},\omega)|^{2}P_{\ell}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2}).(7.29)

In this case, the (synthetic) power spectrumEq.7.16is also independent
ofmmand is given by𝒫ℓm=𝒫ℓ0=2​π​S​(ω)​|𝒦pℓ​0​(robs,rsrc,𝐱^0,ω)|2.\mathcal{P}_{\ell}^{m}=\mathcal{P}_{\ell}^{0}=2\pi S(\omega)|\mathcal{K}_{p}^{\ell 0}(r_{\rm obs},r_{\rm src},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{0},\omega)|^{2}.(7.30)

## Remark 19.

These expressionsEq.7.29andEq.7.30are computationally cheaper than the general expression
of the power spectrum given byEq.7.16andEq.7.22as they require
only one simulation form=0m=0and for a single source (no integration overθ\thetais required).

## 7.2Validation of helioseismic products without rotation

## Power spectrum

We first consider the case without rotation and background flow.
A representation of the power spectrum𝒫l0​(ω)\mathcal{P}_{l}^{0}(\omega)ofEq.7.30is given inFigure13.
Without rotation, it requires the simulation for one source on
thezz-axis141414To avoid having the source where a boundary
condition is imposed on∂Da\partial D^{a}and
avoid numerical
instabilities, the source is implemented
slightly away from thezz-axis in
the simulations, atϵ=​10−12\epsilon=${10}^{-12}$.and only form=0m=0, seeAlgorithm1.
Here we use the source heightrsrc=0.999 86r_{\mathrm{src}}=$0.999\,86$, and
observation height is set torobs=1.000 21r_{\mathrm{obs}}=$1.000\,21$.
In this experiment, the axisymmetric system is solved for
every frequency from 2µ​Hz\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro Hz}to 9mHz\mathrm{mHz}with
step 2µ​Hz\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro Hz}. To compute the integral inIdentity7,18 00118\,001data-points are saved at the receiver height1.000 211.000\,21,
for equipartitioned angles between 0 andπ\pi.
The regions of strong power correspond to the eigenvalues of the forward operator,[3,49]and are in good agreement with the
eigenvalues computed from the eigenvalue solverGyre[60].
Nonetheless, the Green’s function gives us a more complete
representation of the wavefield and contains for example information
about the wave attenuation.(a)Spectrum computed with frequencies
from0.50.5µ​Hz\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro Hz}to 9mHz\mathrm{mHz}and harmonic degreeℓ\ellfrom 0 to200200.
On the right, the ridges are labelled as
f-mode and p-modes.(b)Zoom between frequencies
from 2.9 and 3.1mHz\mathrm{mHz}and harmonic degreeℓ\ellfrom
0 to100100to compare with the eigenvalues
obtained fromGyre(cyan).Figure 13:Power spectrum𝒫l0​(ω)\mathcal{P}_{l}^{0}(\omega)Eq.7.30without rotation (background color)
and comparison with eigenvalues (cyan crosses) computed
with theGyrecode.
The bottom right panels show slices with frequencies at
fixedℓ\ell, with theGyreeigenvalues indicated by the
cyan vertical lines.

## Cross-covariance

The power spectrum is typically used in global helioseismology,
for example to infer the solar differential rotation.
In local helioseismology, the cross-covarianceEq.7.29is
the main observable.
It is the basic ingredient to compute wave travel-times used
in time-distance helioseismology or to compute holograms for
helioseismic holography,[62]. We compute it from the power spectrum
usingEq.7.29and performing a Fourier transform in time.(a)Time-distance diagram showing
all angular positions for𝐱^2\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2}between0and180180°\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}.(b)Corresponding time-series at three different angular position.Figure 14:Time-distance diagram showing the cross-covarianceEq.7.29for𝐱^1\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},𝐱^2\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2}at height1.000 211.000\,21.
The source is positioned on thezz-axis (i.e.,𝐱^1\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1}).

A classical representation used in helioseismology is the
time-distance diagram that represents the cross-covarianceC¯​(𝐱^1,𝐱^2,t)\overline{C}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2},t)as a function of
separation distance between the points𝐱^1\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1}and𝐱^2\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2}and time.
We use a maximum harmonic degree of 200 and, to avoid aliasing,
we multiply the power at eachℓ\ellby a filter[1−tanh​(0.03​ℓ−3)]/2[1-\textrm{tanh}(0.03\ell-3)]/2.
The resulting time-distance diagram is shown inFigure14where we can see the
first skip which is the fastest wave that is travelling directly
from𝐱^1\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1}to𝐱^2\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2}. The second skip corresponds
to waves that reflect once at the surface between𝐱^1\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1}and𝐱^2\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2}and so on. All these skips give information
about different depths and are thus valuable inputs in the
perspective of inversion.

## 7.3Validation of helioseismic products with rotation

In this section, we compute the
synthetic power spectrum obtained for various
rotation profiles listed inSubsection7.3.1.
The most realistic one, called solar rotation, is
inferred from global-mode helioseismology[37].
With rotation incorporated, the power spectrum now depends on
the azimuthal ordermm,Eq.7.16,
and so do the eigenvalues.
Note that these eigenvalues correspond to the position of the
peaks in the power spectrum, as depicted inFigure13without rotation.
InSubsection7.3.2, we show how this effect is quantified,
in terms of the a-coefficientsaja_{j},j∈ℕj\in\mathbb{N},
which are coefficients of a specific polynomial expansions,
cf.Eq.7.43.
For the solar rotation,
we then compare the a-coefficients computed from our numerical power spectrum
with those obtained from observed power spectrum and given in[56].
In this section, we choose a reference rotation frame
with an angular velocityΩc/(2​π)=460nHz\Omega_{c}/(2\pi)=$460$$\mathrm{nHz}$.
To compute the integrals, the simulations of these sections use361361source positions and the solution is saved at18011801receivers.

## 7.3.1Rotation profiles forΩ\Omega

The differential rotationΩ​(r,θ)\Omega(r,\theta)that defines the
background flow in the rotating frame𝐯0\mathbf{v}_{0}(seeEq.3.2) is often
written on a basis function in the angular direction such that,Ω​(r,θ)=∑s=0NsΩs​(r)​ϕs​(θ).\Omega(r,\theta)=\sum_{s=0}^{N_{s}}\Omega_{s}(r)\phi_{s}(\theta).(7.31)

As the variations are relatively smooth with latitude, good approximations can be obtained with a limited number of coefficients. Classical choices areϕs​(θ)=coss⁡(θ)\phi_{s}(\theta)=\cos^{s}(\theta)or derivatives of Legendre polynomials. In this latter case, the first basis functions areϕ0​(x)=1,ϕ2​(x)=−5​x2+1,ϕ4​(x)=21​x4−14​x2+1.\phi_{0}(x)=1,\quad\phi_{2}(x)=-5x^{2}+1,\quad\phi_{4}(x)=21x^{4}-14x^{2}+1.(7.32)

We will writeΩi\Omega_{i}the coefficients in the basis of derivatives of Legendre polynomials andΩ~i\tilde{\Omega}_{i}the ones in the cosine expansion.

## Profiles under consideration

For the numerical tests, we consider three different rotation profiles:
- 1.

Non-rotating SunΩ​(r,θ)=0\Omega(r,\theta)=0which means that𝐯0=−r​sin⁡θ​Ωc​eϕ\mathbf{v}_{0}=-r\sin\theta\Omega_{c}\mathrm{e}_{\phi}(corresponding to the tracking rate).
- 2.

Asimplified rotationprofile depicted inFigure15a, such that,[22],Ω​(r,θ)=Ω~0+Ω~2​cos2⁡θ+Ω~4​cos4⁡θ,for​r>0.7andΩ~c​otherwise,\Omega(r,\theta)=\tilde{\Omega}_{0}+\tilde{\Omega}_{2}\cos^{2}\theta+\tilde{\Omega}_{4}\cos^{4}\theta,\quad\textrm{for }r>0.7\,\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\Omega}_{c}\,\text{ otherwise,}(7.33)

## 7.3.2Effect of rotation on the power spectrum

By incorporating rotation in the equations,
the power spectrum depends on the azimuthal
ordermm,Eq.7.16, and so
do the eigenvalues, that we labelωn​ℓ​m\omega_{n\ell m}with their radialnn, latitudinalℓ\elland
longitudinalmmwavenumbers.
These eigenvalues
represent position of peaks in magnitude of the power spectrum. The extraction
of their value from the power spectrum is discussed below.

## Definition 4.

For a fixednnandℓ\ell,
we define the frequency shiftΔ​ωn​ℓ​m\Delta\omega_{n\ell m}caused by rotation as the difference with
the eigenfrequency form=0m=0, such that,Δ​ωn​ℓ​m:=ωn​ℓ​m−ωn​ℓ​0,−ℓ≤m≤ℓ.\Delta\omega_{n\ell m}:=\omega_{n\ell m}-\omega_{n\ell 0},\quad-\ell\leq m\leq\ell.(7.42)

## Extraction of eigenfrequency from spectrum

To compute the shiftEq.7.42, the eigenfrequencyωn​ℓ​m\omega_{n\ell m}is extracted from the power spectrum𝒫lm​(ω)\mathcal{P}_{l}^{m}(\omega)by fitting a Lorentzian function, as shown inFigure16. This approach is more accurate than using directly the maximum value of the power spectrum whose accuracy is limited by the frequency resolution

## Shift quantification by a-coefficients

To quantify the effect of rotation, the shiftΔ​ωn​ℓ​m\Delta\omega_{n\ell m}Eq.7.42is next represented as,Δ​ωn​ℓ​m≃∑j=1jmaxaj​(n,ℓ)​Πj(ℓ)​(m),−ℓ≤m≤ℓ.\Delta\omega_{n\ell m}\,\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,0}\simeq}\,\,\sum_{j=1}^{j_{\rm max}}a_{j}(n,\ell)\,\Pi_{j}^{(\ell)}(m),\quad-\ell\leq m\leq\ell.(7.43)

At a fixedℓ\ell, this fit is performed for all values ofmmwith|m|≤ℓ\lvert m\rvert\leq\ell. Hereaj​(n,ℓ)a_{j}(n,\ell)are the so-called a-coefficients andΠj(ℓ)\Pi_{j}^{(\ell)}is a polynomial of degreejj. The maximum degreejmaxj_{\rm max}depends on the complexity of the rotation profile and of the noise level in the case of observations. Standard helioseismic inversions usejmax=6,18,j_{\rm max}=6,18,or 36[37].
Here, we usejmax=6j_{\rm max}=6.

For the solar rotationFigure15b, we will compare the numerical a-coefficients (obtained by fitting the synthetic power spectrumEq.7.16withEq.7.43) with the measured one given in[37]:a1/2​π=442.85±0.05​nHz,a3/2​π=22.15±0.08​nHz,a5/2​π=−3.21±0.10​nHz.a_{1}/2\pi=442.85\pm 0.05~\textrm{nHz},\quad a_{3}/2\pi=22.15\pm 0.08~\textrm{nHz},\quad a_{5}/2\pi=-3.21\pm 0.10~\textrm{nHz}.(7.44)

For the case of simplified rotationEq.7.33, the computation
of the a-coefficients is carried out with polynomialsΠ~j(ℓ)\tilde{\Pi}_{j}^{(\ell)}which are equivalent toΠj(ℓ)\Pi_{j}^{(\ell)}at largeℓ\ell, cf.Remark21. In this case, the a-coefficients can be computed analytically as shown inRemark22.

## Remark 21(On the functionΠj\Pi_{j}and their asymptotics).

When measuring these coefficients
on the Sun, the basis functions are chosen such thatΠi(ℓ)​(ℓ)=ℓ,∑m=−ℓℓΠi(ℓ)​(m)​Πj(ℓ)​(m)=0for​i≠j.\Pi_{i}^{(\ell)}(\ell)=\ell,\quad\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\Pi_{i}^{(\ell)}(m)\,\Pi_{j}^{(\ell)}(m)=0\quad\textrm{for }i\neq j.(7.45)

These polynomials are computed recursively starting fromΠ0(ℓ)​(m)=ℓ\Pi_{0}^{(\ell)}(m)=\ell(see Appendix A in[56]). For large values ofℓ\ell, these polynomials are asymptotically equivalent toΠ~j(ℓ)​(m)=L​Pj​(m/L)withL=ℓ​(ℓ+1),\tilde{\Pi}_{j}^{(\ell)}(m)=LP_{j}(m/L)\quad\textrm{with}\quad L=\sqrt{\ell(\ell+1)},(7.46)

wherePjP_{j}is the Legendre polynomial of orderjj.

## Remark 22(Computing a-coefficients for simplified rotation).

For simplified rotationEq.7.33, the fitting is carried out with theΠ~j(ℓ)​(m)\tilde{\Pi}_{j}^{(\ell)}(m)defined inEq.7.46, i.e.Δ​ωn​ℓ​m≃∑j=1jmaxaj​(n,ℓ)​Π~j(ℓ)​(m),for simplified rotationEq.7.33.\Delta\omega_{n\ell m}\,\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\simeq}\,\,\sum_{j=1}^{j_{\rm max}}a_{j}(n,\ell)\,\tilde{\Pi}_{j}^{(\ell)}(m)\,,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{ for simplified rotation \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eq:rotation-v1}.}(7.47)

In this basis, we can relate the a-coefficients
to the rotation coefficientsΩi\Omega_{i},[34],ak​(n,ℓ)=Ωk−1​(1−1In​ℓ​∫0R[2​Un,ℓ​Vn,ℓ+Vn,ℓ2]​ρ0​r2​𝑑r),a_{k}(n,\ell)=\Omega_{k-1}\left(1-\frac{1}{I_{n\ell}}\int_{0}^{R}\left[2U_{n,\ell}V_{n,\ell}+V_{n,\ell}^{2}\right]\rho_{0}r^{2}dr\right),(7.48)

whereUn​ℓU_{n\ell}andVn​ℓV_{n\ell}are the radial and horizontal eigenfunctions of the mode at frequencyωn​ℓ\omega_{n\ell}andIn​ℓI_{n\ell}is the mode mass defined asIn​ℓ=∫0R[Un,ℓ2+ℓ​(ℓ+1)​Vn,ℓ2]​ρ​r2​𝑑r.I_{n\ell}=\int_{0}^{R}\left[U_{n,\ell}^{2}\,+\,\ell(\ell+1)V_{n,\ell}^{2}\right]\rho r^{2}dr.(7.49)

Here, we have used that our simplified rotation profile does not depend on depth. The second term inEq.7.48corresponds to the influence of the Coriolis force and is of order1/L1/L([56]), thus theaja_{j}coefficients give an approximation ofΩj−1\Omega_{j-1}.

For the mode considered here, (ℓ=85\ell=85andn=7n=7), we compute the eigenfunctionsUn,ℓU_{n,\ell}andVn,ℓV_{n,\ell}withGyre[60]and we obtain that the second term inEq.7.48is equal to1.3×10−31.3\times 10^{-3}. We thus expect for the simplified rotation profile (7.33)a1/2​π=435.9​nHz,a3/2​π=21.1​nHz,a5/2​π=−3.6​nHz,a_{1}/2\pi=435.9~\textrm{nHz},\quad a_{3}/2\pi=21.1~\textrm{nHz},\quad a_{5}/2\pi=-3.6~\textrm{nHz},(7.50)

which is obtained by multiplying the input
coefficientsΩj\Omega_{j}by(1−1.3×10−3(1-1.3\times 10^{-3})
followingEq.7.48.

## Numerical results

InFigure16, we show the power spectra𝒫lm​(ω)\mathcal{P}_{l}^{m}(\omega)computed forℓ=85\ell=85in the range of frequency (2.9-3.1 mHz) which contains the mode withn=7n=7. Three different rotation profiles listed inSubsection7.3.1are considered: without rotation, with a simplified model of solar rotation, and the profile inferred from global helioseismology.
We remind that in all the cases the tracking rate
is fixed atΩc/2​π=460\Omega_{c}/2\pi=460nHz.
In each case, the location of maximum power on the spectra is different
for eachmmand the shift is compared to the reference frequency ofm=0m=0to recover information about the differential rotation. For eachmm, we fit the power spectrum with a Lorentzian as shown inFigure16ain order to obtain the frequency of maximum powerωn​ℓ​m\omega_{n\ell m}.

We have the following observations:
- –

For the case without rotation,Figure16b, the shift in frequency depends linearly onmmwith slopeΩc\Omega_{c}. We see that the maximal power in the simulations matches perfectly this slope.
- –

For the case with the simplified rotationFigures15aand7.33, the theoretical a-coefficients can be computed analyticallyEq.7.48and the location of maximal power matches very well the polynomialΠ~(ℓ)\tilde{\Pi}^{(\ell)}obtained with these coefficientsFigure16a.
- –

For the rotation inferred by helioseismology[37], the a-coefficients are the input of the inversion procedure. Using these coefficients in the functionΠ(ℓ)\Pi^{(\ell)}, we see that the maximal power in the simulations matches this polynomialFigure16c.

To better estimate the accuracy of our simulations, we fit the numerical power spectra to obtain a-coefficients and compare them to the observations for the solar profile and to the analytic solution for the simplified profile, seeTable3. In both cases, the accuracy is better than 0.03 nHz which is below the noise level in the measurements.a-coeffEq.7.43in (nHz)a1/(2​π)a_{1}/(2\pi)a3/(2​π)a_{3}/(2\pi)a5/(2​π)a_{5}/(2\pi)Solar profileFrom simulation442.8622.17-3.22From observation[37]442.85±\pm0.0522.15±\pm0.08-3.21±\pm0.10Simplified profileFrom simulation435.8921.13-3.63Theoretical435.9021.11-3.61Table 3:Comparison of the a-coefficients obtained by fitting the numerical simulations to the reference obtained analytically for the simplified rotation profile and from the observations for the profile infered by helioseismology.(a)Using the approximate solar rotation profile ofFigure15a.Left: Cut form=11m=11and associated Lorentzian fit to determine

## Remark 23.

This quantitative comparison of the a-coefficients allows us to validate the implementation of rotation in our code and to estimate the numerical accuracy. It is stronger than the qualitative comparison presented in[22]where the measured solar a-coefficients were compared to the ones obtained from the simulations with a simplified rotation profile. A consistent comparison is also performed in[59]between their numerical simulation and the eigenfrequencies computed using first-order perturbation but only a visual comparison is presented.

## 8Conclusion

We have developed a forward solver for the equations of
stellar oscillations with background flow and gravity,
and have validated it in the context of helioseismology.
This is realized with the Hybridizable Discontinuous Galerkin
method, and used to solve the equations for realistic solar backgrounds.
These backgrounds are challenging due to their drastic
variations and the non-zero buoyancy frequency in the case
with gravity, which leads to
a change in the nature of the problem from ellipticity
to hyperbolicity, at zero attenuation and for frequencies below acoustic cut-off.
Despite encoding attenuation, appropriate choices of HDG stabilization and meshing
are required in numerical implementations to maintain accuracy.
It is not only due to the existence of these regions,
but also to the fact that
the formation and observation heights employed in helioseimology
are positioned in the transition zone between the two behaviors.

From the Green’s kernels, we derive helioseismic products such as
the power spectrum. The positions of high power match the
eigenfrequencies obtained independently with the GYRE eigensolver.
Including rotation, the power spectrum depends on the azimuthal
ordermmand the change in the eigenfrequency can be determined
analytically for simple rotation profiles.
The validation presented in this paper has been conducted in the solar context,
but our assumption of azimuthal symmetry allows for the extension of our solver
to other objects such as gas giants and stars, including fast rotators like
Altair, which has recently been found to exhibit non-radial pulsations[54].

The developed solver is a valuable tool for generating synthetic observables,
enabling the study of differential rotation using various helioseismic techniques,
such as time-distance[16]and holography[40].
Moreover, we readily get access to the 3D displacement field as the HDG
method solves the mixed system.
This will allow for more accurate modeling of observables, taking into
account projection and radiative transfer effects[20].
These experiments allow us
to validate our code without and with rotation.
This efficient forward solver also provides a crucial step towards nonlinear
inversions (see, e.g.,[43]for iterative holography).
In addition to the acoustic modes that are traditionally used in
helioseismology, the inclusion of rotation further gives access
to inertial modes such as Rossby modes that have recently been
observed on the Sun[41,26]. These modes have
sensitivity deep inside the Sun and have a potential for better
constraining the base of the convection zone[23].

## Identity 9.

## Gradient of radially symmetric function in cylindrical basis

We will employ radially symmetric parametersϕ0\phi_{0},ρ0\rho_{0}, andp0p_{0}even with background
containing rotation, cf.Remark7. It is useful to write their components in this basis.
We have𝐞r=ηr​𝐞η+zr​𝐞z,𝜶g=αg​(r)​𝐞r⇒[𝜶g]=αg​(r)​(η/r0z/r),\displaystyle\mathbf{e}_{r}=\dfrac{\eta}{r}\mathbf{e}_{\eta}+\dfrac{z}{r}\mathbf{e}_{z},\quad\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{g}=\alpha_{g}(r)\mathbf{e}_{r}\hskip 5.69046pt\Rightarrow\hskip 5.69046pt\left[\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{g}\right]=\alpha_{g}(r)\begin{pmatrix}\eta/r\\
0\\
z/r\end{pmatrix},(A.21)⟹∇ϕ0=∂rϕ0​(η/r0z/r),[𝜶p+𝜶ρΓ1]=(αp+αpΓ1)​(η/r0z/r).\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\hskip 8.5359pt\nabla\phi_{0}=\partial_{r}\phi_{0}\begin{pmatrix}\eta/r\\
0\\
z/r\end{pmatrix},\quad\left[\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}+\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\rho}}{\Gamma_{1}}\right]=\left(\alpha_{p}+\dfrac{\alpha_{p}}{\Gamma_{1}}\right)\begin{pmatrix}\eta/r\\
0\\

## Appendix BInvestigations with synthetic background parameters

In this appendix, we consider synthetic backgrounds to
analyse the numerical behaviour of the solutions when
there are both𝔹ell\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}and𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}within the computational
domain.
We first perform a convergence study of the solver
using a manufactured solution inSubsectionB.1,
and investigate the choice of scaling factor for the HDG
stabilization.
Then, inSubsectionB.2, we consider localized sources
and highlight the phenomenon arising when the
source is close to the interface between𝔹ell\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}and𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}},
called the sonic line151515This terminology follows[47]..
InSubsectionB.3, we further investigate the
numerical behaviour as the attenuation is reduced, although
non-zero attenuation is a key requirement for ensuring well-posedness,
as discussed in the paper.

We consider the wave systemEq.3.24, with radial
coefficients given by𝑨​(r)=−σ2​𝕀​𝕕+N2​(r)​𝐞r⊗𝐞r,withσ2:=ω2+2​i​ω​γatt,\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}(r)\,=\,-\sigma^{2}\mathbb{Id}+N^{2}(r)\,\mathbf{e}_{r}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{r},\qquad\text{with}\qquad\sigma^{2}=\omega^{2}+2\mathrm{i}\omega\gamma_{\mathrm{att}},(B.1)ϱ​(r)=1/c02,with sound speedc0,𝜷1=𝜷2=0.\displaystyle\varrho(r)=1/c_{0}^{2},\text{ with sound speed $c_{0}$}\,,\hskip 70.0001pt\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\,=\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\,=0\,.

In particular, in this case we have,𝑨−1​(r)=−1σ2​𝕀​𝕕+(1N2​(r)−σ2+1σ2)​𝐞r⊗𝐞r.\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}(r)\,=\,-\dfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\mathbb{Id}+\left(\dfrac{1}{N^{2}(r)-\sigma^{2}}+\dfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\right)\mathbf{e}_{r}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{r}\,.(B.2)

## Variation profile forN2N^{2}

The distinction between the regions𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}and𝔹ell\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}is determined
by the sign of(N2−ω2)(N^{2}-\omega^{2}).
To ensure their co-existence, we constructN2N^{2}as
a smooth function so that(N2−ω2)(N^{2}-\omega^{2})ranges between±ω2\pm\omega^{2}.
Furthermore,N2N^{2}is parametrized by the scalar parametersaaandbb, whose difference controls the width of the
transition region. The sonic line is located atr=(a+b)/2r=(a+b)/2, whereN2=ω2N^{2}=\omega^{2}. To satisfy these condition, we chooseN2N^{2}defined on(0,rmax)(0,r_{\max})with0<a<b<rmax0<a<b<r_{\max}, asN2​(r)=2​ω2​χ​(r),whereχ​(r)=12​(1+tanh⁡(r−(a+b)/2(b−a)/6)).N^{2}(r)\,=\,2\omega^{2}\chi(r)\,,\qquad\text{where}\quad\chi(r)\,=\,\dfrac{1}{2}\left(1+\tanh\left(\dfrac{r-(a+b)/2}{(b-a)/6}\right)\right)\,.(B.3)

## B.1Convergence test with a manufactured solution

Given a manufactured solutionwexw_{\mathrm{ex}}, the corresponding right-hand
sidehexh_{\mathrm{ex}}associated withEq.3.24and the background
parameters defined inEq.B.1is radial
and given byhex​(r)=ϱ​wex​(r)+uex′​(r)+2​uex​(r)r,h_{\mathrm{ex}}(r)\,=\,\varrho w_{\mathrm{ex}}(r)\,+\,u_{\mathrm{ex}}^{\prime}(r)+2\dfrac{u_{\mathrm{ex}}(r)}{r}\,,(B.4)

withuex​(r)=−wex′​(r)N2​(r)−σ2,anduex′​(r)=(N2​(r))′(N2​(r)−σ2)2​wex′​(r)−wex′′​(r)N2​(r)−σ2.u_{\mathrm{ex}}(r)\,=\,-\dfrac{w_{\mathrm{ex}}^{\prime}(r)}{N^{2}(r)-\sigma^{2}}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad u_{\mathrm{ex}}^{\prime}(r)\,=\,\dfrac{(N^{2}(r))^{\prime}}{\left(N^{2}(r)-\sigma^{2}\right)^{2}}w_{\mathrm{ex}}^{\prime}(r)\,-\,\dfrac{w_{\mathrm{ex}}^{\prime\prime}(r)}{N^{2}(r)-\sigma^{2}}\,.(B.5)

The numerical experiments are carried out on the
meridional half-disk of radiusrmax=1r_{\max}=1, and we consider
an oscillatory solution defined
in terms of the Bessel functionJ0J_{0}:wex​(r)=J0​(k​r)​(1−r2)2,with​k=ω/c0,forr∈(0,1).w_{\mathrm{ex}}(r)\,=\,J_{0}(k\,r)\,\,(1-r^{2})^{2}\,,\qquad\text{with }k=\omega/c_{0}\,,\qquad\text{for $r\in(0,1)$}.(B.6)

Fromwexw_{\mathrm{ex}}, the right-hand sidehexh_{\mathrm{ex}}is computed
fromEq.B.4and is used in the
numerical solver.
We employ the following background parameters:N2fromEq.B.3witha=0.1andb=0.9,c0=1,ω=8​π,γatt=2.\text{$N^{2}$ from \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eq:appendix-N2-var} with $a=0.1$ and $b=0.9$},\qquad c_{0}\,=\,1\,,\qquad\omega\,=\,8\,\pi\,,\qquad\gamma_{\mathrm{att}}\,=\,2\,.(B.7)

## B.1.1Scaling factor of the HDG stabilization

The HDG discretization involves a stabilization parameter,
as given inEq.5.57. Here we investigate the
choice of the scaling factorqtuneq_{\mathrm{tune}}that
appears within this stabilization term, seeEq.5.59.
InFigure17, we plot
theL2L^{2}error between the manufactured solutionwexw_{\mathrm{ex}}Eq.B.6and the numerical solution that
uses the right-hand sidehexh_{\mathrm{ex}}fromEq.B.4and the background parameters givenEq.B.7.
Each numerical solutionwτw_{\tau}is associated with a choice
of scaling factorqtuneq_{\mathrm{tune}}. We letqtuneq_{\mathrm{tune}}vary in the complex plane with real and imaginary parts between±​106\pm${10}^{6}$,
excluding 0. We further consider polynomial
orders33,44, and55.Figure 17:Evolution of theL2L^{2}error between the solutionwexw_{\mathrm{ex}}fromEq.B.6and the
numerical solution as a function of the
stabilization scaling factorqtuneq_{\mathrm{tune}}used in the HDG formulation.
Here,qtuneq_{\mathrm{tune}}varies in the
complex plane and the results are shown for
polynomial orders33,44, and55.

We observe that the error decreases as the polynomial order
increases, as expected. Comparing among the different choices ofqtuneq_{\mathrm{tune}}, the error increases
in the vicinity of the origin, particularly for values with a
positive imaginary part.
When either the real or imaginary part of the
scaling factor has a large magnitude (>​104>${10}^{4}$), the error is
minimal and remains essentially unchanged regardless of the
choice ofqtuneq_{\mathrm{tune}}.
In the following numerical experiments, we chooseqtune=−​106​iq_{\mathrm{tune}}=-${10}^{6}$\mathrm{i}.

## B.1.2Convergence test

We now investigate the convergence rate of the method.
InFigure18, we plot theL2L^{2}error
between the manufactured solutionEq.B.6and the corresponding numerical solution as a function of the
mesh size𝔥\mathfrak{h}, where𝔥\mathfrak{h}denotes the mesh edge length.
We consider polynomial orders from22to66and
report the corresponding convergence rates by indicating the
slopes of the resulting curves.Figure 18:Evolution of theL2L^{2}error between the manufactured
solutionwexw_{\mathrm{ex}}fromEq.B.6and the numerical solutionwwas a function of the
mesh size for polynomial orders from22to66.
The computed errors are indicated by the bullets, while the
solid lines represent the fitted convergence rates,
whose orders are indicated.

We observe the expected convergence rate of𝔥p+1\mathfrak{h}^{p+1}for a
polynomial approximation of orderpp, confirming the
theoretical accuracy of the HDG discretization.
Moreover, for a fixed mesh size𝔥\mathfrak{h}, increasing the
polynomial order consistently reduces theL2L^{2}error.
These results confirm that the proposed formulation
retains its convergence properties even in the co-existence of𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}and𝔹ell\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}regions.

## B.2Propagation with localized sources near the sonic line

In this section, we consider a configuration with a sharper
transition between𝔹ell\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}and𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}, intended to be more representative
of the one occurring in the solar backgrounds (seeFigure4).
The computational half-disk domain has radiusrmax=0.55r_{\max}=$0.55$,
and the sonic line is located atr=0.5r=$0.5$, so that the region𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}is relatively small and confined near the surface.
The synthetic background is defined as follows:N2fromEq.B.3witha=0.499andb=0.501,c0=1,ω=40​π,γatt=1.\text{$N^{2}$ from \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eq:appendix-N2-var} with $a=0.499$ and $b=0.501$},\qquad c_{0}\,=\,1\,,\qquad\omega\,=\,40\,\pi\,,\qquad\gamma_{\mathrm{att}}\,=\,1\,.(B.8)

## B.2.1Solutions with different type of sources

We consider a localized source for the right-hand side, which is either
a Dirac source located at position(ηsrc,zsrc)(\eta_{\mathrm{src}},z_{\mathrm{src}}),
or a Gaussian-shaped functionhGh_{\mathrm{G}}centered
at this position and with a varianceσG\sigma_{\mathrm{G}}such that,hG​(η,z)=exp⁡(12​(η−ηsrcσG)2+(z−zsrcσG)2).h_{\mathrm{G}}(\eta,z)\,=\,\exp\left(\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\dfrac{\eta-\eta_{\mathrm{src}}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{G}}}\right)^{2}\,+\,\left(\dfrac{z-z_{\mathrm{src}}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{G}}}\right)^{2}\right)\,.(B.9)

InFigure19, we show the numerical solutions
depending on the position of the sources and of the variance of the Gaussian
source. The sources are positioned alongzsrc=0z_{\mathrm{src}}=0, at different
locations near the sonic line (inr=0.50r=$0.50$), namely
withηsrc=\eta_{\mathrm{src}}=0.480.48,499499and0.5050.505,
corresponding respectively to the top, middle and bottom rows
ofFigure19. The simulations use polynomial
order44and a refined mesh with about120×103120\text{\times}{10}^{3}cells.(a)The center of the source is positioned inηsrc=0.48,zsrc=0\eta_{\mathrm{src}}=$0.48$,\,z_{\mathrm{src}}=0.(b)The center of the source is positioned inηsrc=0.499,zsrc=0\eta_{\mathrm{src}}=$0.499$,\,z_{\mathrm{src}}=0.(c)The center of the source is positioned inηsrc=0.505,zsrc=0\eta_{\mathrm{src}}=$0.505$,\,z_{\mathrm{src}}=0.Figure 19:Comparison of the real parts of the solutions depending on the source type.
For each row, the three columns on the left employ Gaussian sources
with varianceσG=​10−2\sigma_{\mathrm{G}}=${10}^{-2}$,5×10−35\text{\times}{10}^{-3}and​10−4{10}^{-4}, respectively. The right column corresponds to a Dirac point source.
The computational half-disk has radius0.550.55with
the sonic line between𝔹ell\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}and𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}atr=0.5r=$0.5$(indicated by the dotted black line). Each panel
also shows a zoom of the solution near the source
and close to the surface, in the zone(0.47,0.55)×(−0.10,0.18)($0.47$,$0.55$)\times(-$0.10$,$0.18$).

When the source is located in𝔹ell\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}and sufficiently away from the sonic line,
withηsrc=0.48\eta_{\mathrm{src}}=$0.48$,
the solution remains smooth across the sonic line, as shown inFigure19a.
The wavefield is attenuated within the region𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}},
and all sources (Dirac or Gaussian with different variances)
produce essentially the same oscillatory pattern.
When the source is moved closer to the sonic line,
either below or above it, namely forηsrc=0.499\eta_{\mathrm{src}}=$0.499$inFigure19bandηsrc=0.505\eta_{\mathrm{src}}=$0.505$inFigure19c,
the solution in the interior region𝔹ell\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}remains similar for all
sources but the solutions in𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}have different behaviours.
For Gaussian sources with a relatively large variance
(left column ofFigure19), the
solution is smooth and strongly attenuated within𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}.
As the Gaussian variance decreases, however, the solution develops
increasingly localized cone features.
For the sharpest Gaussian and the Dirac sources, the
propagation cone clearly emerges from the source location
and propagates back and forth within the𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}region.
This phenomenon occurs when the source is sufficiently close to
the sonic line and when the source is a sharp Gaussian
or a Dirac. On the other hand, smoother source distributions
suppress these features.

## B.2.2Convergence with mesh refinement

Without an analytic solution to evaluate the error,
we rely on numerical solutions obtained with a refined mesh.
To obtain a reference solutionwrefw_{\mathrm{ref}}, we employ
a mesh containing about120×103120\text{\times}{10}^{3}elements,
which is employed for the simulations inFigure19.
It corresponds to a characteristic mesh edge
size𝔥=3.5×10−3\mathfrak{h}=$3.5\text{\times}{10}^{-3}$.
InFigure20, we visualize the
solutions obtained with coarser meshes, as well as the difference between
the reference solution and the simulation. We consider the source that
is close to the sonic line, withηsrc=0.499\eta_{\mathrm{src}}=$0.499$,
and observe the persistence of the zigzag pattern with all meshes.(a)On a mesh with​103{10}^{3}elements: real part of the wavefield solution (left) and difference
with a reference solution (right).(b)On a mesh with5×1035\text{\times}{10}^{3}elements: real part of the wavefield solution (left) and difference
with a reference solution (right).Figure 20:Simulations with different meshes for a source
atηsrc=0.499\eta_{\mathrm{src}}=$0.499$and
polynomials of order44. The reference solution
is computed with a mesh containing120×103120\text{\times}{10}^{3}elements.
Zooms in the zone(0.47,0.55)×(−0.10,0.18)($0.47$,$0.55$)\times(-$0.10$,$0.18$)are also provided.

For the coarsest meshes, small differences are visible in the zoom
near the source center, which disappear as the mesh is
refined. The discrepancy is primarily localized around the source,
in particular within the cone-feature that appears in𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}.
The expected convergence behaviour is observed: the error decreases
under mesh refinement, and the numerical solution approaches the
reference one.

To further assess convergence, we present inFigure21the relativeL2L^{2}error between the reference solution and simulations performed on
successively refined meshes. We consider polynomial orders 3 and 4, and compare
the Dirac and Gaussian source withσG=​10−2\sigma_{\mathrm{G}}=${10}^{-2}$.
We show results for a source located near the sonic line, as well as for
a source in𝔹ell\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}.
Furthermore the error is computed after excluding the cells in the
immediate vicinity of the center of the source.(a)With polynomial order33.(b)With polynomial order44.Figure 21:Evolution of theL2L^{2}difference depending on the source
position and polynomial orders for Dirac and Gaussian sources.
Error is obtained with a reference solution computed
on a refined mesh with edge size𝔥=3.5×10−3\mathfrak{h}=$3.5\text{\times}{10}^{-3}$.
The Gaussian source has a variance ofσG=​10−2\sigma_{\mathrm{G}}=${10}^{-2}$.
The source position is excluded to compute the difference.

For the case in which the source is sufficiently far from the sonic line,
both source formulations have similar behaviour, recovering the expected
convergence order ofp+1p+1, even slightly higher here. When the source is
closer to the sonic line, both sources retain the
behaviour in𝔥p+1\mathfrak{h}^{p+1}, although we notice a slight degradation
for the Dirac source compared to the Gaussian one.

## B.2.3Scaling factor for the HDG stabilization

We next evaluate accuracy as a function of the stabilization
coefficientqtuneq_{\mathrm{tune}}. Similarly to what is
carried out inSubsectionB.1,
we present maps of the relative error
inFigure22withqtuneq_{\mathrm{tune}}varying in the complex plane.
Solutions are obtained with a mesh containing
about5×1035\text{\times}{10}^{3}elements, while the reference solution
employs the finest mesh.Figure 22:L2L^{2}difference between the reference solution
obtained with a refined mesh and the numerical solution
as a function of the stabilization scaling factorqtuneq_{\mathrm{tune}}used in the HDG formulation.
Here,qtuneq_{\mathrm{tune}}varies in the
complex plane and the results are shown for
polynomial orders33and44.
The Dirac source is positioned atηsrc=0.499\eta_{\mathrm{src}}=$0.499$.

We observe a similar pattern to that obtained for
the manufactured solution: When the stabilization magnitude
is sufficiently large, solutions have the
smallest difference compared with the reference solution,
and a specific choice of coefficient is
relatively flexible. As the magnitude of the stabilization coefficient
is reduced, the discrepancy increases, particularly
for those with positive imaginary part.

## B.3Attenuation level

Attenuation is a key ingredient in the mathematical analysis of
the problem particularly for well-posedness.
Here, we investigate numerically the behaviour of the solutions as
the attenuation is reduced. We consider the same configuration
as inSubsectionB.2, with a source located below
the sonic line, centered atηsrc=0.499\eta_{\mathrm{src}}=$0.499$,zsrc=0z_{\mathrm{src}}=0.
InFigure23, we present the resulting
wavefields for four attenuation levels,γatt=\gamma_{\mathrm{att}}=1,​10−1{10}^{-1},​10−2{10}^{-2}, and​10−3{10}^{-3}.
We compare between the Dirac and Gaussian source,
the latter with varianceσG=​10−2\sigma_{\mathrm{G}}=${10}^{-2}$.
All simulations are performed with polynomial order 4
and the aforementioned reference mesh.(a)With a Gaussian source of varianceσG=​10−2\sigma_{\mathrm{G}}=${10}^{-2}$.(b)With a Dirac source.Figure 23:Comparison of the simulations depending on the attenuation level and the source type.
From the left to right columns, attenuationγatt=\gamma_{\mathrm{att}}=11,​10−1{10}^{-1},​10−2{10}^{-2}and​10−3{10}^{-3}are employed.
The source is positioned inηsrc=0.499\eta_{\mathrm{src}}=$0.499$and
the sonic line between𝔹ell\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}and𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}atr=0.5r=$0.5$(indicated by the dotted black line). Each panel
also shows a zoom of the solution near the source
and close to the surface, in the zone(0.47,0.55)×(−0.10,0.18)($0.47$,$0.55$)\times(-$0.10$,$0.18$).

For the Gaussian source, reducing the attenuation does not modify the
structure of the solution, but only increases its amplitude. In contrast,
for the Dirac source, the wavefield in𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}is significantly modified
as the attenuation is reduced.
At higher attenuation, only a single cone from the source is visible.
As the attenuation decreases, however, multiple lines emerge, forming
a grid-like pattern within𝔹hyp\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{hyp}}.
To determine whether in the cases at very low attenuation, e.g.
withη=10−1\eta=10^{-1}or10−310^{-3}, the characteristic paths are
space-filling is a scope of future work.
A clearer understanding will also require devising new discretization methods
to treat the mixed-type closed BVP, granted its well-posedness at zero attenuation.
